EXECUTABLE NATURAL LANGUAGE
The Missing Grammar of Thought and SOP Lang English CNL
A RESEARCH PROGRAMME FOR AUDITABLE KNOWLEDGE REPRESENTATION AND EXECUTABLE SCIENCE
ScriptaHub
Abstract
Large language models make natural language a practical interface to computation, but scientific and technical prose remains an unstable execution surface. A model can alter quantifier scope, resolve an ambiguity silently, merge distinct identities, introduce a bridge premise, or convert reported evidence into accepted fact before an exact solver is called. The engineering problem addressed here is the construction of an auditable semantic layer between unrestricted language and computation, with explicit provenance, alternatives, types, contexts, and execution contracts.
The research programme combines four sources of evidence. English grammar identifies recurrent structures that a compiler must normalise. Cross-linguistic typology identifies distinctions that English often leaves optional, including evidentiality, clusivity, participant continuity, temporal remoteness, possession type, perspective, and spatial frame. Philosophy and systems research contribute distinctions such as process identity, collective agency, causal depth, reversibility, scale, boundaries, and distributed responsibility. Formal semantics, knowledge representation, programming-language methods, and controlled natural languages provide the mechanisms needed to represent and test these distinctions.
SLEnglish, short for SOP Lang English, is the common representation proposed for these experiments. It is a regular, typed, path-addressed language designed to receive candidate translations from English while remaining readable enough for human inspection and strict enough for deterministic parsing, validation, comparison, and execution. A dedicated chapter defines its syntax, operator contracts, parameters, namespaces, contexts, rules, and queries after the linguistic and conceptual requirements have been established.
The target application is executable science: research artefacts in which claims, evidence, assumptions, data, code, models, workflows, reviews, and corrections have explicit semantic dependencies. Such artefacts can support bounded queries, contradiction checks, dependency analysis, selective rebuilding, source-grounded explanation, and comparison of alternative interpretations. The research question is whether a small translation step from natural language into SLEnglish makes these operations more reliable and easier to audit than direct LLM reasoning or ad hoc formalisation.
Introduction: Purpose and Research Method
A scientific paper can be computationally reproducible while its semantic dependencies remain implicit. Code may run again even when the relation between a numerical result and the sentence it supports is unavailable to a machine. References may be complete while the path by which a premise entered an argument remains unrepresented. A dataset correction may therefore fail to invalidate an affected conclusion because the dependency exists only in the reader's interpretation. Executable science requires a representation in which these relations become explicit enough to inspect and recompute.
The first technical requirement is a visible formalisation boundary. A source statement, its interpretation, the evidence used for grounding, the selected semantic profile, the execution record, and the rendered result must remain distinct artefacts. Proof of a formal expression establishes a consequence of that expression; fidelity to the source is established by the translation record, source anchors, alternative interpretations, validation tests, and review. This distinction prevents exact reasoning from certifying an inaccurate formalisation.
The second requirement is conceptual coverage. English provides an efficient surface language, but many distinctions relevant to scientific reasoning are optional or compressed. The world's languages supply a comparative inventory of alternatives: source of information, inclusive and exclusive participation, participant continuity across clauses, temporal remoteness, aspect, possession type, classifier systems, logophoric perspective, social deixis, and absolute spatial reference. These observations are used here as candidate semantic variables. The analysis of English and other languages has one operational output: the operator and parameter tables in the appendices.
SLEnglish provides the experimental target representation. Ordinary English constructions, cross-linguistic grammatical distinctions, scientific operations, epistemic states, social relations, and systems concepts can each be represented as typed executable operators when they affect denotation, inference, provenance, time, authority, or computation. The common outer grammar keeps translation and tooling simple; the operator libraries carry the semantic detail. This structure allows the language to grow by adding reviewed operator contracts rather than by changing the parser.
The standardisation target is a common executable envelope for multiple ontologies and interpretation profiles. Operator libraries, parameter registries, namespace paths, type declarations, translation policies, and backend adapters are explicit artefacts that can be versioned and compared. SLEnglish can be interpreted by rule engines, graph systems, solvers, temporal models, generated code, specialised domain tools, or semantic circuits. The language remains stable at the source level while experiments determine which interpretation technologies are most effective for each class of task.

CHAPTER 1
The Problem of Executable Natural Language
The Formalisation Gap and the Trust Boundary
The word execution should be used with restraint. A program is executable because an operational semantics determines how its state changes. A database query is executable because relational operators determine which records are selected, joined, grouped, or excluded. A planning instance is executable because actions have preconditions and effects, and a planner searches a formally delimited state space. A logical theory is executable in a broader sense because a defined procedure can derive consequences, find models, report inconsistency, or return a certificate. In each case, the result is produced by an artefact whose behaviour is independent of the persuasiveness of the surrounding explanation.
An LLM response has a different status. The model maps a context to a distribution over continuations. Tool use can improve accuracy, but the model still determines which tool to call, what problem to give it, and how to interpret the output. A generated Python script may calculate exactly while modelling the wrong variable. An SMT solver may return satisfiable because an exception was omitted during translation. A theorem prover may establish a theorem whose quantifiers differ from those in the source. Execution moves an error from arithmetic or search into formalisation; it does not abolish error.
Executable natural language must therefore be defined as a relation among artefacts rather than as an intrinsic property of prose. The minimum chain contains a source revision, a task contract, one or more interpretations, grounding evidence, a selected formal semantics, an execution record, and a licensed realisation. The source revision establishes what was present. The task contract states which question is being asked and what degree of assurance is required. Interpretations expose the semantic choices introduced by the compiler. Grounding connects symbols to spans, records, sensors, definitions, or declared assumptions. The formal profile states what consequence means. The execution record carries proof, witness, counterexample, or bounded result. The realisation is subordinate to that record.
This relation is conditional by design. A result may be mechanically correct relative to a particular ontology, identity map, set of admissible readings, closed-world boundary, and solver semantics. It need not be true without qualification in the world. The architecture should make the conditions visible rather than flatten them into the word verified. Structural validity, semantic grounding, execution correctness, empirical adequacy, and institutional authorisation are distinct layers. A system can satisfy one and fail another.
The decisive practical metric is false assurance. False assurance occurs when the system presents a determinate, apparently verified conclusion even though the source, grounding, interpretation set, or coverage does not justify it. In high-assurance domains, a precise unknown is often more valuable than a fluent answer. A system that stops because two people may have been merged is better than one that silently chooses. A system that says no absence conclusion is licensed because the corpus is incomplete is better than one that treats failure to retrieve as proof of non-existence. Appropriate abstention is part of execution semantics, not a failure of usability.
This perspective preserves a useful ambition while rejecting totalisation. Scientific claims can be linked to evidence and executable analyses. Requirements can be connected to tests and state machines. Policies can be compiled into decision tables under an explicit jurisdiction and date. Protocols can be checked for temporal contradictions. Long documents can be queried against reusable theories without asking a model to improvise the same reasoning each time. What becomes executable is a declared projection whose omissions and assumptions are inspectable.
SLEnglish is proposed as the canonical pivot for that projection. At this point it is enough to state its purpose without presenting its syntax. It must give every semantic node a stable identity, make roles explicit, keep proposition construction separate from assertion or report, preserve alternative interpretations, and connect directly to a dependency graph that declared interpreters can validate and evaluate. It must be readable enough for review and regular enough for deterministic tooling. Because an LLM performs most of the initial translation, naturalness is less important than correction cost, semantic visibility, and stable compilation.
The trust boundary follows from these requirements. Generative models may propose forms, alternatives, ontology mappings, and explanations. They may not silently establish truth, provenance, or authority. Parsers may validate syntax but may not invent missing semantics. Type checkers may ensure that roles are legal but may not certify empirical truth. Solvers may determine consequences under a profile but may not reinterpret the source. Renderers may improve readability but may not strengthen modality, erase uncertainty, or add claims. Operational effects require explicit capabilities and policy. Human judgement remains necessary where meanings, values, or side effects are contested.
The formalisation gap is the distance between a source expression and the formal object executed in its name. It is the most persistent weakness in systems that combine language models with external tools. Benchmarks often report that a solver improved the final answer, but answer accuracy does not reveal whether the symbolic problem preserved the source. In constrained arithmetic tasks, short programs and immediate execution feedback make many errors visible. In policy, science, law, security, and long-document reasoning, a formal artefact can be internally impeccable while quietly omitting the condition that mattered.
Grounding is not one operation. Lexical grounding maps words or spans to candidate symbols. Referential grounding determines which mentions denote the same entity. Ontological grounding decides which categories and relations are available in the selected model. Temporal grounding locates events and states in intervals and calendars. Evidential grounding links propositions to observations, reports, datasets, or assumptions. Normative grounding identifies jurisdiction, authority, mandate, and effective date. Each layer can fail independently, and a serious compiler records the failure at the layer where it occurs.
Identity deserves particular emphasis. A document may mention ‘the board,’ ‘the organisation,’ ‘the model,’ or ‘the sample’ across many pages. Treating repeated labels as identical can create false continuity; treating every occurrence as distinct can destroy legitimate inference. For clouds, rivers, organisms, software versions, institutions, and datasets, identity itself may be qualified by material, processual, genealogical, informational, functional, or legal continuity. The executable representation should not conceal this work behind one generic equality predicate.
Coverage is equally important. Open-world sources can omit facts that are true. A collection of retrieved emails is not automatically complete evidence that no notification was sent. A literature search is not proof that no prior art exists. A database table may be closed for a named period and schema, while a public web corpus is not. Negative conclusions require a coverage token that states the domain searched, source revision, retrieval method, exclusions, and completeness policy. Without that token, absence remains unknown rather than false.
Ambiguity should be materialised as alternatives when it can affect consequence. A pronoun may refer to two actors; a requirement may admit two scopes; a causal sentence may describe intention or only correlation. A model can rank readings, but confidence is not proof. If a downstream verdict is invariant across all admissible readings, execution may proceed with a must conclusion. If it holds under at least one reading, the result is may. If it fails under every reading, it is cannot. If the interpretation set is incomplete or the abstraction too coarse, the honest result remains unknown.
This creates a productive role for abstract interpretation. The collecting semantics is the set of admissible interpretations of the source under the current grounding policy. An abstract domain can summarise that set through properties such as possible identities, temporal intervals, modalities, or evidence states. A sound abstract computation can show that a conclusion holds across the represented set or remains possible within it. Its guarantee is conditional on coverage of interpretations. The abstraction does not prove that the compiler imagined every meaning a competent reader might endorse.
Validation must therefore target the translation relation itself. Bidirectional checking renders a formal object back into controlled prose and asks whether the material commitments match the source. Contrastive tests alter one linguistic feature and verify that only the corresponding semantic field changes. Paraphrase tests compile meaning-preserving variants and compare canonical graphs. Extensional tests search for cases on which two candidate formalisations differ. Counterexample mapping links every solver witness to source spans and asks whether the scenario is linguistically and scientifically admissible. Human correction time is a crucial metric because a representation that is formally rich but impossible to review is not a trustworthy boundary layer.
The system must also treat source text as untrusted data. Documents can contain prompt injections, misleading headings, code-like fragments, hostile Unicode, fabricated ontology declarations, or quoted instructions. A compiler should preserve exact source structure while operating under fixed authority. Formal artefacts should be data, not unrestricted code. Extensions that perform algorithms or tool calls require reviewed contracts, locked digests, typed inputs and outputs, budgets, determinism declarations, and explicit capabilities. Security outcomes must be evaluated separately from semantic accuracy.
A defensible guarantee can now be stated. Given a named source revision, task, ontology, admissible interpretation set, grounding policy, formal profile, implementation version, and execution bounds, the reported canonical result follows and can be replayed. This is weaker than claiming that a machine fully understands language. It is stronger than asking a model to reason and trusting the fluency of its answer. The guarantee is valuable precisely because its frontier is visible.
From Formal Semantics to Auditable Compilation
The research programme has a long intellectual lineage. Controlled Natural Languages show that readable fragments can map deterministically to formal representations when vocabulary, grammar, and interpretation rules are restricted. Attempto Controlled English demonstrated discourse representation, querying, and theorem-proving integration. Common Logic Controlled English offered an English-like route to first-order representation. PENG and related systems showed that processable English can compile into logic-programming and Answer Set Programming profiles. SBVR made structured vocabulary and business rules accessible to domain experts. These systems establish feasibility while exposing the trade-off among precision, expressiveness, naturalness, and simplicity identified in Kuhn’s survey of CNLs.
Formal semantics contributes a different discipline. Model-theoretic semantics, Discourse Representation Theory, neo-Davidsonian event structures, natural logic, and typed semantic representations explain how quantification, anaphora, events, roles, modality, and discourse context can be represented. They also warn that a syntactic parse is not a grounded world model. A discourse referent can be introduced formally while its identity remains empirically uncertain. A lexical entailment can be valid in one ontology and invalid in another. The executable layer needs linguistic structure, domain definitions, evidence, and task-specific commitments.
Semantic parsing is the closest engineering predecessor. It converts language into executable database queries, logical forms, regular expressions, robotic commands, or programs. Its successes show that external execution improves systematicity. Its failures show how easily models exploit spurious correlations, memorise schemas, or generate programs that happen to answer a dataset without representing the question. The LLM era improves open-domain candidate generation but does not repeal the requirement for denotation, type, scope, and compositional tests.
Knowledge representation contributes mature families of semantics. Description logics and OWL support ontology reasoning under carefully chosen decidability restrictions. RDF and property graphs support exchange and graph navigation. Datalog supports recursive relational consequence. Answer Set Programming handles defaults and non-monotonicity. Common Logic provides a first-order interchange framework. Constraint solvers handle arithmetic and combinatorial conditions. Temporal logics and event calculi represent changing worlds. No one formalism dominates because the questions differ.
Programming-languages research adds the tools needed to reason about incomplete and evolving interpretations. Abstract interpretation computes sound approximations over potentially enormous state spaces. Symbolic execution constructs path conditions and witnesses. Model checking explores transition systems against temporal properties. SAT, SMT, and CSP engines decide bounded or theory-rich constraints. Dataflow analysis and fixpoint evaluation support dependency propagation. Proof assistants and proof-producing solvers can reduce the trusted base. These methods should not be used as metaphors; each has a specific semantics, completeness frontier, and certificate story.
Recent LLM research has rediscovered many of these structures under new names. Program-aided reasoning delegates calculations to interpreters. Constraint-based systems generate declarative problems for SAT or SMT. Logic-oriented systems translate text into first-order rules. Temporal translators generate specifications for model checking. Planning systems ask LLMs to construct domains and instances that sound planners solve. LLM-assisted program analysis proposes invariants, abstract transformers, or path summaries that formal checkers accept or reject. The strongest results occur when the target formalism is narrow and an independent component controls admission.
Neuro-symbolic architectures can be injective, in which symbolic information is absorbed into a neural model, or federative, in which neural and symbolic components retain separate identities and exchange structured artefacts. For executable natural language, federation is attractive because it preserves a visible trust boundary, allows replay, and permits multiple engines. It is not automatically superior on every benchmark. The empirical case must be established per task, but the architectural case for separated authority is strong.
The lineage suggests a synthesis. A source-grounded semantic pivot should preserve task-neutral distinctions and alternatives. A portfolio should lower selected fragments into specialised formal engines. Execution substrates should expose dependencies, budgets, evidence, and proof objects. LLMs should propose and explain, not silently own consequence. SLEnglish is intended to make this synthesis implementable with a common, inspectable source language.
Formal precision requires conceptual precision. A triple asserting that a company caused a loss can be syntactically exact while leaving unresolved whether company denotes a legal person, a board, a distributed organisation, a management decision, or an emergent result of local incentives. A theorem about the same model can be valid while same conflates identical parameters, common architecture, lineage, functional equivalence, and institutional ownership. Executable knowledge therefore needs explicit distinctions before it needs stronger inference.
The linguistic and philosophical survey is used as a requirements-discovery method for SLEnglish. Linguistic diversity contributes evidence source, clusivity, participant continuity, remoteness, aspect, classifier choice, possession type, social deixis, logophoric perspective, and spatial frame. Philosophy contributes identity, causation, infinity, part-whole relations, collectives, and responsibility. Systems analysis contributes reversibility, lock-in, feedback, scale, externality, path dependence, and threshold distance. Each useful distinction is converted into a candidate operator, parameter, type refinement, context field, or validation rule and then tested on concrete reasoning tasks.
SLEnglish uses profile-dependent obligatoriness. An epistemic profile can require evidence mode and source depth for empirical claims. An identity profile can require a declared continuity criterion for process entities. An institutional profile can require mandate and decision architecture when a collective is represented as acting. A scientific profile can require units, uncertainty, validity interval, and provenance. The profile therefore determines which omissions are compilation errors for a particular task.
Cross-linguistic operator design is grounded in descriptive grammars, typological surveys, corpora, and specialist review. When a candidate operator draws materially on a living linguistic tradition, its semantics should be checked against the relevant scholarship and speaker expertise. The transferable engineering object is a precisely defined distinction with a testable contract; the source language remains the evidence for that distinction rather than a label for an allegedly universal category.
Semantic distinctions are represented as first-class, extensible verbs. Evidentiality, switch-reference, logophoricity, clusivity, causative voice, process identity, collective agency, measurement, inference, and ordinary event structure can each own an operator contract when their parameters and consequences differ. This keeps the distinction visible in source code, documentation, tests, and execution traces instead of hiding it behind a small set of generic verbs.
In SLEnglish, verb denotes an executable semantic operator rather than only a lexical English verb. A familiar action such as transfer, a grammatical construction such as inclusive-we, an epistemic act such as infer, a relation such as part-of, a scientific operation such as measure, and a systems concept such as feedback-positive can all be verbs when they have declared schemas and interpretation contracts. Named parameters provide the microgrammar of each verb while every statement shares the same outer syntax.
This design separates a stable parser from an extensible semantic vocabulary. The parser recognises one statement envelope, references, paths, named parameters, and values. Operator libraries add domain and linguistic semantics through files that declare types, required parameters, optional parameters, constraints, examples, tests, and compatible interpreters. A compiler can therefore validate the generic structure of a statement even when a specialised operator must be routed to another interpreter.
The research sequence follows directly from this architecture. Chapters on English and cross-linguistic variation identify candidate distinctions; the conceptual chapters refine identity, collective agency, causation, uncertainty, and systems concepts; the SLEnglish chapter turns those distinctions into executable operator contracts; the execution and knowledge-representation chapters test how well the representation lowers to existing technologies. The appendices collect the resulting verbs and parameters as the working vocabulary for implementation and experiments.
Term
Explanation
Controlled natural language
Attempto Controlled English and related CNLs establish deterministic mappings for restricted readable forms (Fuchs, Kaljurand, and Kuhn 2008; Kuhn 2014).
Formal semantics
Discourse representation, natural logic, event semantics, and semantic parsing supply richer structures than untyped triples (Kamp and Reyle 1993; Bos 2008; Angeli and Manning 2014; Abzianidze et al. 2019).
Formal execution
Abstract interpretation, symbolic execution, model checking, and SMT provide distinct guarantee and witness mechanisms (Cousot and Cousot 1977; King 1976; de Moura and Bjørner 2008; Baldoni et al. 2018).
LLM plus tools
Program-aided, logic, constraint, planning, and autoformalisation systems show the value of narrow verified targets, not the formality of probabilistic generation itself (Gao et al. 2023; Ye et al. 2023; Pan et al. 2023).
Executable-language programme
The present synthesis combines controlled-language research, formal execution, cross-linguistic semantic distinctions, typed operator libraries, and backend-neutral interpretation.
CHAPTER 2
English as a Semantic Substrate
The Structural Core of English
SLEnglish begins from English for practical rather than metaphysical reasons. English is a major language of science, software, regulation, and international technical work; contemporary language models also handle it with broad coverage. None of this makes English a neutral model of thought. It makes English the source language whose recurrent structures, efficiencies, ambiguities, and historical irregularities must be understood before they are regularised. A controlled executable extension cannot improve what it has not first analysed.
Modern English is structurally analytic compared with many Indo-European relatives. It relies heavily on word order, auxiliary verbs, prepositions, articles, pronouns, and lexical choice rather than on extensive case or agreement morphology. The basic declarative clause is often described through a subject followed by a verb phrase, yet this familiar formula conceals several layers. The subject is a syntactic position, not a stable semantic role. It may denote an agent, experiencer, patient, theme, instrument, location, event, proposition, dummy element, or formally raised constituent. The verb phrase can contain auxiliaries that mark tense, aspect, modality, voice, and negation, while complements and adjuncts distribute information about participants, circumstances, and discourse status (Huddleston and Pullum 2002).
The distinction between syntactic function and semantic role is central. In ‘Alice opened the door’, Alice is both subject and agent. In ‘Alice noticed the error’, the subject is an experiencer. In ‘Alice received the file’, the subject is a recipient. In ‘The key opened the door’, the subject is an instrument or cause. In ‘There appeared a problem’, the grammatical subject is the expletive there while the post-verbal phrase introduces the entity. A machine that maps the first noun phrase mechanically to agent will therefore produce systematic errors even in simple clauses.
English transitivity is similarly flexible. Verbs may appear without an object, with one object, with two object-like complements, with a predicative complement, or inside constructions that change the normal alignment between syntax and semantic roles. ‘The glass broke’ and ‘Alice broke the glass’ share a change but differ in explicit causation. ‘Alice gave Bob the file’ and ‘Alice gave the file to Bob’ package the same transfer with different information structure. ‘The committee elected Alice chair’ combines an affected participant with a resultative role. Construction Grammar is useful here because it treats argument-structure patterns as conventional pairings of form and meaning rather than assuming that the lexical verb alone supplies every participant relation (Goldberg 2006).
English noun phrases combine determination, quantification, modification, complementation, possession, and reference. Articles are not merely decorative. The contrast between a, the, and bare forms interacts with identifiability, discourse status, genericity, countability, and number. Quantifiers introduce scope that surface order does not always settle. ‘Every researcher reviewed a paper’ may describe one shared paper or a different paper for each researcher. Relative clauses and prepositional phrases can attach at several levels. Nominalisations such as ‘the destruction of the archive’ compress event structure and may omit the destroyer, instrument, time, intention, and authority. This compression is efficient for prose but costly for executable interpretation.
Tense in English is morphologically sparse. The finite verb contrasts primarily past with non-past, while richer temporal meanings are assembled through auxiliaries, aspect, adverbials, lexical aspect, and context. The progressive presents an event or state from within its unfolding; the perfect relates a prior situation to a later reference point; modal auxiliaries express possibility, necessity, prediction, permission, obligation, or inference. The same surface modal can cross domains. ‘Must’ can report a rule, a logical consequence, or an evidential inference. A SLEnglish translation must therefore represent the modal source rather than preserve the word as an uninterpreted token.
Negation appears simple but interacts strongly with scope and pragmatic inference. ‘Alice did not review every paper’ does not normally mean that she reviewed none. ‘Every paper was not reviewed’ is unstable across speakers and registers. Negative polarity items, exceptive phrases, downward-entailing contexts, and presuppositions further complicate direct compilation. English also distinguishes sentence negation from lexical opposition, denial, absence, failure, prevention, and inability. These contrasts motivate separate operators rather than one global Boolean prefix.
Clause combination carries relations that punctuation and conjunctions only partly determine. Coordination can express addition, contrast, sequence, consequence, concession, or reformulation. Subordination can introduce time, cause, purpose, condition, comparison, report, or evidential stance. Participial and infinitival clauses often omit their understood subject. ‘After reviewing the data, the conclusion changed’ is grammatically possible even though the surface form encourages an implausible controller. Relative clauses, complement clauses, and reported speech introduce perspective centres and anaphoric dependencies that may extend across sentences.
Discourse interpretation supplies much of what grammar leaves open. Pronouns require antecedents; definite descriptions rely on shared context; ellipsis recovers missing material; tense is anchored to a discourse time; information structure distinguishes topic, focus, contrast, and background; implicature enriches literal meaning; presupposition treats some content as already accommodated. A reader handles these operations through linguistic competence and world knowledge. An executable system must either represent the decision, preserve alternatives, or declare that the distinction is outside the active formalisation.
English morphology also contains historical irregularity that has little semantic value for a machine-facing representation. Strong and suppletive verbs, irregular plurals, inconsistent derivation, fossilised case forms, orthographic ambiguity, and variable stress complicate authoring and parsing without improving reasoning. SLEnglish therefore uses canonical symbols, no agreement, no conjugation, and no dependence on articles or inflection. The lexical familiarity of English is retained; its accidental morphology is not part of the executable contract.
English structure
Semantic consequence for an executable representation
Subject position
The grammatical subject does not identify a single semantic role; the role must be named as agent, experiencer, patient, theme, instrument, location, proposition, or another declared relation.
Argument structure
Participants are contributed jointly by verbs and constructions. Named parameters prevent double-object, prepositional, passive, and resultative alternations from generating different hidden schemas.
Noun phrase
Reference, identity, quantity, countability, genericity, possession, and modification must be separated rather than compressed into determiner order.
Tense and aspect
Past/non-past morphology is insufficient. Event time, reference time, progressive, perfect, habituality, completion, duration, and temporal distance require explicit operators or parameters.
Modality
Possibility, obligation, permission, prediction, inference, and logical necessity must not share one untyped modal value.
Negation
Explicit contradiction, absence, non-occurrence, failure to derive, prevention, inability, and lexical opposition require distinct semantics.
Clause linkage
Cause, purpose, condition, concession, sequence, report, and elaboration need declared relations instead of being inferred from punctuation or generic conjunction.
Reference and discourse
Anaphora, quotation, perspective, topic, focus, presupposition, and implicature must be resolved, represented as alternatives, or marked outside scope.
Morphological irregularity
Irregular conjugation, plural formation, and spelling are removed from the executable layer because they add parsing cost but no intended semantic distinction.
The corpus perspective reinforces this analysis. Biber and colleagues show that English grammar is not one uniform system used identically in conversation, fiction, news, and academic prose. Registers differ in clause density, nominalisation, stance markers, pronoun use, lexical bundles, and information packaging (Biber et al. 1999). Scientific prose in particular concentrates information in noun phrases and passive constructions. That density is useful to experts but difficult for source-grounded execution because causal roles and evidential status are often distributed across several sentences or hidden inside nominal modifiers.
A regular intermediate language should not attempt to reproduce every surface choice. It should preserve the distinctions that change denotation, inference, temporal organisation, evidence, authority, or downstream action. Voice can often be normalised into role-labelled event structure. Word-order variants can map to the same operator. Synonymous surface verbs can map to a shared semantic contract when the domain warrants it. Conversely, superficially similar uses must remain separate when they carry different commitments. ‘The model predicts’, ‘the paper reports’, ‘the instrument records’, and ‘the team concludes’ cannot all become a generic relation between a subject and a proposition.
This is why the proposed operator libraries include ordinary English constructions as well as categories discovered through cross-linguistic comparison. The appendices treat declaratives, interrogatives, imperatives, passives, progressives, perfects, conditionals, reported speech, anaphora, nominalisation, coordination, comparison, possession, measurement, and other recurrent structures as candidate executable contracts. The purpose is not to encode English grammar for its own sake. It is to make the translation from English auditable at the points where a grammatical choice changes the semantic object handed to an interpreter.
Where English Leaves Meaning Implicit
English succeeds partly because it permits efficient underspecification. Speakers need not repeat what participants can recover from the situation. Articles, pronouns, tense, lexical aspect, conventional scripts, and background knowledge coordinate attention at low cost. The same economy becomes a liability when a sentence is detached from its original setting, incorporated into a knowledge base, reused by an agent, or asked to support a decision months later. The problem is not that English cannot express precision. It is that precision is optional, unevenly distributed, and often expensive.
The verb be illustrates the issue. It supports strict identity, classification, role occupancy, temporary state, location, scheduling, constitution, approximation, and predication. ‘Alice is the director’ can describe current office, legal authority, informal leadership, or an identification in a particular record. ‘Water is H2O’ concerns theoretical identity of a different kind. ‘The meeting is Monday’ locates an event in time. A formal language that assigns all these to one predicate either overgenerates substitution or pushes decisive semantics into untyped arguments.
The word same is more consequential. It can indicate numerical identity, material continuity, structural resemblance, genealogical descent, functional equivalence, version lineage, legal continuity, or context-dependent sufficiency. A cloud can remain the same process while exchanging most of its material. A company can remain the same legal entity after complete personnel turnover. Two software deployments can run the same source while differing in configuration and behaviour. English allows the relevant criterion to remain tacit because human interlocutors often negotiate it locally. Executable knowledge cannot assume that the criterion will remain stable across queries.
Collective nouns compress participant structure and authority. ‘The company decided’ may refer to a board vote, an executive instruction, a delegated automated policy, a distributed practice, or an outcome that nobody intended. ‘Science says’ can mean a meta-analysis, a current disciplinary majority, one institutional statement, or a journalist’s synthesis. ‘We’ can include or exclude the addressee, share or deny authority, distribute or conceal responsibility, and associate some people with costs they did not choose. English can clarify these relations, but its ordinary grammar does not require the clarification.
Causal language is equally compressed. ‘X caused Y’ can denote a triggering event, enabling condition, background structure, intervention, statistical contributor, omission, necessary condition, sufficient configuration, or retrospective attribution. Active voice may imply intention even when the mechanism is systemic. Passive voice may hide the actor when the actor is unknown, irrelevant, obvious, strategically omitted, or institutionally protected. Scientific explanation needs to separate causal type, evidential basis, model, time scale, and intervention semantics rather than equating grammatical transitivity with causal force.
Epistemic provenance is mostly optional. English can say that a proposition was observed, measured, remembered, reported, inferred, simulated, quoted, or generated, yet it also permits an unmarked declarative that gives no clue about the source. Hedges such as apparently, probably, perhaps, or I think mix confidence, politeness, indirectness, and source. A report may be grammatically presented as fact after several transformations. For scientific and institutional knowledge, source mode and source depth should be independently addressable.
Temporal meaning is distributed across tense, aspect, adverbials, lexical semantics, and world knowledge. ‘The system fails’ may describe a current event, a habitual pattern, a generic disposition, a scheduled test result, or a conditional consequence in an omitted context. ‘The model has improved’ establishes a prior comparison but may leave baseline, measurement, evaluation set, and interval unspecified. ‘Soon’ and ‘recently’ are relative to speaker, domain, and time scale. A language intended for executable science must distinguish valid time, observation time, transaction time, reference interval, duration, recurrence, and temporal distance when they affect consequence.
Scope is another hidden variable. Quantifiers, negation, modality, conditionals, and attitude reports can combine in several orders. ‘A reviewer checked every claim’ may involve one reviewer or several depending on context. ‘The regulator may not approve the system’ can mean permission to withhold approval or uncertainty about approval. ‘All models are not robust’ may be read as none are robust or not all are robust. A controlled representation should make scope structural, because no deterministic reasoner can recover a distinction that was erased by the compiler.
Reference is rarely a matter of string equality. Names vary, organisations change names, titles refer to roles, pronouns depend on discourse, and descriptions may identify an entity only under a time and jurisdiction. The same label can denote a legal person, a brand, a software service, or a group of employees. Entity resolution is therefore an empirical and ontological task. SLEnglish preserves stable node identities and permits qualified identity relations rather than assuming that repeated words denote the same object.
Countability and boundaries also depend on conceptual choice. English alternates between mass and count construals, as with experience, paper, coffee, data, or software. A noun can impose a unit where the phenomenon is continuous. A classifier may be omitted even when it determines what counts as one case, one event, one model, or one species. Measurement appears exact only after a unit, instrument, precision, and boundary rule have been selected. The executable layer should record the classifier or individuation criterion whenever a count licenses inference.
Normative statements combine content with authority. ‘The sample must be destroyed’ may be a legal requirement, a protocol obligation, a safety recommendation, a prediction, or an inference from observed evidence. ‘May’ can grant permission or state possibility. ‘Should’ can express weak obligation, expected behaviour, advice, or counterfactual consequence. Jurisdiction, issuer, mandate, effective time, exceptions, and affected parties determine whether an instruction is binding. A semantic compiler cannot leave these fields to tone.
The implicitness of English is not a defect to be removed globally. A language that demanded source, time, identity mode, causal mechanism, authority, and scope in every sentence would be unusable. SLEnglish instead supports profiles and operator contracts. A scientific claim profile may require units, uncertainty, source spans, and valid time. An institutional decision profile may require decision procedure, mandate, represented collective, and affected population. An informal annotation profile may accept more omissions and return a weaker guarantee. Precision is attached to purpose.
This principle also controls the relation between SLEnglish and natural language. The source text remains authoritative as text. A compiled representation is one declared projection used for a task. It may omit literary effects, social implication, emotion, irony, or productive ambiguity. The system should not claim that the projection is the complete meaning of the passage. Instead it records coverage, alternatives, unsupported constructs, and the exact source spans that gave rise to each node. Auditability replaces the fiction of complete translation.
Implicit English distinction
Required SLEnglish treatment when the distinction affects consequence
Identity criterion
Name strict, material, structural, processual, genealogical, informational, functional, role-based, or legal continuity.
Participant role
Replace position-based inference with named roles such as agent, experiencer, patient, theme, recipient, source, instrument, and beneficiary.
Evidence source
Distinguish observation, measurement, testimony, quotation, memory, inference, simulation, generation, and source depth.
Temporal frame
Represent event time, valid time, transaction time, reference interval, aspect, recurrence, and remoteness where relevant.
Quantifier scope
Encode bindings and structural scope for universal, existential, numerical, modal, and negative operators.
Collective attribution
State whether the relation is statistical, authorised, delegated, distributed, coordinated, emergent, or merely metonymic.
Causal type
Separate trigger, contribution, enabling condition, mechanism, omission, structural cause, prevention, and intervention.
Normative authority
Record issuer, mandate, jurisdiction, effective period, affected party, exception, and strength.
Implicit English distinction
Required SLEnglish treatment when the distinction affects consequence
Boundary and classifier
Record the operational rule by which a unit, member, event, category, or dataset row is individuated.
Discourse dependence
Resolve reference and perspective, retain alternatives, or mark the dependency as outside the selected projection.
The English analysis supplies a baseline against which cross-linguistic categories can be assessed. The next chapter does not present other languages as repositories of exotic solutions. It asks a narrower question: which distinctions have natural languages shown to be stable enough for recurrent grammatical or lexical treatment, and which of those distinctions expose omissions that matter for knowledge representation? The operator tables at the end of the book are the engineering consequence of that survey.
The relationship is not one-to-one. A natural-language morpheme can combine meanings that SLEnglish separates. A single SLEnglish operator may integrate evidence from several linguistic traditions, formal theories, and engineering requirements. Some English constructions deserve their own operator because they change inference; others are normalised into a shared frame. Some rare grammatical categories become useful only as optional parameters. The correct granularity is an empirical question to be evaluated through translation errors, reviewer correction, query behaviour, and implementation complexity.
English therefore serves neither as the universal grammar nor as an adversary. It supplies the practical lexical interface, a rich history of grammatical description, and a large body of machine-readable text. Its limitations provide test cases. SLEnglish is an attempt to preserve that accessibility while moving decisive semantic commitments from context into typed, versioned, and inspectable structures.
CHAPTER 3
Language Diversity and the Missing Grammar of Thought as Design Evidence
Relativity, Salience, and the Ethics of Comparison
The idea that language shapes thought has survived repeated declarations of death because it captures an experience almost every bilingual speaker recognises. A thought that feels direct in one language can become awkward in another. A social relation changes when one must choose between a familiar and deferential pronoun. A poem becomes not merely less elegant but differently organised when translated. Some languages seem to invite the speaker to say how an event was known, whether an action was completed, or whether “we” includes the listener. It is tempting to infer that speakers inhabit different mental worlds.
Temptation, however, is not evidence. The history of linguistic relativity is full of plausible anecdotes, selective translations, romantic portraits of non-European peoples, and experiments that confound language with education, ecology, literacy, or task strategy. The strongest version of the claim—that a language makes certain thoughts impossible—has little support. It also underestimates human inventiveness. Speakers constantly paraphrase, borrow, point, draw, calculate, and coin terms. Science itself is a long demonstration that a language can be extended beyond the distinctions inherited from ordinary life.
From worldview to experiment. Wilhelm von Humboldt, Franz Boas, Edward Sapir, and Benjamin Lee Whorf did not formulate one clean hypothesis. Their work contains different claims about grammatical classification, habitual attention, cultural interpretation, and the relation between language and experience. What later came to be called the “Sapir–Whorf hypothesis” was partly a retrospective packaging of a heterogeneous tradition. John Lucy’s historical and methodological reconstruction remains indispensable precisely because it replaces slogans with researchable questions (Lucy 1992, 1997).
A useful distinction separates linguistic determinism from linguistic relativity. Determinism says that thought is bounded by language: without a linguistic category, a speaker cannot entertain the corresponding concept. Relativity says that habitual linguistic patterns can bias or organise cognition in ways that differ across language communities. Even this weaker thesis is too vague until four variables are specified: which linguistic structure, which cognitive process, under what task conditions, and through what mechanism?
Consider colour. Russian conventionally divides the English category blue into goluboy for lighter blues and siniy for darker blues. Winawer and colleagues found that Russian speakers were faster to discriminate colours across this lexical boundary than within a category, an advantage disrupted by verbal interference but not spatial interference (Winawer et al. 2007). The result does not show that English speakers cannot see the difference. It suggests that an available lexical boundary can be recruited online and alter processing speed. The effect is real, local, and mechanistically modest—exactly the kind of finding a mature theory should expect.
Spatial cognition provides a stronger family of cases. In languages that habitually use absolute frames of reference—roughly north, south, east, west, or directions anchored in prevailing geography—speakers must maintain orientation to converse fluently. Cross-linguistic studies have found correlations between linguistic frames and strategies in non-verbal spatial tasks (Pederson et al. 1998; Levinson 2003). Haun and colleagues showed that Dutch and Namibian children preferred different spatial coding strategies in line with their linguistic environments, and that performance suffered when participants had to switch frames (Haun et al. 2011). Again, the conclusion is not that one group lacks egocentric space. It is that repeated linguistic practice can train a strategy until it becomes the path of least resistance.
Number offers both dramatic evidence and a warning against exaggeration. Work with Pirahã and Mundurukú speakers distinguishes approximate numerical competence from exact symbolic number. People without an extensive conventional counting system can estimate and compare quantities, but exact matching becomes difficult when memory, transformation, or displacement prevents direct one-to-one correspondence (Gordon 2004; Pica et al. 2004; Frank et al. 2008). Exact number words appear to function as a cognitive technology: they stabilise cardinal values across time and operations. Yet these studies do not show that a language permanently seals numerical capacity. Training, material practices, and education matter. Recent work also demonstrates that semantic task structure can outweigh language-specific metaphors. A preregistered 2026 study across four languages found that the semantic content of arithmetic problems, not the languages’ preferred temporal metaphors, drove ordinal versus cardinal strategies (John, Macedo-Rouet, and Gros 2026). Relativity is a family of conditional effects, not a master key.
Language is not the whole mind. The most serious challenge to expansive claims comes from dissociations between language and thought. People with severe language impairment can retain arithmetic, music, causal reasoning, social understanding, and planning. Brain-imaging work distinguishes the frontotemporal language network from systems supporting many forms of domain-general reasoning. Fedorenko, Piantadosi, and Gibson argue that language is primarily adapted for communication and cultural transmission rather than serving as the medium of all thought (2024). Their case is an important corrective to any theory that treats inner speech as the operating system of intelligence.
But the choice is not between “language is thought” and “language is cognitively irrelevant.” A map is not the territory, yet a map changes where travellers go. A programming language is not computation itself, yet its abstractions alter which programs are easy to write, inspect, and maintain. A graphical user interface is not the underlying machine state, yet its defaults shape behaviour. Language can be one representational resource among several and still have large cumulative effects.
This middle position also clarifies the relevance of large language models. Fluent models show that formidable formal linguistic competence can be acquired from patterns in text, while reliable planning, world modelling, causal intervention, and grounded judgment remain separable problems. Mahowald and colleagues distinguish formal linguistic competence—knowledge of linguistic patterns—from functional linguistic competence, the use of language in real-world reasoning and communication (2024). The distinction cautions against inferring thought from eloquence. It also makes language design newly consequential: AI systems increasingly mediate human writing, summarisation, search, policy, and scientific work. Their linguistic defaults may become cognitive defaults at societal scale.
The mechanism: salience, compression, and obligation. A defensible theory can be built from three mechanisms.
Salience concerns what is brought to attention. Naming a distinction makes it easier to notice, discuss, remember, and teach. Odour provides a striking example. English speakers often name smells by source—smells like lemon—and perform poorly at abstract odour naming. Speakers of Jahai, an Aslian language with a rich abstract smell lexicon, name odours with ease and agreement comparable to colour naming (Majid and Burenhult 2014). Maniq shows a similarly structured odour vocabulary (Wnuk and Majid 2014). The result is not that English noses are defective. English offers little conventional scaffolding for treating odour qualities as stable, discussable dimensions.
Compression concerns the cost of holding and transforming a distinction. A numeral such as seventeen compresses a cardinality into a portable symbol. An evidential suffix can compress “I infer this from visible consequences” into a routine grammatical choice. Compression matters because working memory is limited. A distinction that occupies one familiar slot can participate in more complex reasoning than one that requires a sentence of explanation.
Obligation concerns what grammar makes difficult to omit. English can express evidentiality, clusivity, remoteness, and source depth. The issue is not expressive possibility but default omission. Alexandra Aikhenvald documents languages in which every declarative statement requires a choice among information-source categories (2004). A speaker may have several sources and must still select a form. The grammar imposes what we might call mandatory epistemic bookkeeping.
These mechanisms suggest a more precise formulation of linguistic relativity: A linguistic system influences cognition when its recurrent categories alter the salience, representational cost, or habitual obligatoriness of distinctions used in perception, memory, inference, or coordination.
The formulation predicts variability. Effects should be stronger when language is actively recruited, when the task aligns with the encoded distinction, when a category is obligatory and frequent, and when alternative strategies are costly. They should weaken under verbal interference, when perceptual evidence dominates, when bilingual speakers operate in a different language, or when task semantics provide a more powerful structure. Gennari’s recent review emphasises precisely this situated character: language effects depend on task goals, context, and alignment between linguistic and cognitive representations (2025).
English is not neutral. The scientific debate has itself been distorted by the dominance of English. Blasi and colleagues argue that cognitive science has too often treated properties of English—its vocabulary, morphosyntax, modalities, and interactional norms—as transparent windows onto the human mind (2022). This is an epistemic problem rather than a complaint about cultural prestige. If experiments are designed in English, conducted by English-speaking researchers, and interpreted through English categories, the field may overlook cognitive variables made more visible elsewhere.
Anna Wierzbicka’s Imprisoned in English pushes the critique further. Terms such as emotion, cooperation, politeness, mind, and fairness are not culture-free atoms simply because they travel easily in Anglophone scholarship (Wierzbicka 2013). Her Natural Semantic Metalanguage programme is controversial, particularly in its claim to identify universal semantic primes, but the diagnosis is powerful: scholars often universalise the metalanguage in which they theorise.
N. J. Enfield offers a complementary warning. Language is extraordinarily effective for coordination, persuasion, narrative, and social action, yet those strengths do not make it a neutral instrument for representing reality (Enfield 2022). Natural language routinely leaves boundaries vague, assigns agency strategically, compresses causal chains, and foregrounds what matters in interaction rather than what would satisfy a scientific ontology. The defects are not accidental. A communication system optimised for fast coordination among embodied social beings will not automatically be optimised for auditing distributed responsibility in a multinational platform or tracing uncertainty through an AI-generated literature review.
Against two symmetrical mistakes. The first mistake is to dismiss linguistic relativity because speakers can paraphrase. Possibility is the wrong metric. A Turing-complete language can in principle compute any computable function, but programming languages still differ enormously in safety, legibility, and development cost. The second mistake is to romanticise grammatical differences as windows into superior cultures. A language that marks evidence obligatorily can still be used to lie. A language with inclusive and exclusive pronouns does not abolish political exclusion. Absolute spatial systems can support extraordinary orientation while imposing costs in unfamiliar settings. Every grammar illuminates and obscures.
Indo-European languages themselves prevent crude oppositions. English lacks a grammatical evidential system, but Bulgarian has rich evidential and related forms. English has lost the dual that survives in languages such as Slovenian and was present in older Indo-European languages. Slavic aspect forces distinctions that English often distributes across lexical choice and context. Albanian has an admirative category associated with unexpected information. The object of criticism is therefore not an imagined uniform
“Indo-European mind.”
It is a historically contingent bundle of defaults, especially those of modern standard English as the global language of science, technology, and administration.
The thesis of this book can now be stated without metaphysical drama: Language does not determine the limits of thought. It helps determine the price of thought.
A costly thought can still be thought. But costs accumulate. Institutions repeat familiar categories; databases require fields; laws stabilise definitions; metrics reward what can be named and counted; AI systems reproduce patterns in their training language. The task ahead is not to escape language. It is to inspect and redesign some of the toll gates.
Evidence, Perspective, Participation, and Social Grammar
Humanity did not design a single language and then diversify it cosmetically. The world’s languages vary in sound, morphology, syntax, semantics, discourse, gesture, and the social distribution of speech. Large typological resources such as the World Atlas of Language Structures and Grambank convert parts of this diversity into comparable data. Grambank currently encodes hundreds of thousands of observations across more than two thousand language varieties and nearly two hundred structural features; its analyses show both deep genealogical inheritance and substantial flexibility (Skirgård et al. 2023). The databases remain necessarily incomplete, but they overturn the intuition that familiar European grammar exhausts the natural options.
It is fashionable to call languages
“experiments,” but the metaphor requires care.
Languages are not controlled trials. A grammatical feature travels with culture, environment, history, contact, subsistence, literacy, and institutions. Correlation between grammar and cognition cannot by itself establish causation. Yet diversity performs an indispensable conceptual service before any causal claim is made: it proves that a distinction is learnable, transmissible, and usable in ordinary life. What English treats as cumbersome may elsewhere be routine.
Who belongs inside
“we”.
English we is politically convenient because it is semantically underdetermined. It can mean speaker plus listener, speaker plus others but not listener, a whole institution, a nation, humanity, a professional class, or a rhetorical coalition invented for one sentence. Many languages grammaticalise a basic part of this ambiguity through clusivity: inclusive we contains the addressee; exclusive we does not.
WALS data show that this distinction is widespread outside Europe and absent from the European languages in its sample (Cysouw 2013). Mandarin provides a familiar lexical contrast: zánmen normally includes the listener, whereas wǒmen need not. Other systems extend the distinction through dual, trial, or paucal number. Paamese, for example, distinguishes categories that allow speakers to refer not merely to
“us” but to inclusive or exclusive groups of two, three or a few.
Clusivity is not a built-in theory of democracy. It does, however, expose a variable that English political language can hide.
“We have decided that sacrifices are necessary” sounds different when grammar forces the speaker to choose we-with-you or we-without-you. A designed extension could go further and distinguish we-authorised-by-you, we-claiming-to-represent-you, and we-affected-with-you. Natural grammar supplies the proof of usability; conceptual engineering identifies the modern accountability problem.
Number beyond singular and plural. English compresses most cardinal distinctions into singular versus plural. The difference between two people, three people, and a small coalition is lexical information, not a general grammatical category. Yet dual, trial, and paucal systems show that number can be organised differently. Such systems may be especially elaborated in pronouns, where the structure of participation matters socially.
The cognitive significance should not be overstated. A grammatical dual does not make its speakers better at arithmetic. Its value is attentional: it treats dyads as a recurring type rather than an accidental quantity. This matters because pairs often have properties that larger groups do not. Reciprocity, mutual monitoring, intimacy, deadlock, and shared secrecy change discontinuously between two and three. A language for institutional analysis might profit from distinguishing not only numerical size but coordination topology: pair, chain, hub, clique, hierarchy, and swarm.
Counting through ontology. Numeral-classifier languages require a mediating category when nouns are counted. In Minangkabau, classifiers include forms associated with humans, non-human animates, and elongated objects. WALS distinguishes languages without numeral classifiers from those with optional or obligatory systems (Gil 2013). The categories differ widely, and their use is often shaped by conventional rather than philosophically pure taxonomies.
The larger insight is that quantity need not be ontologically neutral. English applies the same syntactic frame to three chairs, three companies, three storms, and three promises. But what constitutes one unit differs. A
“company” may be counted legally, operationally, by brand, by corporate group, or by decision system. A
“storm” can be individuated meteorologically or by named event.
A
“model” can be counted by architecture, weight file, deployed endpoint, or lineage. Modern data systems often fail because they request a number before defining the unit.
A useful conceptual classifier would not ask what physical shape an entity has. It would ask what principle makes it countable: bounded object, continuing process, legal person, information pattern, causal lineage, temporary coordination, or statistical cluster. The classifier would act as an ontological checksum attached to numerical claims.
Possession is not one relation. English my can connect a person to a hand, a coat, a birthplace, an idea, a child, an employee, a debt, a disease, or a country. The word’s grammatical uniformity hides substantially different relations: part–whole, ownership, kinship, authorship, control, association, responsibility, experience, and identification. Languages with possessive classification and alienability contrasts demonstrate that grammar can separate some of these relations. WALS cautions that the label
“alienable versus inalienable” oversimplifies systems whose classes may be organised around kinship, body parts, spatial relations, or semantic properties of nouns (Nichols and Bickel 2013).
This is not a minor grammatical curiosity. Political and economic disputes often exploit possessive ambiguity.
“Our data,”
“my body,”
“their territory,”
“the company’s model,” and “humanity’s atmosphere” invoke different normative structures while sharing possessive syntax.
A language designed for contemporary governance might distinguish custody, legal title, control, authorship, dependency, stewardship, membership, and constitutive part. The distinction between a resource one may transfer and a relation that partly constitutes the self should not be left entirely to context.
Tracking perspective and continuity. Some languages mark whether the subject of one clause is the same as or different from the subject of an adjacent clause. Switch-reference systems can therefore preserve agent continuity through discourse even when noun phrases are omitted. The markers may also interact with temporal or event continuity, and their analysis remains theoretically contested (McKenzie 2025). Whatever the correct syntax, the functional achievement is striking: grammar can make a change of protagonist visible at the joint between propositions.
Logophoricity tracks a different question: whose perspective, speech, knowledge, or consciousness is represented? English pronouns and reported-speech constructions often leave ambiguity between the narrator’s and a character’s commitments. Logophoric pronouns in some languages identify a privileged perspective-holder. Obviation, especially associated with Algonquian languages, ranks third persons by discourse prominence through proximate and obviative marking. These are not merely reference-management devices. They make the architecture of perspective grammatically present.
The need is acute in the age of synthetic media and AI. A generated report may contain the user’s assertion, a model inference, a retrieved source’s claim, and an evaluator’s judgment in the same paragraph. Ordinary quotation and citation are too coarse. A future grammar of provenance could preserve not only who uttered a sentence but who perceived, inferred, endorsed, transformed, or merely transmitted each proposition.
Time with distance, not merely tense. English marks tense and aspect, but it normally leaves temporal distance to adverbs. Many languages have grammatical remoteness distinctions. WALS reports systems separating today’s past from earlier pasts, and Yagua has been described with five degrees ranging from a few hours ago to a distant or legendary past (Dahl and Velupillai 2013). Such categories do not replace dates. They position events relative to the speaker’s temporal horizon.
The design opportunity is not to copy a particular tense inventory. Modern institutions operate across multiple clocks: milliseconds in automated trading, days in news cycles, quarters in corporations, electoral terms in democracies, decades in infrastructure, centuries in climate, and geological durations in nuclear waste. English can state each scale but offers no grammatical friction when a claim slides between them.
“Efficient,”
“safe,”
“profitable,” and “sustainable” are often meaningless until a temporal address is supplied.
Experience beyond the visual hierarchy. English has a rich visual vocabulary and comparatively weak abstract vocabulary for smell. Jahai and Maniq demonstrate that olfactory qualities can be lexicalised systematically and named with speed and agreement (Majid and Burenhult 2014; Wnuk and Majid 2014). Ideophones across many languages vividly depict sensory scenes, motion, texture, intensity, sound, and manner. They resist the assumption that mature language must move away from iconicity toward arbitrary, visually dominated description.
The lesson is broader than smell. Scientific instruments increasingly detect dimensions humans do not perceive directly: molecular signatures, latent variables, network motifs, electromagnetic spectra, uncertainty surfaces. We typically force these into inherited visual and object-centred metaphors. A richer language of patterned sensation might help experts develop intuitions for high-dimensional systems without pretending those intuitions are literal perception.
Social geometry in grammar. Honorific systems, speech levels, kinship terms, and address forms show that grammar and lexicon can make social relation unavoidable. The familiar European T–V distinction—informal versus formal second-person address—is only a small example. Languages may encode relative age, status, intimacy, group membership, deference, and the social direction of an act. Such systems can preserve hierarchy and exclusion; they can also prevent the fiction that communication occurs between context-free individuals.
English is not socially neutral. It encodes hierarchy through titles, register, accent, indirectness, and institutional genre, but often disguises these as stylistic choices rather than relational information. A redesigned language should not restore rigid rank. It could instead mark role and power asymmetry: who can refuse, who bears risk, who controls the agenda, and who is represented but absent. This would turn social context from background implication into inspectable structure.
What typology gives—and what it does not. The catalogue could continue: grammatical animacy, noun classes, inverse systems, pluractionality, distributive and associative number, egophoricity, mirativity, middle voice, applicatives, antipassives, directionals, elaborate aspect, and systems of quotation and report. No single language combines all of them, and no speaker explicitly consults a philosophical matrix while using them. Grammar is mostly automatic.
That automaticity is precisely why typology matters. It shows that habitual distinctions can be made quickly enough for conversation. But typology alone cannot prove cognitive benefit. A category may exist because of historical accident; it may be redundant; speakers may strategically neutralise it; experimental effects may be task-bound. The book therefore adopts a two-stage method. Natural languages first reveal possible interface components. Philosophy and experiment then ask whether a component would improve reasoning in a specified domain.
The goal is not maximal grammatical complexity. Every obligatory distinction consumes attention. A language that forced source, confidence, causal depth, temporal scale, collective mode, and identity criterion into every sentence would be unusable. Good interface design is selective. A cockpit displays different variables from a kitchen timer. Scientific reporting, corporate governance, public news, and intimate conversation need different defaults.
The world’s languages are thus neither prisons nor museums. They are a library of interface patterns. We should read them with gratitude, skepticism, and the intention to build.
Imagine two versions of the same headline: The treatment causes cardiac damage.
The treatment causes cardiac damage — inferred from a retrospective association, reported through two secondary summaries, confidence moderate, causal status disputed.
The first is elegant, portable, and dangerously complete. The second is cumbersome but epistemically honest. Modern English offers no stable point between them. Academic prose can add citations and hedges, journalism can attribute sources, and data systems can record metadata, yet ordinary sentence grammar permits a proposition to travel detached from how it was acquired. Social media and generative AI amplify this detachment. A sentence can be copied thousands of times while its evidential history evaporates.
Natural evidential systems show that source information can be compressed into grammar. They do not solve epistemology, but they expose a remarkable design option: a language can require the speaker to answer, however minimally, How do you know?
Evidence is not confidence. Evidentiality is often confused with certainty. The categories can interact, but they are conceptually different. A speaker may be completely confident in a report and uncertain about what they directly saw. Evidentiality marks the source or mode of access: visual observation, non-visual perception, inference, report, quotation, memory, or another conventional category. Epistemic modality marks possibility, probability, necessity, doubt, or commitment.
WALS separates languages with no grammatical evidentials, languages with indirect evidentials, and languages distinguishing direct from indirect sources. In its 418-language sample, 237 have some grammatical evidential marking; systems are distributed across verbal affixes, tense forms, particles, modal morphemes, and mixed strategies (de Haan 2013a, 2013b). The figures should not be treated as an inventory of all languages, but they make one fact undeniable: grammatical source marking is not an exotic anomaly.
Turkish famously contrasts a witnessed past in -di with an indirect or unwitnessed past in -miş in relevant uses. A simple pair such as Ahmet gel-di and Ahmet gel-miş can distinguish direct access from an event learned indirectly, although the forms have broader tense–aspect–modal functions and cannot be reduced to labels in an English table. Tuyuca and other Eastern Tucanoan languages have more elaborate systems. WALS examples include visual, non-visual sensory, inferential, and reportative distinctions. Koasati has an auditory evidential in examples where a speaker reports what an event sounds like. Aikhenvald’s comparative work documents systems with two terms and systems with six or more, along with the discourse rules that govern selection when evidence comes through several channels (Aikhenvald 2004).
European languages complicate the lazy contrast between “European” and “non-European” thought. Bulgarian has grammatical forms analysed in terms of evidentiality, renarration, inference, doubt, and mirativity. The precise semantics are debated; Koev, for example, analyses the Bulgarian -l form through the relation between the described event and the event of acquiring information (Koev 2017). Albanian admirative forms are associated with unexpected or newly realised information. Germanic modal constructions can develop evidential functions. The typological pattern is areal and historical, not a clean genetic divide.
Does evidential grammar improve source memory. The attractive hypothesis is that obligatory source marking trains source monitoring. Evidence is suggestive but not decisive. Research with Turkish-speaking children has linked mastery of evidential forms to the ability to track how knowledge was acquired, and some cross-linguistic comparisons report advantages in source-memory tasks (Aksu-Koç et al. 2009; Kandemirci et al. 2023). Yet language, schooling, conversational practice, and metacognitive development are difficult to separate. Experimental tasks may inadvertently favour the distinctions prominent in one language. Effects can depend on whether participants expect disagreement or later accountability.
The correct conclusion is therefore conditional. Evidential grammar is a plausible training regime for source attention; it is not a universal vaccine against credulity. Speakers can choose misleading evidentials, exploit conventional exceptions, or repeat false reports with impeccable source marking. What the grammar changes is the structure of omission. A bare claim becomes less normal.
This difference matters institutionally. Contemporary misinformation is not only a problem of false sentences. It is a problem of provenance collapse. A speculative preprint becomes a news article; the article becomes a social post; the post becomes a model-generated summary; the summary becomes “research shows.” At every transition, confidence may rise while contact with the original evidence falls. Citation alone does not prevent the collapse because citations are often copied without being opened, and secondary sources can form circular chains.
Four coordinates of an epistemic checksum. A useful designed system should mark more than sensory source. For scientific and computational communication, a minimal epistemic checksum would have four coordinates:
Term
Explanation
Source mode
observed, measured, inferred, simulated, reported, remembered, or generated
Provenance depth
direct, one transformation removed, two transformations removed, or chain unknown
Confidence state
calibrated probability or a small ordinal band
Revision status
exploratory, provisional, accepted-for-action, contested, superseded, or retracted
