In compact notation, a claim might appear as:
The sentence “The bridge is unsafe” should not stand alone. Its representation should also record that it rests on measurement, is one evidential hop from the observation, carries confidence 0.78, and remains provisional.
This is not proposed as literary English. It is a domain-specific layer for reports, dashboards, research environments, AI outputs, and public claims where epistemic detachment is costly. The notation makes visible what ordinary prose hides. Its fields also separate questions that hedging words blur. Apparently may mean reported, inferred, weakly believed, or politely distanced. The checksum does not force one ambiguous adverb to perform four jobs.
Source depth and the illusion of independent confirmation. Natural evidentials usually classify source type, not the full graph of transmission. Digital culture requires source depth and source independence. Suppose five articles report the same finding, but all derive from one press release. The number of visible sources is five; the number of independent evidential roots is one. Ordinary language encourages a counting error because it individuates documents rather than lineages.
A provenance grammar could mark whether a claim is firsthand, transformed from a primary record, aggregated from independent records, or copied through an unknown chain. It could also distinguish quotation from endorsement. Logophoric and reportative systems show that perspective and transmission can be grammatically tracked. Digital infrastructure can extend the principle with cryptographic provenance, persistent identifiers, and machine-readable evidence graphs.
The cognitive gain would be substantial even without perfect verification. A reader who sees roots:1 beside “reported by five outlets” is less likely to confuse repetition with corroboration. A model trained to preserve source depth would be less likely to launder speculation into fact through iterative summarisation.
Confidence is not a decorative hedge. English confidence terms—possibly, probably, almost certainly, I think—are context-sensitive and socially strategic. People use them to protect face, signal politeness, perform authority, or avoid commitment. Numeric probabilities can create false precision, but uncalibrated adjectives make learning from error difficult. A designed grammar should allow both: a coarse mandatory band for routine use and optional numerical calibration where data support it.
The critical innovation is not the number. It is the coupling between confidence and revision. A confidence marker should belong to a forecast or claim whose later outcome can be checked. Otherwise
“80 percent” becomes costume.
In scientific work, confidence should also distinguish statistical uncertainty, model uncertainty, measurement uncertainty, and uncertainty about whether the chosen question is adequate. These are not interchangeable.
The category unknown is equally overloaded. It can mean that nobody has checked, that available evidence is inconclusive, that the answer is practically inaccessible, that the concept is ill-posed, or that the answer may be unknowable in principle. Treating these states as one encourages both complacency and false certainty. The missing-variables analysis later in this book separates unknown now, unknown to a given community, discoverable with known methods, discoverable only after methodological innovation, and principled indeterminacy.
Mirativity: when information changes the model. Mirativity concerns unexpected information, the
“unprepared mind,” and the speaker’s surprise (Aikhenvald 2004).
Surprise is not merely an emotion added to a fact. It contains information about a prior model. If the result was unexpected, then either the world is unusual, the evidence is wrong, or the model was inadequate.
Science routinely wastes this information. Papers report a finding but rarely encode what the researchers predicted with enough precision to measure surprise. After results are known, narratives are reconstructed and anomalies become
“consistent with” a broad theory.
Preregistration partly addresses the problem by preserving prior commitments. A mirative marker could generalise the principle: model-fit, model-strain, or model-break would state whether the evidence fell within expected variation, required parameter revision, or contradicted the operative structure.
The marker would be valuable for AI systems as well. A system that merely emits confidence can remain confidently wrong within a misspecified model. A system that tracks model surprise can notice when incoming evidence lies outside the distribution its explanations assume. The distinction between
“I am unsure which familiar answer is correct” and “my current answer space may be wrong” deserves a stable linguistic form.
Memory, testimony, and generated claims. Human memory is reconstructive. Yet English commonly presents remembered events with the same declarative form as current observations.
“He said that” may be a verbatim record, a gist, an inference, or a later reconstruction. Legal systems ask witnesses to separate these states, but ordinary grammar does not help.
A future evidential inventory should therefore include memory mode: recorded, contemporaneously noted, repeatedly rehearsed, unaided recall, or reconstructed from later information. It should also distinguish a system’s own inference from generated text that merely resembles one. When an AI states
“I found that…,” the first person invites a false analogy with human discovery.
Machine outputs need markers for retrieval, transformation, interpolation, calculation, simulation, and ungrounded generation.
This is not a call to burden every conversation with metadata. Natural languages already vary register by context. Scientific claims, legal testimony, medical decisions, intelligence analysis, and high-impact automated outputs can sustain more explicit grammar than casual speech. The central design principle is selective obligatoriness: make a distinction mandatory where its omission creates systematic harm.
The politics of epistemic marking. Any grammar of knowing can become a grammar of authority. Institutions may treat direct observation as superior even when instruments or distributed testimony are more reliable. Marginalised speakers may be forced to over-document claims that elites can assert without markers. A platform may attach
“uncertain” to dissent while presenting official statements as unmarked truth.
Evidentiality therefore has a conceptual ethics.
The system must mark claims symmetrically, preserve disagreement, and distinguish source from credibility.
“Reported by the ministry” is a provenance statement, not a guarantee. “Generated by a model” is a production statement, not a refutation.
The aim is not to automate trust but to prevent invisible substitutions among observation, report, inference, and authority.
A well-designed epistemic grammar would change the default question from
“Is this sentence fluent?” to “What kind of epistemic object is this sentence?”
The answer would not settle whether it is true. It would make truth-seeking less blind.
The typological argument changes the SLEnglish design directly. Evidentiality, clusivity, number, classifier choice, possession, perspective tracking, and social deixis are not relegated to anthropological examples. They become candidate typed operators or profile requirements. The parser remains unchanged because the distinctions live in versioned libraries. Appendix D maps each construction to the proposed operator family and records where the mapping is only an engineering analogy.
Space, Time, Experience, and Conceptual Engineering
A child is shown a row of cards depicting a person at different ages and is asked to arrange them in time. An English speaker is likely to place earlier events to the left and later events to the right, especially in a literate setting where writing runs left to right. Rotate the child, move the experiment outdoors, or ask a speaker whose community uses absolute directions, and the answer may change. Time is not visible as a line. Languages and cultural practices recruit space to make it tractable.
This makes time one of the clearest domains in which linguistic diversity reveals cognitive alternatives. It also exposes the danger of overclaiming. Spatial metaphors can guide reasoning, but task meaning, writing direction, gesture, environment, and experimental framing interact. The aim is not to announce that communities
“see time differently” in some total sense.
It is to identify the representational tools they habitually recruit.
Relative bodies and absolute worlds. English spatial description is often egocentric or object-centred: left, right, in front of, behind. Many languages make extensive use of absolute frames based on cardinal directions, prevailing winds, coast–inland axes, uphill–downhill, or stable landscape features. To say where an object is, a speaker may need to know orientation beyond the local body.
Stephen Levinson’s comparative programme argued that linguistic frames correlate with memory, gesture, navigation, and non-verbal spatial reasoning (Levinson 2003). Pederson and colleagues found cross-community alignment between preferred linguistic frames and spatial conceptualisation (1998). Haun and colleagues later demonstrated both developmental effects and cognitive plasticity: participants could use alternative frames, but switching imposed a cost (2011).
The important point is not that absolute speakers possess a mysterious faculty. Conversational practice rewards continuous orientation. If ordinary speech requires something like
“the cup is north of the plate,” losing north repeatedly creates communicative failure.
Grammar and habit turn environmental information into maintained cognitive state.
A comparable deficit exists in modern organisational language. Teams discuss a system component
“upstream,” a risk “down the road,” and a decision “behind us,” but often lack a shared map of causal or temporal position.
We use spatial metaphors without the discipline of an actual coordinate system. A designed conceptual language could require a scale address and system address for claims whose meaning changes with position.
When the past is in front. English conventionally places the future ahead and the past behind. Aymara has attracted attention because lexical and gestural patterns associate the known past with the front and the unseen future with the back. Núñez and Sweetser interpret this through an experiential logic: what has been witnessed lies before the knower; what has not yet occurred cannot be seen and is therefore behind (2006). Later work in other languages has found additional spatialisations, reminding us that
“time moves forward” is not an inevitable cognitive primitive.
The philosophical gain is not a new correct orientation. It is the realisation that temporal metaphors encode epistemology. English ahead suggests goal-directed movement into a visible path. Aymara-style imagery foregrounds the asymmetry between known history and unknowable future. Both reveal one relation while hiding another. Policy discourse needs both: the future is where action is directed, but it is also what cannot yet be inspected.
A richer temporal vocabulary might distinguish action-front from knowledge-front. The future is in front as the target of intervention and behind as the region outside observation. The past is behind as no longer alterable and in front as available evidence. Instead of choosing one metaphor, we can mark which relation is active.
Grammatical distance. English past tense does not normally encode how remote an event is. Time adverbs do the work. Many languages have remoteness distinctions: today’s past, yesterday’s past, recent past, remote past, or legendary past. WALS reports that a substantial minority of sampled languages with past marking make two or more grammatical remoteness distinctions. Yagua is described with five past ranges, from events a few hours earlier to distant or legendary time (Dahl and Velupillai 2013).
These categories demonstrate that temporal distance can be integrated into routine grammar. Yet copying clock intervals would miss the opportunity. Human decisions are organised not only by elapsed time but by institutional horizon. The same delay can be near for climate policy and remote for emergency medicine. A quarterly profit is immediate to a company, while species loss is treated as distant even when irreversible processes begin now.
SLEnglish should therefore use functional horizons:
Term
Explanation
operational
within the cycle of the current action system
strategic
beyond current operations but within planned institutional control
generational
extending beyond present decision-makers
civilisational
affecting durable social or planetary conditions
deep-time
exceeding ordinary institutional continuity
A sentence may state that a technology is beneficial over the operational horizon but hazardous over a generational horizon. Naming both horizons prevents one timescale from impersonating all others.
Aspect and the internal shape of events. Tense locates an event relative to speech; aspect describes its temporal structure—ongoing, completed, repeated, habitual, begun, terminated, or viewed as a whole. Slavic languages often force perfective–imperfective choices more systematically than English, which distributes aspect through auxiliaries, verb forms, lexical choice, and context. Other languages grammatically encode repetition, distribution, or phases of action in different ways.
Aspect is philosophically important because events are not points.
“The company changed its policy” can present a completed transition, while “the company was changing its policy” opens an unfinished process. “The model failed” may refer to one event, repeated failures, a persistent disposition, or degradation over time. Public arguments often shift among these aspectual readings.
Systems thinking needs a further distinction between state, event, process, trend, and regime change. English has these nouns, but they are not obligatory frames. A rising temperature can be a measurement event, a local trend, a feedback-driven process, or evidence of a regime transition. A grammatical event-type marker would reduce category errors, particularly in dashboards and risk reports.
Temporal granularity and institutional amnesia. Every temporal representation chooses a grain. A company dashboard aggregates by month; a news feed by minute; a historian by decade. Events visible at one grain vanish at another. A short-term intervention can look successful because delayed costs fall outside the window. A noisy series can look stable after aggregation. A continuous process can be falsely divided into annual units because budgeting demands it.
The missing distinction is temporal granularity. Claims should indicate the interval over which variation is measured and the horizon over which consequences are judged. This is not merely statistical metadata. It changes ontology. A
“crisis” can be a spike in a long decline, while apparent stability can be a plateau inside accelerating structural change.
Institutions also have memory horizons. Staff turnover, document loss, changing metrics, and political incentives create institutional amnesia. A policy repeatedly rediscovered as new may belong to the same causal lineage even when its name changes. A temporal grammar linked to provenance could mark recurrence: new-form/old-lineage, first-observed-here, or institution-forgotten. Such markers would be valuable in science, where abandoned ideas return, and in governance, where recurring failures are treated as surprises.
Future people and grammatical absence. Future generations are difficult to represent because they lack names, votes, contracts, and present speech acts. English refers to them as an abstract plural. The grammar of agency privileges existing persons: we decide, they will inherit. The pronouns encode a moral asymmetry without making it explicit.
A posterity-scope marker could attach to decisions and benefits: The marker does not solve population ethics or determine how future welfare should be weighted. It prevents temporal externalities from disappearing into an unmarked future. It also distinguishes future people whose existence is highly probable from specific persons whose identity depends on present choices—the non-identity problem in moral philosophy.
Term
Explanation
Present benefit
The benefit is received by participants who can influence the present decision.
Future cost
The cost is transferred to future people who cannot participate in the present decision.
A grammar for multiple clocks. Modern systems run on many clocks at once. Biological repair, software release, financial settlement, legal appeal, political mandate, educational development, infrastructure decay, and climate feedback have different characteristic times. A decision can be reversible on one clock and irreversible on another. A pause that is trivial for bureaucracy can be fatal in medicine; a fast optimisation can lock a society into decades of infrastructure.
The proposed temporal address therefore has three fields: The proposed temporal description therefore records the grain of observation, the horizon of evaluation, and the window within which reversal remains possible.
For example: The intervention appears efficient when evaluated weekly over one quarter, and it can be reversed within approximately thirty days.
The sentence no longer masquerades as a scale-free truth. Another evaluation may show: The same intervention appears harmful when evaluated annually over a generational horizon, and no known reversal procedure is available.
Both can be correct. The contradiction existed only because English suppressed coordinates.
Would this language improve foresight? An experiment could give teams complex policy scenarios with delayed effects. One group would reason in ordinary prose; another would be required to tag grain, horizon, and reversibility. Outcomes could measure recognition of delayed costs, consistency across time scales, and resistance to present bias. The markers might add burden without benefit, but that is an empirical result, not a philosophical defeat.
We cannot decide where tomorrow
“really” is.
We can decide not to discuss it as though all clocks were one.
The argument now changes register. The first ten chapters identified distinctions that natural English often treats cheaply: source of knowledge, identity through change, collective agency, causal depth, temporal scale, reversibility, provenance, and several forms of uncertainty. One response would be lexical reform: invent better words and hope that communities adopt them. Another would be interface design: ask better questions at high-stakes moments. Both remain useful. Neither is sufficient for machines that must store, compare, reason over, and execute knowledge.
A machine cannot execute a philosophical recommendation to
“be clearer about sameness.”
It requires a typed representation of the entities, the two states being compared, the mode of continuity, the criterion, the temporal interval, the degree, and the evidence. Nor is a conventional controlled sentence enough if it merely removes syntactic ambiguity while preserving conceptual underspecification. The research programme therefore moves from cognitive prostheses to an executable intermediate language.
SLEnglish is not meant to replace ordinary English. It is a compiled semantic layer. An unrestricted sentence remains the human source; an LLM proposes SLEnglish; deterministic tools parse, type, validate, reason, and execute it; and a renderer can produce prose again. The original text, the compiled representation, and the proof or execution trace remain linked. This architecture changes what the target language should optimise. Because humans do not need to author every line unaided, naturalness can be traded for regularity and semantic visibility.
The history of artificial language contains a recurring seduction. If ordinary languages are irregular, ambiguous, culturally partial, and metaphysically confused, perhaps a rational designer can replace them with a transparent system. John Wilkins classified the universe into a hierarchy intended to support a
“real character.”
Leibniz imagined a characteristica universalis in which disputes might be converted into calculation. Later projects pursued logical unambiguity, international communication, feminist expression, minimalism, artistic intensity, or extreme semantic compression. Loglan and Lojban explicitly connected engineered language with the possibility of testing linguistic relativity; Lojban developed a machine-readable formal grammar. Ithkuil explored an extraordinary density of grammatical distinctions. Láadan made evidential and experiential categories central to a language designed from feminist concerns.
These projects are intellectually valuable even when they fail as mass communication. They expose a fundamental choice: should language mirror a pre-existing catalogue of reality, or should it help users act within an open and contested world?
The failure of the perfect taxonomy. Seventeenth-century philosophical languages often assumed that concepts could be placed in one rational tree. The taxonomic ambition produced ingenuity and brittleness. A position in the vocabulary encoded a theory of what kind of thing an entity was. When the theory changed, the name became misleading. Borges’s fictional Chinese encyclopedia remains funny because every classification can be made to look absurd from outside its purpose.
Modern knowledge makes the problem worse. The same object participates in many ontologies. A virus is a molecular structure, evolutionary lineage, pathogen, information carrier, legal object, security concern, and ecological actor. No single hierarchical address captures all legitimate relations. A universal language that fixes one place for the virus would confuse consistency with truth.
Jaap Maat’s historical study of Dalgarno, Wilkins, and Leibniz shows that their projects were more diverse and context-sensitive than caricature suggests (2004). Yet the central lesson remains: a language for better thought should not pretend to finish ontology. It should make perspectives, criteria, and modelling choices explicit.
This is why the present project uses operators rather than a complete world dictionary. A legal boundary can coexist with a statistical boundary, and process continuity can coexist with continuity of role. The system makes the relevant axis visible without declaring one axis universally fundamental.
Conceptual engineering, not word worship. Contemporary philosophy calls the deliberate assessment and improvement of concepts conceptual engineering. Herman Cappelen’s Fixing Language examines how words and representational devices can be defective and how difficult intentional meaning change can be (2018). The
volume edited by Burgess, Cappelen, and Plunkett maps disputes about targets, methods, ethics, and
implementation (2020). David Chalmers proposes a broad definition: conceptual engineering includes the design, implementation, and evaluation of concepts, both by repairing existing ones and creating new ones (2020).
This formulation improves the project in two ways. First, design without implementation is only private notation. A concept succeeds when a community can learn it, use it, negotiate its boundaries, and integrate it into institutions. Second, implementation without evaluation can make language worse. A new category may stigmatise, oversimplify, centralise power, or create false certainty.
Conceptual engineering is therefore closer to protocol design than dictionary writing. A protocol needs semantics, syntax, error handling, governance, interoperability, adoption incentives, and tests. Concepts also need deprecation. A term that once improved analysis may become misleading as reality changes.
From analysis to a compiled language. Vocabulary, annotations, structured schemas, and context-sensitive interpretation can be represented inside one stronger architecture. Ordinary prose remains the source layer. SLEnglish is the compiled semantic layer. The practical task of the linguistic chapters is to decide which distinctions deserve first-class verbs, which belong as named parameters, which are type refinements, and which should remain optional contextual metadata. The appendices record these decisions as a revisable operator registry.
This unification matters. Without a canonical intermediate representation, every interface invents its own tags, every ontology binds them differently, and every reasoner requires an adapter. The project becomes a collection of demonstrations rather than an accumulating research programme. A stable target language lets us compare translators, circuits, reasoners, and user interfaces while holding the representation constant.
The language must nevertheless resist the ambition of a final ontology. A virus can be represented simultaneously as a molecular assembly, evolutionary lineage, pathogen, information carrier, legal object, and ecological actor. SLEnglish does not decide which view is metaphysically privileged. It lets modules define perspectives and requires claims to identify the context, basis, or profile under which a relation is asserted. Formalisation should expose modelling choices, not conceal them behind a universal taxonomy.
Selective obligatoriness. Natural grammar teaches that obligation creates attention. Interface design teaches that too many mandatory fields create evasion, noise, and box-ticking. SLEnglish therefore applies obligation by domain.
Scientific synthesis should require source mode, provenance depth, revision state, and uncertainty type. High-impact automated decisions should require identity of the evaluated system, affected parties, responsibility architecture, and appeal reversibility. Infrastructure proposals should require temporal horizon, threshold distance, externality vector, and option loss. Public polling claims should require population, sample, consensus topology, and scale.
Poetry should require none of these. Intimate conversation should not become an audit log. Even scientific discovery needs unstructured phases in which concepts are unstable. A marker can be mandatory at the transition from exploration to public claim without being mandatory during imagination.
The principle is: Make distinctions obligatory at the boundary where private ambiguity becomes public consequence.
Design criteria. A candidate distinction should meet six criteria.
Term
Explanation
Error relevance
It addresses a recurring failure with meaningful cost, not merely an interesting philosophical nuance
Compression
It expresses a structure substantially more cheaply than ordinary paraphrase
Compositionality
It combines predictably with other terms and can be nested, compared, or negated
Auditability
Users can ask why a marker was chosen and what evidence would change it
Pluralism
It can state model dependence and contested criteria rather than freezing one worldview
Learnability
People can use it with limited training and without constant reference to a manual
A seventh criterion should be added for AI-mediated environments: machine tractability without machine sovereignty. The distinction should be representable in software, but the software should not silently settle normative choices. A model can prompt for boundary mode; it should not decide which people truly belong to a community.
AI as translator, critic, and danger. Artificial intelligence makes the project more feasible and more urgent. A language model can translate ordinary prose into a structured claim, ask for missing coordinates, preserve tags through summarisation, and render the result back into readable English. It can detect that “same system” shifts from legal to technical identity or that “we decided” hides an exclusive decision-maker.
The same model can also produce the illusion of precision. It may invent provenance, assign numerical confidence without calibration, or turn ambiguous social judgments into categorical fields. Structured language can launder bias more effectively than vague prose because schemas appear objective.
AI should therefore act in three distinct roles. As translator, it proposes structure while preserving the source text. As critic, it identifies omitted distinctions and alternative interpretations. As executor, it may apply validated rules only when governance authorises them. The trace must distinguish the model’s extraction from the human’s endorsement.
This architecture directly uses the perspective distinctions developed above: An AI-assisted text may have a human author, a structure proposed by a model, a verification supplied by a reviewer, and policy effects produced by the surrounding system. Those roles should remain distinct.
Without such separation, AI-generated conceptual engineering becomes conceptual colonisation by whichever model and dataset dominate the interface.
Why words alone fail. Many reformers assume that once a term exists, cognition improves. History says otherwise. Technical concepts become slogans; precise distinctions blur in popular use; institutions appropriate critical vocabulary without changing practice. Sustainability, resilience, AI, innovation, and evidence-based demonstrate semantic inflation.
A concept survives only if practices reinforce it. Probability becomes useful through scoring rules, forecasting records, and feedback. Provenance becomes useful through citation systems, identifiers, and audits. Reversibility becomes useful through staged deployment and actual rollback mechanisms. A new word without an operational practice can become another layer of rhetoric.
SLEnglish therefore links each term to an action. A source marker links to evidence. A revision marker links to version history. A Goodhart marker links to metric audit. A responsibility vector links to control and remediation. The language is a front end for institutions of correction.
Cultural humility and the danger of extraction. A book that harvests grammatical features from minority and Indigenous languages risks reproducing the very Anglocentrism it criticises. It can treat living languages as warehouses of clever devices, detach categories from communities, or imply that an English-speaking designer has finally recognised their value.
The response must be more than a disclaimer. Examples should be cited to grammars and scholars, checked with speakers where possible, and presented with uncertainty. Communities should share authority and benefit when their linguistic knowledge becomes part of a product or research programme. Natural categories should not be copied under the assumption that a brief English label captures their meaning. Often the correct lesson is structural—“source can be obligatory”—rather than lexical—“borrow this morpheme.”
Nor should languages be ranked by depth. Every language enables subtle thought and every language has habitual omissions. English is criticised here because it has unusual global power, not because it is intrinsically primitive.
A language that can admit its own defects. The final design criterion is reflexivity. SLEnglish must be able to say that its own categories are provisional, contested, scale-dependent, and shaped by purpose. Each schema needs a revision state and boundary grade. Each formalisation should preserve a link to unstructured language and dissenting interpretations.
The ideal cognitive prosthesis is not a cage made of better concepts. It is an interface that makes its handles visible.
Three grades of executability. The phrase needs separate meanings because parsing, reasoning, and authorised external action impose different guarantees.
Grade
Meaning
Structural executability
A statement can be parsed, typed, canonicalised, checked against a schema, compared across versions, and linked to its source.
Epistemic executability
A system can ask questions, derive consequences, detect conflicts, trace provenance, and explain answers under a declared reasoning profile.
Operational executability
Validated semantic nodes may invoke tools, workflows, simulations, agents, or laboratory procedures only through explicit authority and policy.
The proposed language primarily targets structural and epistemic executability. Operational effects should remain outside the language kernel and be mediated by declared interpreters and governance policies. This separation prevents a claim such as “the sample should be heated” from silently becoming a command to heat it. A semantic proposition may describe, prescribe, hypothesise, or query an action; only an authorised runtime may turn the proposition into an external effect.
The distinction also clarifies safety. Deterministic syntax does not make an action safe. Typed roles do not establish that a source is trustworthy. A proof establishes derivability under premises, not truth of the premises. Executability increases power and auditability simultaneously; governance determines whether the power should be exercised.
Term
Explanation
Linguistic relativity
The defensible thesis concerns salience, habitual attention, compression, and obligatory marking rather than cognitive imprisonment (Lucy 1992a, 1997; Wolff and Holmes 2011; Everett 2013).
Typological diversity
WALS and Grambank document broad structural diversity while warning against treating English categories as universal defaults (Dryer and Haspelmath 2013; Skirgård et al. 2023).
Space and time
Cross-linguistic work on spatial frames and temporal metaphors shows measurable diversity with careful limits on causal interpretation (Levinson 2003; Núñez and Sweetser 2006; Haun et al. 2011).
Evidentiality Grammatical source marking is widespread, but experimental evidence for broad cognitive improvement remains mixed and domain-dependent (Aikhenvald 2004; de Haan 2013a, 2013b; Kandemirci et al. 2023).
The survey is preliminary to the operator inventories rather than an independent catalogue of rare forms. Its engineering use is to show that recurrent semantic distinctions can be conventionalised, compressed, and made difficult to omit. The appendices translate that observation into candidate SLEnglish contracts for evidence, perspective, participant structure, time, space, possession, identity, discourse, and social relation.
The mapping is deliberately non-literal. A natural grammatical category may combine several functions, vary across languages, or depend on discourse practice. SLEnglish operators are analytical constructions with declared parameters and tests; they do not claim to reproduce a language-specific morpheme exhaustively. Specialist review remains necessary, and a category should be retained only when it improves translation, reasoning, or audit in the intended domain.
CHAPTER 4
Identity, Collective Agency, and Systemic Concepts
Qualified Identity, Process, Boundaries, and Infinity
At noon, a cloud hangs above the lake. At 12:05 it has moved, expanded, lost water, gained water, split at one edge, and merged with vapour at another. A child points and says,
“That cloud is getting darker.”
The sentence presupposes that the subject persists through change. Without that presupposition, there is no it to get darker. Yet no sharp boundary tells us which droplets compose the cloud, and no material core remains fixed.
Philosophers use clouds to stage the Problem of the Many. Around the vague object are many overlapping aggregates of droplets, each nearly as good a candidate as the others for being the cloud. If one aggregate is the cloud, what disqualifies its almost-identical neighbours? If many aggregates are clouds, why does perception present one? If the boundary is indeterminate, what happens to ordinary identity claims? The problem is not a linguistic trick, but language helps us glide over it. English supplies a count noun, a singular demonstrative, and the relation same. The grammatical package gives a crisp handle to a diffuse process.
The handle is useful. Human action would stall if every reference required a metaphysical inquiry. But when the same linguistic machinery is carried from clouds to organisms, software, firms, states, and artificial agents, useful compression becomes conceptual debt. The word same answers too many questions at once.
Seven continuities hiding inside one word. Consider seven relations that English routinely calls identity.
Continuity criterion
Explanation
Material continuity
asks whether roughly the same matter persists. A repaired stone wall may remain materially continuous; a flame does not. Even material continuity comes in degrees because matter is lost and replaced
Structural continuity
asks whether the organisation or pattern persists. A whirlpool can maintain a form while water passes through it. A company may preserve its reporting structure while every employee changes
Process continuity
asks whether later stages belong to one uninterrupted activity. A flame persists as combustion, a conversation as reciprocal interaction, and an organism as a network of self-maintaining processes
Causal continuity
asks whether each stage arises through the appropriate causal path from its predecessor. A perfect replica assembled elsewhere may match structurally while lacking the same causal history
Genealogical continuity
asks whether one entity descends from another through copying, reproduction, transformation, or branching. This is central to organisms, texts, software repositories, machine-learning models, and institutions
Role continuity
asks whether a socially defined position persists. “The president,” “the court,” and “the company” may remain the same role-bearing entity despite replacement of persons, rules, and material infrastructure
Informational continuity
asks whether an abstract content is preserved. Two files can instantiate the same bit sequence on different media; two models can implement the same function with different numerical representations; two editions can preserve a text while changing typography
A final variable cuts across all seven: scale. At molecular scale, a body is never the same from moment to moment. At the scale of legal responsibility, it may be continuous for a lifetime. Identity is often not a property simpliciter but a relation indexed to purpose, granularity, and tolerated variation.
English can express every item in this list. The problem is that it normally does not.
“Is this the same model?” invites a binary answer where the real question may be: same(model₁, model₂ | weights, architecture, behaviour, lineage, governance, scale) Without an identity parameter, disagreements can remain invisible. One engineer means identical weights; another means equivalent benchmark behaviour; a regulator means the same accountable system; a user means the service with the same name. All can sincerely answer different questions with the word same.
Things may be stabilised processes. Western metaphysics is often narrated as a conflict between substance and process, with Heraclitus and Whitehead representing processual alternatives to a world of enduring things. The historical picture is more complex, and no natural language maps neatly onto a philosophical doctrine. Still, contemporary philosophy of biology gives a rigorous reason to revisit the noun-centred picture.
In Everything Flows, Nicholson and Dupré argue that living systems are better understood as interdependent processes stabilised at different timescales than as substances that merely undergo change (2018). Metabolism, development, immune regulation, symbiosis, and ecological interaction are not optional activities performed by a pre-existing organism. They partly constitute the organism’s persistence. DiFrisco examines criteria by which biological processes can be identified through variation, and Pradeu develops the idea of genidentity—continuity across a series of states—particularly for cases involving metamorphosis, fusion, or fission.
The process view does not abolish objects. It asks what work object language is doing. A rock may be treated as a relatively slow process; a storm as a fast, open one; an organism as a self-producing network; an institution as a norm-governed pattern maintained through documents, roles, memory, and enforcement. The difference lies partly in characteristic timescale and maintenance regime.
This suggests a replacement for the question “What is X?” when X is dynamic: What must continue happening, at what scale, and within what tolerance, for later stages to count as the continuation of X?
For a cloud, the answer may involve atmospheric dynamics and spatiotemporal continuity. For a river, channel, flow regime, watershed, name, and legal designation compete. For a person, bodily, psychological, narrative, relational, and legal continuities overlap without perfectly coinciding. For a company, control, contracts, brand, assets, organisational memory, and decision procedures matter differently to different observers.
Identity in software and AI. Digital systems reveal the inadequacy of ordinary identity with unusual clarity because copies can be exact while histories diverge.
Suppose model A is copied byte-for-byte to create model B. At the moment of copying, A and B are informationally identical but numerically distinct deployments. If B is fine-tuned, it remains genealogically descended from A but is no longer weight-identical. If A is quantised, architecture and intended function may remain while numerical representation changes. If a serving layer adds retrieval, tools, memory, and policy, users may encounter a system whose behaviour differs substantially despite the same base model. If two companies independently train models that converge on similar behaviour, they may be functionally similar without shared lineage.
Blockchain systems add governance to the puzzle. After a fork, which branch is the original? The answer may depend on state continuity, community recognition, economic weight, protocol rules, name, or normative interpretation. “Original” is not a purely technical property once lineage branches.
The emerging legal and scientific vocabulary of AI will fail if it treats “a model” as a simple object. Audits, licences, safety evaluations, and responsibility need identity criteria. A benchmark result attached to one checkpoint does not automatically transfer to a fine-tuned or tool-augmented descendant. A risk assessment of a service cannot be inherited merely because the marketing name remains stable. The grammar of identity is infrastructure for governance.
The proposed identity operators. Rather than invent unrelated words, SLEnglish can begin with compositional operators: The terms are deliberately plain. Their value lies not in elegance but in preventing a binary predicate from swallowing its parameter. A claim such as
Term
Explanation
matter-same
materially continuous within a stated tolerance
form-same
structurally or organisationally continuous
flow-same
one continuing process
line-same
causally or genealogically continuous
role-same
continuous under a social, institutional, or legal role
info-same
informationally equivalent under a declared representation
effect-same
functionally or behaviourally equivalent for a specified test domain
“The upgraded system is line-same and role-same, but not info-same or effect-same” is immediately more informative than “It is the same system.”
These operators require two additional annotations. Tolerance states how much change is permitted. Scale states the level at which comparison is made. The full form becomes: At the scale of the storm system, two observations may count as the same continuing flow when the interruption is shorter than ten minutes.
or Two models may count as effect-equivalent for diagnostic triage among European adults when their relevant outcome difference remains within a two-percent tolerance.
This may look technical, but ordinary language already embeds hidden parameters.
“The same temperature” means within measurement precision; “the same route” tolerates small deviations; “the same story” tolerates wording changes.
Explicit notation is needed only when hidden tolerances matter.
Gradual identity and the tyranny of binary classification. Some continuities are not yes-or-no. A language, city, ecosystem, or institution can change gradually until the point at which observers cease to treat it as the same. There may be no unique threshold. Binary identity is often a decision imposed for law, record keeping, or action, not a discovered boundary.
A boundary-grade marker would distinguish:
Term
Explanation
sharp boundary
sharp boundary fixed by rule
operational boundary fixed
operational boundary fixed for a purpose
fuzzy boundary
fuzzy boundary with central and peripheral cases
contested boundary
contested boundary with rival criteria
indeterminate boundary where
indeterminate boundary where available facts do not settle membership
The distinction matters for clouds, species, diseases, political movements, markets, and AI capabilities. Calling a model
“autonomous,” a conflict “war,” or an organisation “terrorist” can activate legal and moral regimes.
The world may not provide a sharp natural seam; institutions nevertheless need a rule. A boundary-grade marker would make the imposed character of the rule visible without making action impossible.
Identity as a vector, not a verdict. The strongest proposal of this chapter is that identity should often be represented as a vector of continuities rather than a single verdict. For entities A and B: A comparison between two entities should distinguish material, structural, processual, causal, genealogical, role-based, informational, and functional continuity rather than compressing them into one relation.
Each component can be binary, graded, or unresolved. A legal system may then define which components are decisive for a particular purpose. A biologist, engineer, historian, and regulator may use different projections of the same vector without pretending that one possesses the metaphysically complete answer.
Would such a vocabulary change thought? The hypothesis is testable. Participants could evaluate Ship-of-Theseus cases, corporate transformations, software forks, and biological metamorphoses. One group would use ordinary same; another would learn the identity operators. Researchers could measure consistency, transfer across domains, detection of hidden disagreement, and calibration to stated criteria. The expected benefit is not a single correct metaphysics. It is a lower rate of unnoticed equivocation.
A cloud does not need a passport. But the institutions, models, and collective systems we are building increasingly do. Before issuing one, we should decide what kind of sameness it certifies.
Numbers feel less like words than like features of reality. Three stones remain three stones whether one says three, trois, tres, or nothing. Yet the perceptual world does not deliver exact symbolic number in the form mathematics requires. It offers small quantities that can be recognised rapidly, larger magnitudes that can be estimated, and collections that can be matched one-to-one. A conventional counting system turns these capacities into an indefinitely extendable technology.
This distinction is visible in research with communities whose languages lack extensive exact number vocabularies. Pirahã speakers can compare quantities and perform direct matching, but exact performance declines when objects must be remembered or transformed rather than aligned one-to-one (Gordon 2004; Frank et al. 2008). Mundurukú speakers show sophisticated approximate arithmetic despite a restricted exact number lexicon (Pica et al. 2004). Later work argues that exact concepts are constrained by the range of available count words and practices (Pitt, Gibson, and Piantadosi 2022).
These findings have provoked methodological and interpretive debate, as they should. Language, schooling, notation, material practices, and task familiarity are entangled. The safe conclusion is not that people without a long count list
“cannot think number.”
It is that exact cardinal symbols help preserve and transform quantity across time, memory, and operation. Number words are not mere labels attached to a complete prelinguistic arithmetic. They are parts of a cognitive machine.
When counting conceals the unit. Modern societies count compulsively: users, jobs, papers, deaths, incidents, companies, species, models, and votes. The arithmetic can be exact while the ontology is unstable. What is one active user? One disease case? One AI model? One conflict? One scientific contribution? An exact number attached to an underdefined unit creates the appearance of precision.
Numeral-classifier systems remind us that counting can require a category mediating between numeral and noun. Their conventional classes are not solutions to contemporary measurement, but the grammatical principle is powerful: one must state what kind of one is being counted.
A designed count classifier could mark the unit principle:
Term
Explanation
Bounded physical object
a unit individuated primarily by a physical boundary
Individuated event
a unit defined as one occurrence under an event-counting rule
Continuing process
a unit defined by the persistence of an organised dynamic rather than fixed matter
Legal unit
an entity whose countable identity is fixed by law, contract, or registry
Lineage unit
a genealogical continuation that may survive copies, branches, or versions
Deployment unit
one operationally instantiated system in a particular environment
Statistical cluster
a grouping whose unity depends on a stated method and parameterisation
Thus
“twenty models” becomes “twenty deployments of four lineages” or “twenty weight files from one architecture.”
The number becomes more complex and more honest.
Zero and the invention of absence. Zero is often described as a discovery, an invention, or both. The physical world contains empty containers and missing expected objects, but the mathematical zero is not identical to any particular absence. It functions as a number, a placeholder, an identity element, a coordinate, and a boundary between signs. Stable notation allowed absence to participate in calculation.
Ordinary English still handles absence badly.
“There is no evidence” can mean that nobody looked, that a search found nothing, that available methods cannot detect it, that evidence once existed but was lost, or that the hypothesis predicts no observable trace.
“The feature is absent” may mean nonexistence, non-detection, deactivation, irrelevance, or failure of a classification rule.
We need at least four negative states:
Term
Explanation
empty
a defined container has zero members
missing
an expected item is not present
undetected
a search did not find an item within known sensitivity
nonexistent-under-model
the operative ontology contains no such item
A fifth, uninstantiated, applies to a possible type with no current token. A sixth, inapplicable, says that the question itself does not fit the object. Databases often collapse all six into null. Natural language often does the same.
The several infinities hidden in infinity. The word infinite performs even more work than same. It can mean that a process has no last step, that a set is treated as a completed totality, that a magnitude is unbounded, that a series diverges, that a mathematical object has transfinite cardinality, that resolution continues without practical limit, or merely that something is unimaginably large.
The historical distinction between potential infinity and actual infinity remains useful. Potential infinity characterises a process that can always be continued: for every number, another can be produced. Actual infinity treats an infinite collection or structure as complete enough to quantify over mathematically. Modern set theory formalises actual infinite sets; Cantor’s work distinguishes different infinite cardinalities. Analysis uses limits to reason rigorously about unbounded processes and arbitrarily fine variation.
The claim that infinity has “no counterpart anywhere in the real world” is too strong. Whether physical space, time, divisibility, field values, or the cosmos instantiate any form of actual infinity is a live question in physics and metaphysics. Some theories contain infinities; some physicists treat singularities as signs of theoretical breakdown; cosmological observations do not settle whether the universe is spatially finite. The responsible claim is narrower: human beings do not encounter a completed infinite collection through ordinary perception, yet formal infinity has transformed mathematics, theology, cosmology, economics, and culture. The Stanford Encyclopedia’s recent survey rightly treats mathematical, physical, cosmological, and theological infinity as related but distinct problems (Easwaran et al. 2025).
A better ordinary vocabulary should separate:
Term
Explanation
endless
no terminal stage is specified
unbounded
no finite upper bound exists in the model
divergent
a sequence or process fails to converge in a specified way
actual-infinite
a completed mathematical totality governed by an axiom system
transfinite
belonging to formal orders of infinite size
practically inexhaustible
finite or unknown in extent but impossible to enumerate with available resources
outfinit
open-ended beyond every current operational boundary while every realised stage remains finite
The last term is a deliberate conceptual invention. Outfinit is not a substitute for mathematical infinity. It names systems whose continuation has no relevant designed endpoint, although every physical or computational realisation is bounded. An expanding research programme, evolutionary process, generative search, or indefinitely extensible proof practice can be outfinit without containing a completed infinite object. The term protects us from two errors: treating the unfinishable as literally infinite, and treating finite implementation as evidence of a final conceptual limit.
Infinity as cultural operator. Infinity does not remain inside mathematics. Religions use it for divine attributes and eternity. Economics invokes indefinite growth. Technology markets “unlimited” storage or scale while relying on finite infrastructures. Political narratives imagine permanent institutions and inexhaustible progress. Personal identity is sometimes invested in digital traces expected to persist without horizon.
These transfers are rarely innocent. A model that assumes infinite substitutability of resources can erase ecological thresholds. A cultural image of endless growth makes saturation or sufficiency appear as failure. Conversely, an overreaction against infinity can cripple abstraction: calculus, probability, topology, and modern physics rely on disciplined idealisation.
The relevant distinction is between formal permission and ontological commitment. One may quantify over infinite structures inside a mathematical theory without claiming that the universe contains a corresponding completed object. One may use an infinite-horizon optimisation while recognising that institutions and agents are finite. A grammar for infinity should mark the mode: The vocabulary should distinguish formal infinity, infinity as a physical hypothesis, procedural endlessness, and operational outfinity.
Much philosophical confusion occurs when a property moves silently from one mode to another.
Negative, imaginary, and probabilistic objects. Negative numbers once appeared less natural than positive counts; imaginary numbers carried their scepticism in their name. Their power came from rules of operation and integration into systems, not from resemblance to visible objects. Probability similarly converts uncertainty into quantities that can be combined, updated, scored, and compared—imperfectly, but far beyond vague confidence words.
These cases support the book’s constructive thesis. A useful concept need not correspond to a simple object. It must provide stable distinctions, compositional operations, inferential discipline, and practical gain. The goal for collective agency or identity is not to find a hidden
“thing” matching the term.
It is to create representations that support better reasoning.
The analogy has limits. Mathematical symbols work in highly controlled systems; social and metaphysical concepts are open-textured and norm-laden. A responsibility vector cannot attain the universality of arithmetic. But it can still outperform a single vague noun, just as a multidimensional medical profile can improve on the binary
“healthy/sick” without becoming pure mathematics.
What else is hidden by exactness. Modern metrics often combine exact numbers with uncertain constructs. A university ranks seventh; a model scores 91.3; a country has a freedom score of 78; a worker has a productivity index. The precision belongs to the calculation, not necessarily to the concept. Goodhart’s law captures one failure mode: when a measure becomes a target, optimisation can detach it from the underlying goal.
A metric-mode marker should therefore distinguish measurement, proxy, target, and constitutive rule. Temperature is measured; citation count is a proxy for influence; a benchmark becomes a target when developers optimise against it; a legal threshold can constitute eligibility rather than merely detect it. The same number behaves differently in each mode.
Counting is among humanity’s greatest cognitive achievements. Its next refinement may be learning when the unit, boundary, and mode of infinity are doing more philosophical work than the numeral.
Collective Agency, Causation, and Distributed Responsibility
Ten thousand people stand in a railway station. Ten thousand people march toward parliament. Ten thousand birds change direction in a wave. Ten thousand employees contribute to a corporation. Ten thousand citizens are sampled in an opinion poll. English can call each collection a group. The noun performs useful compression, but it erases the very property that makes collective phenomena difficult: the relation between members and the whole.
A crowd can act without deciding. An organisation can decide without every member agreeing. A market can produce a price nobody intended. A state can persist while governments and citizens change. A swarm can display coordinated motion without central control. A committee can issue a judgment that no member would have written alone. When we say
“the group did X,” we may attribute statistical regularity, coordinated behaviour, authorised decision, emergent effect, or legal fiction. These are different ontologies of the plural.
From aggregate to agent. A useful scale begins with the aggregate: entities grouped by an observer but not coordinated with one another.
“People wearing red” may be a category with no shared awareness.
A crowd shares location and may respond to common signals. A network has patterned relations through which information or influence travels. A swarm achieves distributed coordination through local interactions. An organisation has roles, membership rules, decision procedures, and persistent records. An institution adds normative authority, public recognition, and mechanisms that outlast particular occupants.
These forms can overlap. A corporation is an organisation, a legal person, a network of contracts, a set of buildings and systems, a brand, and a coalition of human interests. A nation is population, territory, historical narrative, legal order, administrative apparatus, imagined community, and external recognition. A scientific field is a network of researchers, journals, methods, prestige relations, and shared objects of inquiry. Treating any of these as a simple individual creates both analytical power and moral hazard.
The philosophy of collective agency asks when a group can literally bear beliefs, intentions, and responsibility. Christian List and Philip Pettit argue that suitably organised groups can qualify as rational agents when they possess decision procedures that integrate member inputs into coherent group-level attitudes (2011). Group judgments can be systematically related to yet not reducible to a simple majority of individual judgments. Raimo Tuomela distinguishes acting in an individual
“I-mode” from a “we-mode” in which participants understand themselves as members committed to a group reason or ethos (2013). More recent work emphasises roles, membership rules, and procedures that explain how collectives persist and exercise causal powers despite turnover.
One need not accept every philosophical criterion to see the linguistic problem. English moves too easily from
“members of the organisation produced an outcome” to “the organisation wanted the outcome.”
The singular verb invites a mind where there may be a procedure, an incentive system, or an emergent pattern.
The many meanings of
“France wants”.
Consider the sentence
“France wants stricter regulation.”
It could report:
Term
Explanation
a policy adopted
a policy adopted through authorised government procedure
the preference of
the preference of the current executive
a parliamentary
a parliamentary majority
a polling
a polling majority among residents
a dominant
a dominant media narrative
a strategic
a strategic position attributed by foreign analysts
an outcome
an outcome produced by institutions without majority support
The sentence may be acceptable shorthand, but it is not ontologically innocent. It creates a unitary subject, suppresses internal dissent, and transfers the phenomenology of individual wanting to a complex polity. Similar problems occur in
“the market believes,”
“science says,”
“the company decided,” and “society rejected.”
A collective-mode marker would specify what licenses the singular attribution:
Term
Explanation
Authorised state decision
A policy was adopted through a recognised governmental procedure.
Survey majority
A measured majority of respondents expressed a preference; this is not automatically a state decision.
Institutional output
An output is attributed to the state through a named institutional procedure.
Analytical attribution
An external analyst attributes a strategic objective under an explicit interpretation.
The marker does not forbid metonymy. It audits it.
Clusivity as a primitive of political honesty. Inclusive and exclusive
“we” is the natural linguistic starting point.
English speakers routinely use we to manufacture solidarity or diffuse responsibility. A leader may say
“we made mistakes” when decisions were concentrated among a few, or “we must adapt” when costs fall on people outside the decision process.
A researcher may write
“we know” to mean “a specialised literature currently supports.”
A platform may say
“we value privacy” while no coherent human subject corresponds to the pronoun.
Natural clusivity distinguishes whether the addressee is included. A modern extension should also distinguish participation, authorisation, and burden:
Term
Explanation
we-including-you
we-including-you as participant
we-speaking-on-your-authority
The speaker claims authority to speak for the addressee or included group; the mandate must be declared.
we-sharing-the-consequences
The participant set is defined by shared exposure to consequences rather than by shared decision authority.
we-excluding-you but acting upon you
The speaker excludes the addressee from the deciding group while including the addressee among those affected by its action.
These distinctions would not make rhetoric honest by force. They would make some evasions syntactically visible. A political sentence that says
“we-exclusive-decider require sacrifices from you-inclusive-affected” cannot borrow the warmth of collective inclusion quite so cheaply.
Agency architecture. The next distinction concerns how collective action is produced. Four architectures recur:
Term
Explanation
Centralised agency
an authorised centre selects and executes a decision. A chief executive signs, a court rules, a commander orders
Distributed coordinated agency
participants follow shared protocols or reciprocal expectations without one complete controller. Open-source projects and federated networks often approximate this form
Emergent outcome
local actions produce a macro-pattern no participant selected. Congestion, bank runs, segregation, and many market dynamics belong here
Statistical attribution
a claim describes a distribution of individual states rather than a collective decision—“consumers prefer,” “voters fear,” “scientists believe.”
A fifth category, legal-person agency, records that law attributes acts, rights, and duties to an entity. Legal agency may track a real decision architecture or may function as a practical fiction that bundles responsibility.
The grammatical problem appears when the same active verb applies to all five.
“The platform discriminated” may identify a centrally chosen policy, an emergent effect of optimisation, a data pattern, or a legal attribution. Responsibility depends partly on which architecture generated the harm.
Distributed responsibility without diluted responsibility. Complex systems create a familiar paradox: many people contribute to an outcome, yet no individual contribution seems sufficient. Ordinary language oscillates between scapegoating one visible actor and declaring that
“the system” is responsible, which can leave nobody accountable.
A better grammar separates at least four relations:
Term
Explanation
causal contribution
the action changed the probability or form of the outcome
control
the actor could alter the process or stop it
knowledge
the actor knew or should have known the relevant risk
role obligation
the actor had a duty to monitor, disclose, or intervene
Responsibility is not identical to any single coordinate. A low-level engineer may make a causal contribution with little control over deployment. An executive may never touch the system yet possess control and role obligation. A regulator may lack direct causation but have failed a duty to act. A user may participate in an emergent pattern without meaningful alternatives.
SLEnglish therefore proposes a responsibility vector, not a single blame marker: Responsibility for an outcome should be represented through separate dimensions of contribution, control, knowledge, duty, benefit, and vulnerability.
Again, this is not intended for every sentence. It is a schema for incident reports, audits, legal reasoning, and institutional design. Its purpose is to prevent distributed causation from becoming distributed invisibility.
The persistence of institutions. Institutions survive member replacement through records, rules, routines, assets, symbols, recognition, and enforcement. Their identity is neither purely material nor merely fictional. A university can persist while its faculty, students, buildings, curriculum, and charter change, but at some point continuity becomes disputed. A company can be acquired, renamed, emptied, and repurposed while remaining legally continuous. A state can undergo revolution while claiming inherited debts and treaties.
Institutional persistence therefore combines the identity distinctions developed earlier in this chapter with an explicit account of collective mode. A robust description should answer: The phrase
Term
Explanation
Which decision
Which decision procedure persists?
Which obligations and
Which obligations and claims are inherited?
Which memories
Which memories are preserved, and by whom?
Which members
Which members recognise the continuity?
Which external
Which external institutions recognise it?
Who benefits from
Who benefits from claiming sameness, and who bears inherited liability?
“the same institution” is often a strategic achievement.
Continuity may be invoked to preserve property and authority, while discontinuity is invoked to evade responsibility. A grammar that marks role-same and line-same separately would expose such selective inheritance.
Swarms, AI teams, and synthetic collectives. Collective ontology becomes more urgent as artificial agents participate in organisations. A multi-agent system can divide tasks, critique outputs, maintain shared state, and select actions. Is it one agent, a team, or an orchestration layer? If an AI system acts through dozens of transient model calls, retrieved tools, and human approvals, where does agency reside?
The wrong answer is to copy human singulars. Calling the system
“an AI” may hide the architecture; calling every component an independent agent may exaggerate autonomy. The relevant unit depends on memory, coordination, boundary, goal structure, and control. A collective-mode grammar could mark:
Term
Explanation
Central orchestration
One control centre coordinates the system and can be treated as the principal unit of agency.
Distributed consensus
Agency is distributed across components governed by a consensus mechanism.
Human-authorised chain
Several AI components act through a chain whose authority remains explicitly human.
Emergent coordination
System-level behaviour emerges without one controlling component.
Such markers would improve not only philosophy but engineering. Failures could be assigned to coordination protocols, shared knowledge, role definitions, or control points rather than attributed vaguely to
“the model.”
Collective identity as a measurable profile. The earlier brainstorming proposed a scale based on coordination, memory, boundary, goals, decision, and persistence. It is worth retaining in a refined form. For a collective G: The relevant profile of a collective includes its boundary, coordination, memory, integration of goals, decision procedure, persistence, and accountability.
The profile does not generate a metaphysical threshold above which a group becomes
“really” an agent.
It supports comparison. A flash mob has coordination but little memory or persistence. A market has persistence and distributed information but weak integrated intention. A corporation has boundaries, records, decisions, and legal accountability, though its goals may be internally contested. A nation has extraordinary persistence and symbolic memory but highly plural agency.
This profile could be used experimentally. Participants could receive descriptions of groups and assign mental-state verbs. One group would use ordinary English; another would use collective-mode tags. Researchers could test whether the tags reduce over-attribution of unified beliefs, improve responsibility allocation, or make internal dissent more salient.
The ultimate purpose is not to ban sentences such as
“the country decided.”
It is to make singular collective language answerable to the architecture underneath. Many can become one—but not always in the same way.
A child knocks over a glass. A gust knocks it over. A poorly designed table makes it unstable. A manager ignored repeated warnings. A procurement rule favoured the cheapest surface. An optimisation system rewarded speed over safety. Which of these caused the spill?
Natural language answers by selecting a subject and a verb.
“The child broke the glass.”
“The wind broke the glass.”
“The design caused the accident.”
Grammar packages a causal field into an event with an agent, an action, and a patient. This compression is indispensable for action and memory. It is also one of the main ways language assigns blame.
Cross-linguistic work suggests that preferred descriptions of accidental events can influence memory for agents. English speakers often use agentive constructions even for accidents—
“She broke the vase” —where Spanish or Japanese speakers may prefer less agentive forms roughly corresponding to
“The vase broke.”
In experiments, linguistic framing affected memory for who was involved, and habitual language differences correlated with agent memory for accidental but not intentional events (Fausey et al. 2010; Fausey and Boroditsky 2011). These studies concern specific tasks and languages, not a universal moral psychology. They nevertheless show that syntax can guide attention toward or away from an agent.
Cause is a role in an explanation. Philosophy offers many accounts of causation: regularity, counterfactual dependence, intervention, mechanism, probability raising, process transmission, and more. Ordinary speech uses cause pragmatically. We select the factor that is abnormal, controllable, morally relevant, or useful for intervention. Oxygen is causally necessary for a fire but is rarely named the cause; a spark is selected because it differentiates this fire from normal conditions.
Complex systems defeat this habit because no single factor plays every causal role. A harmful algorithmic decision may involve:
Term
Explanation
Trigger
the immediate input or threshold crossing selected as the proximate cause
Enabling condition
infrastructure or background conditions that made the event possible
Mechanism
the process that translates inputs or conditions into the observed outcome
Structural cause
an incentive, policy, or distribution that repeatedly produces the relevant risk
Maintenance cause
the factor that keeps a harmful regime stable after its initial emergence
Selection cause
the reason this design, institution, or behaviour persisted among alternatives
Term
Explanation
an absence cause
a safeguard that was not implemented
English can list these, but the unmarked sentence “the algorithm caused it” collapses the chain around the most visible technical component. The result is often a form of causal laundering: organisational choices become properties of software, while software’s real causal contribution is either mystified or denied.
A grammar of causal depth. SLEnglish can introduce a causal-layer marker:
Term
Explanation
Trigger cause
the immediate event or threshold crossing that initiates the sequence
Enabling cause
the condition or infrastructure without which the outcome would not occur
Mechanistic cause
the process that carries the influence from source to outcome
Structural cause
the recurrent arrangement of incentives, rules, or resources that generates the risk
Maintenance cause
the factor that keeps a regime or process stable after it begins
Omission cause
the absent action whose performance was expected under a stated duty or counterfactual
A complete incident account need not identify only one. “The sensor error triggered the denial; the scoring mechanism propagated it; the procurement policy structurally favoured a system without review; the absent appeal path maintained the harm.” This language does not eliminate disagreement about causation. It prevents disagreement about different causal roles from masquerading as disagreement about one binary relation.
The marker also improves scientific explanation. A gene may be part of a mechanism, a developmental condition, or a population-level difference maker. A social variable may predict an outcome without identifying an intervention target. A model feature may be statistically important without carrying the causal pathway. Scientific prose often moves silently among association, prediction, explanation, and intervention.
A minimal claim should therefore distinguish:
Term
Explanation
associated-with
Records an association without asserting prediction, mechanism, or intervention effect.
predicts-under-model
Records predictive performance under a named model and evaluation context.
counterfactually-relevant
States that changing the factor changes the outcome under a declared counterfactual model.
mechanistically-linked
Records a specified mechanism connecting the factor to the outcome.
and intervention-supported
Explicitly records the state and intervention-supported.
These are not synonyms. A language that treats them as separate verbs would remove a large amount of manufactured certainty from research communication.
Intention is not one switch. English often contrasts intentional and accidental action. Real responsibility occupies a richer space. An outcome may be:
Term
Explanation
directly intended
The outcome was adopted as an explicit goal of the action.
used
used as a means to another goal
foreseen and
foreseen and accepted
recognised
recognised as a risk but discounted
foreseeable
foreseeable under a role obligation but not actually considered
genuinely unforeseeable
The outcome was not reasonably predictable under the actor's information, role, and available methods.
produced while
produced while the actor lacked control or awareness
contrary to intention
contrary to intention but recurrently generated by the actor’s system
Legal traditions distinguish forms such as purpose, knowledge, recklessness, and negligence, but ordinary public language repeatedly collapses them. “They did not intend the harm” is used as though it answered whether they foresaw it, benefited from it, ignored warnings, or designed conditions that made it likely.
An intent–effect marker could encode the relation between an actor’s plan and an outcome:
Term
Explanation
Intended effect
The outcome belongs to the agent’s stated goal.
Instrumental effect
The outcome is accepted as a means to another goal.
Foreseen and accepted
The outcome is anticipated and accepted without being selected as the principal goal.
Known risk
The outcome is recognised as possible but is not selected as a goal.
Role-foreseeable effect
A competent holder of the relevant role should have anticipated the outcome.
Unforeseeable effect
The outcome falls outside the actor’s declared reasonable predictive capacity.
Systemic effect
The system repeatedly produces the outcome despite the stated intentions of local participants.
The final category matters because institutions can repeatedly produce outcomes they officially oppose. Intention at the level of mission statements may diverge from incentive-compatible behaviour. A company can sincerely contain many people who do not intend discrimination while maintaining a system that predictably generates it. Grammar should not allow individual innocence to settle institutional responsibility.
Responsibility as control over alternatives. Responsibility becomes clearer when linked to available alternatives. Did the actor have the ability, authority, information, time, and resources to choose differently? Was refusal realistically possible? Could the harm be mitigated later? Did the actor create the constraint under which later actors operated?
This leads to counterfactual reach: the set of relevant outcomes an actor could have changed. A senior manager may have wide reach but little direct contact with the event. A frontline worker may have immediate contact but narrow reach. A software developer may change code but not deployment policy. A state may regulate a firm but not foresee a particular failure.
A responsibility language should record both actual contribution and counterfactual reach. Otherwise those near the event are blamed because they are visible, while those who designed the choice architecture remain grammatically distant.
Reversibility changes the moral tense. Actions differ not only in expected benefit and risk but in whether their consequences can be reversed. A reversible experiment permits learning. An irreversible intervention converts uncertainty into permanent exposure. English commonly says “we can change course later” without marking the time window, recovery cost, or whether some losses cannot be restored.
A reversibility grade should distinguish:
Term
Explanation
immediately reversible
immediately reversible at low cost
reversible
