A later epoch can reuse a cached result only when the operator contracts and invalidation rules justify reuse. Full replay remains the reference for validating incremental execution. Historical results retain the environment and assumptions under which they were produced.
A backend must be able to stop with a structured status. Unsupported operator, unresolved type, incomplete coverage, ambiguous grounding, unavailable engine, resource exhaustion, policy denial, inconsistent assumptions, and failed certificate are distinct outcomes. Returning unknown with a reason is preferable to asking an LLM to improvise across an unsupported gap. The result envelope can distinguish entailed, refuted, possible, necessary, conflicted, unsupported, bounded, timed out, and unknown without pretending that all statuses arise from the same logic.
Approximate retrieval and high-dimensional representations may assist operator selection or source discovery, but they do not define accepted consequence. Similarity can nominate a candidate path, related passage, or possible analogy. The candidate returns to the typed and source-grounded pipeline. If lexical search, graph indexes, embeddings, vector-symbolic methods, or a language model are used, the system should compare recall, semantic precision, latency, memory, provenance quality, and downstream false assurance. The language itself remains independent of the winning retrieval method.
The engine router should be driven primarily by declared contracts. An LLM can propose a plan, but a deterministic registry should check that every operator supports the selected lowering and that the requested guarantee is available. The router records why a backend was selected, what fragments were not handled, what conversions occurred, and which certificates or test results were returned. An unsupported request remains an explicit obligation rather than being silently delegated to free-form generation.
Scientific work combines exact proof, statistical estimation, simulation, measurement, causal modelling, database retrieval, interpretation, and institutional judgement. SLEnglish represents their common semantic dependencies while preserving the boundaries among their execution semantics. A result therefore records the operator contracts, conversions, assumptions, evidence, and backend receipts that license it.
The same representation supports several levels of execution. It can serve as a typed annotation format, semantic diff layer, compilation target for domain tools, query-oriented research object, or interchange representation between LLMs and existing systems. Where a general runtime is useful, the dependency graph can coordinate multiple interpreters. This makes runtime choice an empirical engineering variable rather than a property encoded into the source grammar.
The backend-agnostic position is therefore an empirical commitment to modularity. It can be falsified. If most operators require backend-specific surface syntax, a common source may become an unhelpful abstraction. If adapters introduce unacceptable semantic loss, direct authoring in established formalisms may be better. If the dependency structure adds little to correction or reuse, a simpler frame representation may suffice. The programme should preserve the evidence required to reach any of these conclusions.
Interpreter family
Appropriate SLEnglish contribution and required caution
Datalog or deductive database
Supports typed facts, recursive closure, and explainable rules; requires explicit treatment of negation, stratification, and closure.
Answer Set Programming
Supports defaults, exceptions, competing models, and optimisation; result semantics and priorities must be named.
RDF, SPARQL, and graph systems
Support graph interchange, federation, and query; n-ary events, context, and profile assumptions require explicit modelling.
Description logic or OWL
Supports class and property reasoning within decidable profiles; it should receive only compatible fragments.
SMT, CSP, SAT, and optimisation
Support arithmetic, finite-domain, scheduling, and constraint obligations; units, bounds, and encoding correctness must be recorded.
Temporal engine or model checker
Supports event ordering, persistence, state transitions, and temporal properties; the chosen time and transition semantics must be explicit.
Theorem prover or proof assistant
Supports deductive proof under formal definitions; fidelity of the formalised statement remains a separate obligation.
Generated conventional code
Supports procedures, simulations, tests, and integrations; environment, effects, versions, and semantic tests remain part of the record.
Dynamic SOP Lang circuit runtime
May exploit typed dependencies, bounded execution, caching, and provenance; it is an optional substrate described in a separate research programme.
Human or institutional interpreter
Remains necessary for contested meaning, legitimacy, ethical authority, and domain judgement; the role and decision must be recorded rather than hidden.
CHAPTER 7
Executable Science as a Semantic Build System
From Reproducible Computation to an Executable Argument
Executable science is the broader application that motivates the language, but the phrase must remain narrower than the claim that science can be reduced to software. Scientific work includes concept formation, measurement, modelling, interpretation, criticism, ethical judgement, institutional authority, and revision. The proposed change is that a larger share of the operational and evidential structure should become inspectable and queryable, while unresolved judgement remains explicit rather than being simulated as deterministic output.
The semantic build-system analogy is useful because scientific claims depend on artefacts and assumptions in ways that ordinary documents record only partially. A build system knows which outputs depend on which inputs and what must be recomputed after change. An executable research object should similarly know which conclusions depend on a dataset revision, a calibration, an identity rule, a causal interpretation, a software environment, a reported source, or a reviewer-approved exception. The analogy does not imply that every dependency is mechanical; it requires each dependency to state its kind.
Executable science is often reduced to reproducible code. Reproducible computation is indispensable, but it does not make a scientific argument executable. A notebook can rerun a regression while leaving unclear which sentence the regression supports, which assumptions are required, which uncertainty is statistical rather than structural, which dataset revision was used, and which claims depend on a contested interpretation of identity or causation. The gap is not execution of the calculation; it is execution of the argument’s semantic dependencies.
A scientific project can be treated as a semantic build system. Concepts, hypotheses, protocols, instruments, observations, datasets, transformations, models, simulations, results, claims, critiques, and conclusions become typed nodes connected to source material and executable artefacts. A build resolves definitions, checks units and scopes, validates protocol obligations, executes analyses, derives eligible claims, reports contradictions, and materialises proof and provenance graphs. The prose remains a human view over that build rather than being replaced by it.
SLEnglish files do not duplicate datasets, code, or workflow specifications. They state the relations among them. A measurement node identifies the variable, entity, unit, uncertainty, instrument, calibration, source field, and time. An analysis node identifies code, environment, inputs, method, parameters, and output. A claim node states which results support it, under which assumptions, population, temporal scope, identity criteria, and causal mode. A review node can challenge one premise without rewriting the original claim.
Claims become build targets. A conclusion depends on observations, statistical analyses, simulations, background theory, methodological assumptions, and interpretive choices. Some dependencies are deductive; some probabilistic; some defeasible; some institutional. The objective is not to force them into one proof calculus. It is to type the dependency and preserve the provenance so that changes propagate appropriately.
When a dataset is corrected, a software dependency changes, a source is retracted, or an ontology is revised, the build graph identifies affected downstream claims. Some conclusions may remain textually identical while their support weakens. Others may need recomputation. Superseded results remain in prior epochs. This is the semantic analogue of incremental compilation and gives scientific revision a more precise memory.
Research objects, FAIR workflows, notebooks, RO-Crate, provenance standards, containers, and workflow languages already solve important parts of this problem. They package data, code, environments, runs, and metadata. SLEnglish targets the missing semantic layer of the prose claim and its relation to those artefacts. It should interoperate with mature tooling rather than replace it.
Executable science should not be confused with fully automated science. A machine may establish that a conclusion follows from declared assumptions while experts dispute the assumptions, measurement validity, causal interpretation, or social relevance. Formalisation localises that disagreement; it does not eliminate judgement. The system’s success lies partly in making disputes addressable at the node where they arise.
The strongest near-term use cases are bounded: verifying that reported numbers match outputs; checking unit and population consistency; linking claims to exact source spans and analysis runs; exposing unsupported causal language; tracking retractions; validating protocol conditions; comparing theory versions; and asking structured questions across a research package. These tasks can demonstrate value without claiming machine understanding of all science.
A paper in this architecture has two aligned surfaces. The first is ordinary prose, which preserves rhetoric, explanation, uncertainty, and disciplinary style. The second is an executable semantic package containing the claims selected for formal treatment. Alignment may be paragraph-level, sentence-level, or span-level depending on importance. Readers can stay in prose until they need to inspect evidence, query a claim, compare versions, or reproduce a derivation.
The package begins with a concept layer. Terms are defined, mapped to external vocabularies where useful, and versioned. Identity criteria are explicit for samples, organisms, datasets, models, software versions, institutions, and populations. Units, scales, temporal frames, and missing-value semantics are declared. Competing definitions can coexist in namespaces rather than being collapsed into a supposedly neutral ontology.
The hypothesis layer distinguishes conjecture, operational hypothesis, statistical hypothesis, mechanistic model, and prediction. A hypothesis specifies conditions under which it would be challenged, but executable science should not reduce scientific status to one falsification operator. Replication, calibration, exploratory analysis, model comparison, severe testing, Bayesian updating, and qualitative evidence require different profiles. The semantic package makes the test relation visible rather than imposing one philosophy of science.
The protocol layer connects planned actions to actual execution. Preconditions, interventions, measurement schedules, randomisation, blinding, inclusion criteria, exclusions, stopping rules, and deviations can be represented. External workflow engines and laboratory systems remain operational authorities. SLEnglish records the semantic contract and imports receipts. A protocol query can identify which conclusions rely on a deviation or unavailable measurement.
The result layer distinguishes raw observation, processed measurement, statistical estimate, model output, simulation result, and derived quantity. Probability is separated from confidence in the estimate. Statistical uncertainty is separated from structural, measurement, and model uncertainty. Bounds and units are mandatory under scientific profiles. A result without source and method remains a proposition but cannot be promoted to an empirical claim.
The claim layer records modality and scope. An association is not a causal effect. A population average is not an individual prediction. A result inside a simulation is not an observation in the actual world. A bounded verification is not an unbounded theorem. A claim states which transformation licensed it and which assumptions remain external. The renderer uses these distinctions to prevent familiar overstatements.
The discussion layer can represent competing interpretations rather than only the authors’ preferred narrative. A reviewer may accept data and analysis while rejecting an identity criterion or causal layer. An alternative model can share evidence but derive different implications. May/must analysis can show which conclusions are robust across the admissible interpretations. The paper becomes capable of exposing its own hinge assumptions.
The publication artefact records a build lock: source digests, library versions, compiler, model profile, engine versions, environment, policies, and reviewer decisions. This does not guarantee that the science is correct. It makes the machine-mediated transformations inspectable and the computational parts replayable. The reproducible unit becomes the argument package, not only the notebook.
SLEnglish contributes a human-facing representation for this semantic layer. Existing workflow and research-object standards remain responsible for packaging files, data, code, environments, and runs. Domain repositories remain responsible for authoritative records. Formal systems remain responsible for their own proofs or certificates. SLEnglish links these artefacts to propositions, contexts, evidence, interpretations, and queries so that the relation between a calculation and a published claim does not remain an informal caption.
The build graph should distinguish normative from descriptive dependencies. A protocol may require an action without establishing that it occurred. A dataset may contain an observation without licensing a causal conclusion. A reviewer may authorise publication without endorsing every interpretation. A legal permission may allow data use without establishing scientific validity. The common representation is valuable only if these different relations remain typed.
Scientific change is then represented as revision rather than overwrite. A corrected dataset, altered calibration, retracted source, changed code dependency, or new identity criterion creates a new epoch. The system identifies potentially affected claims, but it does not declare all of them false. Some require recomputation, some require reinterpretation, some retain support through independent evidence, and some become unsupported. This distinction is more faithful than treating a scientific article as either permanently valid or globally retracted.
The minimal useful outcome is not an autonomous research system. It is a document whose selected claims can answer bounded questions about source, scope, support, contradiction, dependence, and change. A reader should be able to ask which measurements support a conclusion, which assumptions are external, what would change under a competing identity model, which claims rely on a retracted source, and which parts of the argument cannot yet be represented.
Research object
Semantic build obligation
Concept or definition
Declare version, scope, competing definitions, mappings, and the claims whose meaning depends on the choice.
Observation or measurement
Record target, time, instrument, unit, uncertainty, method, source field, and applicable identity criterion.
Dataset
Record revision, schema, coverage, exclusions, provenance, licence, consent scope, and relation to source observations.
Code or model
Record version, environment, inputs, parameters, assumptions, outputs, tests, and generated or human-authored status.
Analysis
Connect method, data, code, environment, statistical or formal profile, result, and known limitations.
Scientific claim
State modality, population, scale, temporal scope, causal status, evidence, assumptions, and support relation.
Review or challenge
Target a specific node or dependency and record evidence, authority, requested change, response, and unresolved status.
Publication view
Render only licensed claims while retaining links to the maintained semantic package and its locked versions.
Revision
Create successor nodes, preserve history, calculate affected dependencies, and rebuild only where the contracts permit.
Review, Governance, and Bounded Scientific Use
Peer review becomes more precise when criticism can target semantic nodes and dependencies rather than only paragraphs. A reviewer can accept a measurement but reject its unit conversion, accept an association but reject a causal operator, challenge the identity rule used to group samples, mark two sources as non-independent, or request a sensitivity analysis. The review itself becomes a versioned semantic artefact linked to the claim it contests.
Automated review agents can perform bounded checks: verify citations and spans, compare reported values with analysis outputs, detect missing units, identify unsupported modality changes, inspect temporal consistency, test rules, search for contradictory claims, and generate counterexamples under declared models. They should not return a universal quality score. Each finding carries evidence, profile, coverage, and verifier status. Unknown remains acceptable when the required semantic layer has not been materialised.
Rebuttal can also become executable. Authors respond by linking a correction, alternative interpretation, new analysis, or reasoned rejection to the review node. The build shows which downstream claims changed. Disagreement can persist without being erased. A journal or consortium can declare which unresolved issues block publication and which remain visible after publication.
Post-publication correction is a central advantage. Retractions and dataset updates can invalidate dependencies automatically. A literature graph can identify claims downstream of a retracted source without assuming that every dependent claim is false. The status is affected and requires review. Replacement evidence can restore support in a new epoch. This is more faithful than both permanent citation and indiscriminate deletion.
Research compliance benefits from the same structure. Privacy constraints, consent scopes, copyright licences, data-use agreements, jurisdictional rules, and funder policies can be represented as versioned modules and checked against project nodes. The compliance engine remains distinct from scientific truth. A result can be scientifically credible yet legally unusable, or compliant yet scientifically weak. The language must preserve those axes.
The social risk is bureaucratic capture. Once claims become machine-checkable, institutions may reward what is easy to formalise and penalise work whose value depends on new concepts, qualitative interpretation, or productive ambiguity. Metrics may become targets. A SLEnglish profile must therefore expose Goodhart pressure, coverage, and scope rather than presenting formal completeness as epistemic superiority. Executability should expand criticism, not narrow science to what current operators can express.
Another risk is authority laundering. A formal vocabulary can make contested classifications appear natural. A library may encode one legal doctrine, medical taxonomy, or social ontology and then describe outputs as objective. Namespaces, versioning, provenance, governance, and bridge modules are safeguards, but they do not replace political scrutiny. The source of a semantic operator and the community authorised to maintain it should be visible.
The system should support literature and education as well as verification. Controlled smoothing, perspective marking, and deliberate omission can be legitimate in diplomacy, pedagogy, and art. The objective is not to purge ambiguity. It is to detect and govern when ambiguity is being used, and to require stricter profiles where consequences demand them. A language that treats every unformalised sentence as defective would misunderstand both human communication and science.
A credible pilot must be smaller than the vision. It should select one research package with bounded data, a limited ontology, a tractable set of claims, and several genuinely different reasoning needs. Suitable candidates include a computational experiment with a published dataset, a privacy-preserving learning protocol, a systematic review with explicit inclusion criteria, or a formalised software-security study. The package should contain prose, data, code, claims, and at least one contested interpretation.
The pilot begins with manual gold work rather than end-to-end automation. Domain experts define a reference SLEnglish package, identify source spans, declare key ambiguities, and specify expected query outcomes. This reference is not assumed metaphysically complete. It is an evaluation instrument. Independent annotators record disagreements so that the benchmark contains legitimate alternatives rather than forcing one hidden interpretation.
The first experiment tests compiler fidelity. LLM, encoder, hybrid, and direct-authoring paths produce candidate packages under the same libraries. Metrics include syntax validity, verb and parameter accuracy, source alignment, assumption invention, ambiguity preservation, correction time, and semantic equivalence on queries. Token count is secondary. A verbose representation is justified only if it reduces material error and review effort.
The second experiment tests execution and update. Queries cover provenance, identity, time, units, causality, and rebuild impact. The system executes through relational, constraint, temporal, and proof profiles where needed. A dataset field and a library definition are then changed. Incremental results are compared with full replay. Every changed claim should be explainable through the dependency graph.
The third experiment compares representation stacks. A competent team implements the same package using RDF/OWL/SHACL/SPARQL/PROV and conventional workflow metadata. Another uses a frame-oriented CNL or structured JSON. Direct LLM reasoning and tool-augmented prompting form additional baselines. The comparison evaluates authoring effort, number of artefacts, semantic locality, interoperability, query accuracy, update trace, false assurance, and expert preference.
The fourth experiment tests the missing-grammar hypothesis. Participants solve source-monitoring, collective-agency, process-identity, causal-depth, or reversibility tasks using ordinary English views or SLEnglish-informed views. The objective is not to prove that a language globally improves thought. It is to measure whether selected explicit operators reduce predictable errors enough to justify their verbosity. Negative results should remove or demote profiles.
The fifth experiment tests governance. A red team introduces malicious source instructions, stale world knowledge, incompatible modules, ontology poisoning, ambiguous licences, and effectful requests. The system must preserve source as data, reject unauthorised effects, pin versions, expose conflicts, and produce stopped states. Security and semantic accuracy are reported separately.
Success is bounded. A pilot succeeds when experts can inspect and correct translations, queries execute with traceable evidence, changes rebuild the correct claims, competing interpretations remain visible, and the package outperforms at least one simpler baseline on a target metric without unacceptable cost. It does not establish general executable language or autonomous science. It establishes that a more ambitious experiment is justified.

Explanation
Research objects
FAIR workflows, RO-Crate, notebooks, containers, and provenance records package computation; SLEnglish targets the semantic dependencies of prose claims (Soiland-Reyes et al. 2022; Wilkinson et al. 2025).
Executable review
Claims, objections, rebuttals, corrections, and retractions become source-linked artefacts whose downstream effects can be rebuilt.
Governance risk
Formalisation can amplify bureaucratic preference, Goodhart pressure, and authority laundering; profiles must expose scope and contested definitions.
Bounded scope
The near-term target is semantic audit and executable dependency, not autonomous judgement or complete formalisation of science.
The social risk is not secondary. Formalisation can convert a contestable category into infrastructure. A profile for risk, quality, novelty, eligibility, or responsibility may appear technical while encoding institutional interests. SLEnglish should therefore represent definition provenance, affected parties, authority, appeal, dissent, and revision. A transparent ontology can still be unjust, but opacity makes contestation harder.
Domain sensitivity is essential. Formal mathematics can use proof kernels once the theorem is correctly formalised. Software can use tests and static analysis while remaining vulnerable to specification error. Laboratory science depends on calibration and contact with measured reality. Clinical and social research involve consent, distributional effects, confounding, and long validation latency. Historical and interpretive disciplines require source criticism and plural argument rather than one universal execution metric. The common substrate is evidence and dependency, not a uniform standard of truth.
Governance must separate capability from authority. A runtime may be able to execute code, query a database, or recommend a laboratory action without being authorised to do so. A model may identify a likely error without having the standing to retract a claim. A reviewer may request a change without controlling the underlying data. SLEnglish can represent these boundaries, while institutional processes determine who may accept, reject, publish, or act.
The practical adoption path should begin with narrow checks that have clear evaluators: claim-to-output consistency, citation-span verification, unit and population checks, protocol conformance, retraction propagation, source provenance, and semantic diff. Broader causal or interpretive reasoning can be added only where the representation and evaluation are adequate. The language should expand through demonstrated correction rather than through catalogue growth alone.
A successful executable-science package will remain readable as ordinary scholarship. The formal companion should not force all readers into the technical layer or turn the paper into a generated report. Its role is to expose what prose cannot maintain reliably at scale: exact dependencies, versions, evidence paths, alternative interpretations, and bounded machine actions.

CHAPTER 8
SLEnglish in Relation to Ontologies and Formal Knowledge Representation
What Existing Standards and Formalisms Already Solve
The comparison belongs after the language proposal because otherwise SLEnglish is easily defined only by opposition. RDF, OWL, SHACL, SPARQL, PROV, Common Logic, controlled natural languages, Datalog, Answer Set Programming, constraint solvers, temporal formalisms, and workflow standards each solve substantial problems. The research question is not whether they can represent knowledge. It is whether an additional source-level language improves the passage from natural language to auditable, heterogeneous execution.
RDF provides a general graph data model and a mature ecosystem for identifiers, linked data, federation, and interchange. OWL provides description-logic semantics for classes and properties under defined profiles. SHACL validates graph shapes. SPARQL queries RDF datasets. PROV supplies a standard vocabulary for entities, activities, agents, derivation, attribution, and generation. Common Logic provides a family of first-order representations. These capabilities are baselines and targets for interoperability, not obsolete technologies to dismiss.
Controlled natural languages address a different boundary. Attempto Controlled English, Common Logic Controlled English, PENG, and related systems show that readable restricted language can map deterministically to formal structures. Their trade-offs among precision, expressiveness, naturalness, and simplicity have been studied systematically (Kuhn 2014). SLEnglish differs in degree and architecture: it sacrifices more surface naturalness, assumes LLM-assisted translation, exposes SSA identity, and uses open path-addressed operator contracts. Those differences require evidence, not rhetorical claims of novelty.
A new representation language should begin by acknowledging what existing formalisms already do well. Controlled Natural Languages provide readable entry points with explicit interpretation rules. Formal semantics offers principled accounts of quantification, discourse, events, modality, and reference. RDF provides a simple graph interchange model; RDFS and OWL support ontology semantics; SHACL validates graph shapes; SPARQL queries graphs; PROV-O represents entities, activities, agents, and derivations. Common Logic provides a first-order interchange framework. Datalog, Answer Set Programming, temporal calculi, SAT, SMT, CSP, theorem provers, and workflow systems each supply mature execution semantics for particular classes of problems.
The relevant comparison is therefore not expressive possibility in the abstract. Almost any finite structure can be encoded in JSON, RDF, relational tables, or a general programming language. The questions are engineering questions: Which representation is easiest for an LLM to generate correctly? Which keeps an n-ary event, its roles, and its provenance locally inspectable? Which permits stable semantic diffs? Which exposes a dependency graph without a separate reconstruction step? Which makes it cheap to route one claim to OWL and another to SMT while retaining common identity and source links? Which failures are visible to a reviewer rather than buried in adapters?
Traditional CNLs often preserve a substantial amount of English surface grammar. That can improve naturalness and direct authoring, but it requires a grammar sophisticated enough to regulate articles, agreement, relative clauses, quantifier scope, anaphora, and permitted verb frames. A representation that is designed primarily as an LLM compilation target can make a different trade. It can abandon subject–verb–object as its computational substrate, expose roles by name, and tolerate a more visibly formal surface. Human users review the semantics rather than pretending to write ordinary prose without ambiguity.
Formal semantic representations such as Discourse Representation Structures are richer than flat triples and can preserve discourse referents, negation, quantification, and anaphoric structure. Their breadth is valuable for interpretation research. They are not automatically ideal as a stable authoring and execution language for scientific packages. Production systems still need source identities, package versioning, module imports, operator contracts, engine routing, coverage, incremental invalidation, effect policies, and proof receipts. SLEnglish treats these engineering obligations as part of the language environment rather than as downstream infrastructure.
The most important principle inherited from the earlier survey is that the semantic pivot should not pretend to be the final logic. It should preserve identities, roles, propositions, contexts, evidence, alternatives, and dependency information sufficiently well that specialised lowerings remain possible. Some questions can be answered relationally over the pivot. Others require a temporal formula, a constraint system, a probabilistic model, a transition system, or a proof assistant. The common language coordinates these methods; it does not erase their differences.
This portfolio approach creates its own obligations. Every lowering must declare which source distinctions it preserves, approximates, rejects, or ignores. A proof in the target formalism is useful only when linked to the compilation assumptions. A contradiction produced by one engine must not be silently combined with a default conclusion from another profile. Certificates require a common envelope without forcing a common proof calculus. SLEnglish profiles are intended to make these boundaries explicit and testable.
The research claim is narrow enough to falsify. SLEnglish should be retained only if its uniform source form reduces generation errors, adapter complexity, review time, and provenance loss on target workloads. If a conventional CNL yields better fidelity and correction cost, the surface should become more natural. If RDF plus SHACL and a rule engine produces equivalent packages with less complexity, SLEnglish may remain only a source syntax or be abandoned. A serious alternative earns its place by measurable workflow properties, not by novelty of notation.
The Semantic Web stack is the strongest baseline because it separates concerns deliberately. RDF offers Web-scale identifiers and a uniform graph model. OWL provides description-logic-based ontology profiles with well-understood entailment. SHACL defines validation over RDF graphs. SPARQL provides query and update. PROV-O supplies reusable provenance concepts. JSON-LD connects graph data to familiar serialisation. These are not failed technologies. They solve interoperability problems that any new system would be foolish to ignore.
The mismatch arises from the target workflow. A scientific claim may involve an n-ary event, source attribution, evidence mode, confidence, probability, temporal validity, measurement uncertainty, a causal interpretation, a validation constraint, a derivation rule, a query, and an execution trace. In an RDF-centred implementation these concerns are normally distributed across event nodes, reification or triple terms, named graphs, shapes, ontology axioms, provenance vocabularies, rule engines, query languages, and workflow systems. The architecture is modular, but each boundary introduces identifiers, mappings, serialisations, version rules, and ownership questions.
An LLM compiler must then generate a coordinated artefact across several languages. A human reviewer must reconstruct one semantic unit from dispersed triples and metadata. A runtime must decide which layer owns negation, closure, defaults, time, conflict, and explanation. SLEnglish proposes a syntactically uniform source in which a semantic operator and all its named roles remain local to one node. It can still compile to RDF and related standards; the difference is authoring locality and circuit identity, not a claim that triples lack expressive power.
N-ary events illustrate the point. A transfer may include agent, theme, recipient, origin, destination, instrument, time, purpose, and resulting ownership. RDF represents this correctly by reifying the event as a resource and attaching role triples. SLEnglish does essentially the same semantically, but keeps the roles in one canonical node whose SSA identity already functions as a graph vertex and cache key. A reviewer can see the event as one unit. A schema can validate required roles. A circuit sees typed input ports without another graph-construction pass.
The open-world assumption of OWL is invaluable for incomplete distributed knowledge. It is not a universal policy. A scientific dataset may be closed for a declared table and revision. A compliance rule may require defaults and exceptions. A planning query may need negation as failure. A contradiction-tolerant review may need paraconsistent status. A numeric claim may require SMT rather than description-logic reasoning. The SLEnglish source must therefore name its profile and coverage rather than allowing one backend’s assumptions to become ambient.
Interoperability remains a core requirement. A class hierarchy should be exportable to OWL when it falls inside an OWL profile. Graph-shaped facts should be exportable to RDF. Validation constraints should lower to SHACL where semantics align. Provenance should map to PROV-O. Queries may compile to SPARQL, Datalog, SQL, or another declared interpreter. The compiler must document semantic loss in both directions. The project fails if it reinvents mature standards while offering no measurable advantage at the compilation and execution boundary.
The fairest experiment is package-level. Given the same natural-language research material, one team builds an RDF/OWL/SHACL/SPARQL/PROV package and another builds a SLEnglish package with equivalent lowerings. Metrics include compiler validity, human correction time, artefact count, semantic locality, source alignment, query accuracy, update impact, proof trace quality, interoperability, and false assurance. The outcome may support a hybrid in which SLEnglish is an authoring and circuit language over standard interchange formats. That would still be a successful result.
Logic-programming and solver traditions provide the operational semantics that SLEnglish itself does not attempt to replace. Datalog supports recursive, explainable closure over relations. ASP supports defaults, exceptions, alternative stable models, and optimisation. SAT, SMT, and CSP systems support bounded logical and arithmetic constraints. Temporal logics, event calculi, automata, and model checking address change and behaviour. Theorem provers address deductive proof. The relevant question is how reliably a SLEnglish fragment can be lowered to each formalism while preserving source alignment and reporting unsupported content.
Graph and ontology standards also provide governance lessons. Stable identifiers, explicit vocabularies, profile restrictions, validation languages, and federated publication are mature practices. A SLEnglish implementation should reuse them where possible. A path-addressed operator may export an RDF identity; a type hierarchy may compile to OWL; constraints may compile to SHACL; provenance may map to PROV; graph queries may compile to SPARQL. The adapter must state whether the mapping is exact, partial, approximate, or one-way.
The source-level proposal is justified only where it improves semantic locality. An n-ary event encoded as several triples is not intrinsically defective, but the author or reviewer may find it easier to inspect one canonical statement containing named roles. A path-based contract can keep schema, tests, rendering, and lowering near the operator definition. SSA identities can support diffing and dependency analysis before graph expansion. These are engineering hypotheses about authoring and maintenance, not claims that graph representation is incapable of expressing the content.
Existing approach
Capability that SLEnglish should reuse or match
RDF and linked data
Stable identifiers, graph interchange, federation, established serialisations, and a broad tooling ecosystem.
OWL and description logics
Formal class and property semantics with decidable profiles and mature reasoners.
SHACL
Declarative validation of graph shapes, cardinalities, and constraints.
SPARQL
Standard graph query, federation, filtering, aggregation, and update mechanisms.
PROV
Interoperable provenance for entities, activities, agents, use, generation, derivation, and attribution.
Common Logic
A standard framework for exchange among first-order logic dialects.
Controlled natural languages
Deterministic mappings from constrained readable input to formal semantics.
Datalog and ASP
Rule evaluation, recursion, defaults, exceptions, model generation, and explainability.
SMT, CSP, SAT, and model checking
Efficient decision procedures, witnesses, unsatisfiable cores, and bounded verification.
Workflow and research-object standards
Execution metadata, packaging, environments, artefact lineage, and reproducibility.
The Additional Hypothesis and the Conditions under Which It Should Be Rejected
Abstract interpretation and symbolic execution occupy different places in the architecture. Abstract
interpretation was developed to compute safe approximations of program behaviour over domains too large or infinite to enumerate. It replaces concrete states with abstract properties and propagates them through sound transfer functions until a fixpoint or frontier is reached. In language, one established use certifies a neural model’s behaviour over a formally defined family of textual perturbations. That guarantee concerns model stability under the chosen perturbation relation; it does not prove that every perturbation preserves meaning or that the original answer is true.
A second use is more speculative and central here. The concrete domain can be a finite or symbolically represented set of admissible semantic interpretations. The abstraction may record possible referents, intervals, worlds, modalities, or evidence states. A may result holds in at least one represented interpretation. A must result holds in all. Cannot means no represented interpretation supports it. Unknown records insufficient coverage or abstraction precision. This gives a disciplined semantics to ambiguity, but soundness remains conditional on the interpretation set and transfer rules.
Symbolic execution complements this summary by searching for concrete witnesses. An abstract analysis can report that a violation remains possible. A symbolic engine, SMT solver, model checker, or planner can attempt to construct an interpretation and path that realises it. A replayable witness strengthens the finding. An infeasible witness or unsatisfiable core can reveal that the abstraction merged entities, widened time, ignored an exception, or treated a modal claim as actual.
This leads to counterexample-guided semantic refinement. Traditional CEGAR refines a program abstraction after a spurious counterexample. In the language setting, the refinement targets semantic commitments. A conflict is mapped back to source spans and the exact identity link, temporal boundary, quantifier, modality, or ontology choice responsible. An LLM or human revises only that local commitment. The rest of the semantic graph remains stable, and the analysis is replayed. This is more informative than asking a model to reread an entire document and try again.
Different engines contribute different evidence. Datalog provides recursive closure and explainable rules. ASP handles defaults, exceptions, and optimisation. SMT and CSP handle arithmetic, finite structure, and resource constraints. Automata and chart methods handle ordered patterns. Model checking handles temporal behaviour. Symbolic execution handles path witnesses. Theorem provers and proof assistants handle deductive obligations. Statistical engines estimate empirical parameters. A uniform result envelope must record semantics, coverage, assumptions, certificate, and frontier without claiming that these methods are interchangeable.
The engine router is therefore a typed compiler, not an LLM guess at runtime. Verb schemas and module profiles declare compatible lowerings. A query states the guarantee it requests. The planner selects engines under capability and budget contracts. The canonical result cites the source nodes, lowering version, engine, proof or witness, and unresolved assumptions. If no compatible engine exists, the correct output is an unsupported obligation rather than an improvised answer.
The reference architecture should be understood as a compiler and verification toolchain rather than as one agent prompt. Each stage materialises an immutable or content-addressed artefact so that errors can be assigned, replayed, and compared. Learning may improve a producer or theory, but it may not retroactively change the meaning of a published run.
The first artefact is the source package. It contains canonical text or data, revision digest, language, structure, channels, and stable spans. Paragraphs, headings, tables, quotations, equations, code blocks, and external records retain identity. The source package is lexical authority: it states what was present, not what it means. The second artefact is the task contract, which defines the question, source scope, world, jurisdiction, desired guarantee, allowed assumptions, budgets, and output policy. Semantic demand follows from the task.
The third layer is the evidence graph. Deterministic producers add structure and exact observations. Controlled parsers add high-confidence analyses for recognised fragments. LLMs propose entities, events, relations, definitions, and alternatives linked to spans. Retrieval systems add candidate background material with versioned sources. Coverage records state what was examined and what remained unavailable. Nothing becomes accepted merely because it appears in this graph.
The fourth layer is the interpretation set and grounding state. Candidate nodes are typed against selected libraries. Identity links, temporal locations, modalities, quantities, source relations, and contexts are checked. Alternatives remain separate. Human decisions are recorded as commitments rather than erased into the parser output. SLEnglish is the canonical language in which these nodes and commitments are expressed.
The fifth layer is the executable theory. Task-relevant nodes are lowered into one or more formal engines. Query-first materialisation can avoid formalising irrelevant parts of a long document, but it must expose coverage. A relational query, rule closure, temporal property, constraint system, symbolic path problem, or theorem is generated together with a source map. Compiler loss and unsupported constructs become diagnostics.
The sixth layer is execution and evidence. Engines return bindings, models, unsatisfiable cores, proofs, counterexamples, traces, statistical outputs, or stopped states. Independent checkers replay certificates where possible. Execution-level provenance records which nodes and versions contributed. A result inherits the weakest relevant guarantee: a verified proof over model-proposed premises remains a verified derivation over proposed premises, not an unconditional fact.
The final layer is constrained realisation. A renderer or LLM can produce accessible prose, but every sentence must be licensed by the canonical result. It may not convert conditional into unconditional language, possibility into actuality, conflict into consensus, or missing evidence into evidence of absence. Round-trip tests compare the realisation with the semantic record. The generated explanation is a view, not the authority.
Governance surrounds every stage. Libraries, compilers, prompts, models, engines, policies, and renderers have versions and digests. Learning agents work in staging. Publication requires conformance tests, semantic mutations, benchmark evidence, and explicit approval. Production reads immutable releases. Current-world knowledge packs have dates and jurisdictions. This architecture turns failure into a named artefact—unsupported type, unresolved identity, incomplete coverage, ambiguity, compilation loss, unsatisfiable theory, unavailable engine, or unlicensed sentence—rather than a vague hallucination.
Term
Explanation
Semantic Web standards
RDF, OWL, SHACL, SPARQL, and PROV-O are mature interoperability, reasoning, validation, query, and provenance standards (W3C 2012, 2013, 2017, 2026).
Common Logic
ISO/IEC 24707 supplies a framework for a family of first-order logic languages and remains an important interchange target.
CNL trade-offs
Precision, expressiveness, naturalness, and simplicity cannot all be maximised; SLEnglish intentionally sacrifices surface naturalness for regular execution (Kuhn 2014).
Counterexample-guided refinement
The CEGAR pattern motivates local semantic repair from infeasible witnesses and unsatisfiable cores rather than global “try again” prompting.
Term
Explanation
Semantic Web standards
RDF, OWL, SHACL, SPARQL, and PROV-O are mature interoperability, reasoning, validation, query, and provenance standards (W3C 2012, 2013, 2017, 2026).
Common Logic
ISO/IEC 24707 supplies a framework for a family of first-order logic languages and remains an important interchange target.
CNL trade-offs
Precision, expressiveness, naturalness, and simplicity cannot all be maximised; SLEnglish intentionally sacrifices surface naturalness for regular execution (Kuhn 2014).
Counterexample-guided refinement
The CEGAR pattern motivates local semantic repair from infeasible witnesses and unsatisfiable cores rather than global “try again” prompting.
The additional hypothesis is that a uniform semantic source layer can reduce the cost of translating, reviewing, and revising knowledge that must move among several backends. The same statement envelope can contain an observation, a reported claim, a qualified identity relation, a rule template, a query, or an operational request while its path and profile determine specialised semantics. A human reviewer sees one regular notation; an adapter sees a typed contract; an existing standard remains available as an interchange or reasoning target.
This hypothesis has several failure modes. The operator libraries may fragment into incompatible local dialects. The generic envelope may hide rather than expose backend-specific semantics. Path-addressed files may create maintenance and federation problems. LLM translation may remain too unreliable or too expensive. Human reviewers may prefer forms, diagrams, or ordinary controlled English. RDF and established standards may provide equivalent locality when used well. A code generator or typed host language may prove a simpler target.
A fair comparison therefore uses the same source material and questions. One implementation can use an RDF, OWL, SHACL, SPARQL, and PROV stack; another can use SLEnglish with adapters to equivalent components; a third can use direct structured LLM output; a fourth can use a conventional CNL or frame representation. The study measures valid compilation, semantic correction time, query accuracy, false assurance, artefact count, source traceability, update impact, interoperability, execution cost, and user comprehension.
A likely engineering outcome is hybrid. SLEnglish can operate as an authoring and semantic-diff layer over RDF and formal engines, as a compact representation for LLM-to-tool boundaries, or as a specialised representation for evidence and claims while mathematical and workflow artefacts retain their native languages. The comparison therefore measures where SLEnglish provides locality, readability, provenance, and compilation advantages and where established standards remain the better representation.
The comparison is technical and empirical. The relevant measures are correctness, locality, compositionality, governance, performance, tooling, migration cost, semantic preservation, and the ability to show how a natural-language commitment became a computational object. RDF, OWL, SHACL, SPARQL, PROV, Common Logic, Datalog, ASP, graph systems, and conventional CNLs provide strong baselines for these measurements.
CHAPTER 9
Research Programme and Evaluation
Research Questions, Baselines, and Measurements
The programme treats syntax, operator granularity, parameter commonality, path organisation, translation strategy, reasoning profiles, and user interfaces as separate hypotheses. Each can be evaluated against strong baselines while the others are held constant. Frozen corpora, canonical fixtures, semantic mutation tests, independent parsers, and backend adapters provide the experimental controls needed to identify which choices add value.
The primary outcome is false assurance: a determinate answer presented with stronger support than the source, coverage, formalisation, or execution justifies. Evaluation therefore rewards justified abstention, preserved ambiguity, explicit unknown states, correct source anchoring, and accurate dependency tracking in addition to task accuracy.
Evaluation should separate source-to-representation fidelity, representation quality, execution correctness, human review economics, and institutional usability. A strong solver cannot compensate for a mistranslation. A faithful graph that no reviewer can understand may not be deployable. An efficient language whose governance allows silent semantic drift is not a scientific infrastructure. Each layer needs its own baselines and measurements.
The first research question concerns semantic coverage. Can open, namespaced verb libraries represent ordinary event structure, scientific claims, and selected typological distinctions without proliferating incompatible microdialects? Coverage should be measured on held-out corpora from science, policy, software, and social analysis. Failures are classified as missing verb, missing parameter, ontology gap, unsupported discourse structure, context gap, or irreducible ambiguity. The registry evolves only from observed pressure.
The second question concerns translation fidelity. Can LLMs compile prose into valid SLEnglish while preserving negation, modality, quantifier scope, evidence, identity, time, and participant roles? The relevant metric is not parse success alone. It includes invented assumptions, lost distinctions, source-span accuracy, alternative preservation, correction time, and query equivalence. A stricter language may improve validity while worsening semantic drift; both must be measured.
The third question concerns cognitive utility. Do explicit operators for evidence, clusivity, identity, collective agency, causal depth, reversibility, scale, and uncertainty improve specific reasoning tasks? Experiments should compare plain English, annotated English, SLEnglish forms, and graphical views. Effects may be local rather than general. An operator that helps source monitoring but harms comprehension elsewhere belongs in an optional profile, not the universal core.
The fourth question concerns execution architecture. Does the SSA dependency form improve incremental recomputation, provenance, explanation, and multi-engine integration? Benchmarks should include full versus incremental replay, cache validity under contexts, parallel scheduling, recursive rules, alternative worlds, and non-monotonic updates. A correct full replay is the reference evaluator. Performance gains never justify semantic divergence.
The fifth question concerns interoperability. Can SLEnglish lower to RDF/OWL/SHACL, Datalog, ASP, SQL/SPARQL, SMT, temporal systems, and workflow tools with explicit preservation reports? Can existing artefacts be imported without semantic laundering? A small number of deep, tested adapters is more persuasive than many superficial ones. The project should publish both successful and failed mappings.
The seventh question concerns social and scientific governance. Can competing libraries coexist without hidden priority? Can operator authority, cultural provenance, jurisdiction, and deprecation be represented? Do users understand guarantee labels and unknown states? Does formalisation create bureaucratic pressure or false objectivity? Technical success without governance usability would not justify deployment in science.
Evaluation must be decomposed because one aggregate score hides where a system failed. Source-anchor accuracy measures whether nodes point to the correct spans. Verb and parameter precision measure structural compilation. Type validity measures conformity to libraries. Identity-link precision and calibration measure referential grounding. Temporal, modal, evidential, and causal accuracy measure specialised semantics.
Candidate-set coverage evaluates whether legitimate alternatives were retained. Query equivalence tests consequence rather than surface similarity.
False assurance receives its own metric. A case counts as false assurance when the system presents a determinate accepted result although a missing source, unresolved ambiguity, invalid grounding, incomplete coverage, unsupported lowering, or solver limitation should have prevented it. Appropriate abstention is rewarded. Coverage, latency, cost, and answer rate remain relevant, but they are reported beside false assurance rather than combined into a flattering number.
Human evaluation should ask narrow questions. Experts judge whether a node preserves the source, whether an identity link is justified, whether an alternative is material, whether a witness is feasible, whether assumptions are complete, and whether a rendered explanation changes modality. They should not be asked only whether an answer looks good. Inter-rater disagreement is data about semantic ambiguity and should not be forced into one gold label without analysis.
Metamorphic tests provide scalable pressure. Adding a negation should invert only relevant polarity. Replacing a direct observation with a report should change evidence mode but not event content. Changing inclusive to exclusive we should alter membership. Splitting one entity into two should invalidate identity-dependent conclusions. Moving a date across a legal threshold should affect only temporally scoped rules. These tests can be generated for every verb contract.
Mutation testing evaluates execution adapters and verifiers. Operators are altered to drop a parameter, swap roles, ignore a context, collapse alternatives, reuse a stale cache, or omit a source. Tests should detect the mutation. A high answer score with a low mutation score indicates decorative verification. Differential execution compares independent implementations or alternative lowerings on the same canonical package.
Security testing treats source and packages as hostile. Prompt injection, Unicode confusables, malicious imports, cyclic dependencies, resource exhaustion, forged provenance, stale signatures, and effect escalation are tested. A semantic node cannot authorise an operation merely by describing it. Capability checks and package signatures are verified outside the LLM. Privacy and copyright rules constrain captured prompts, datasets, and generated artefacts.
Baselines must be strong. They include direct LLM answering, chain-of-thought-equivalent prompting where permitted, tool-augmented LLMs without persistent semantic artefacts, JSON schema outputs, a conventional frame CNL, RDF/OWL/SHACL/PROV, single-formalism translation, and an optional circuit backend without SLEnglish. Ablations remove SSA IDs, explicit alternatives, source spans, open verb libraries, profiles, or independent verification to reveal which complexity earns its cost.
Evaluation object
Measures and comparison
Natural-language compilation
Parse validity, operator selection, role accuracy, scope accuracy, identity accuracy, alternative recall, source-span precision, and unsupported-content detection.
Human correction
Time to understand, number of edits, locality of edits, disagreement between reviewers, and stability after correction.
Semantic representation
Canonical stability, type coverage, library fragmentation, path resolution, migration cost, and ability to express required distinctions.
Reasoning and query
Answer accuracy, proof or witness validity, contradiction handling, unknown calibration, coverage compliance, and profile fidelity.
Update behaviour
Affected-node precision, selective rebuilding, equivalence with full replay, stale-cache detection, and version trace quality.
Interoperability
Round-trip preservation with RDF, PROV, rule engines, solvers, databases, code, and research-object formats.
Security and policy
Injection resistance, malicious import handling, capability enforcement, provenance forgery detection, resource limits, privacy, and copyright constraints.
Human-facing output
Claim-licensing compliance, condition preservation, uncertainty preservation, readability, and source traceability.
Overall value
False assurance, correct abstention, review cost, implementation complexity, latency, storage, maintenance, and domain adoption.
Baseline
Reason for inclusion
Direct LLM response
Establishes the value and limitations of the simplest natural-language interface.
Baseline
Reason for inclusion
Tool-using LLM without persistent semantics
Tests whether explicit semantic artefacts add value beyond ordinary tool orchestration.
Typed JSON or function schema
Provides a strong minimal structured-output baseline with low implementation cost.
Conventional frame CNL
Tests whether greater naturalness or subject–verb–object structure improves authoring and review.
RDF, OWL, SHACL, SPARQL, and PROV
Provides the mature graph, ontology, validation, query, and provenance baseline.

Generated conventional code

SLEnglish with alternative backends
Separates the value of the source representation from the value of any particular runtime.
Expert-authored formal representation
