knowledge/risk/epistemic.sle
Represents uncertainty arising from limited knowledge or evidence. Required: target uncertainty. Optional: coverage source-depth context. Output: epistemic-risk proposition.
knowledge/risk/deep-uncertainty.sle
Represents disagreement or ignorance about models, probabilities, or outcomes. Required: target. Optional: model alternative uncertainty context. Output: deep-uncertainty proposition.
knowledge/risk/unknown-unknown.sle
Records a recognised frontier where relevant possibilities or questions are not enumerated. Required: target. Optional: basis coverage context. Output: unknown-unknown status.
knowledge/risk/reversible.sle
Marks an action or state as reversible under explicit cost and time conditions. Required: target. Optional: value unit time context. Output: reversibility proposition.
knowledge/risk/irreversible.sle
Marks an action or state as practically or physically irreversible. Required: target. Optional: basis time scale context. Output: irreversibility proposition.
knowledge/risk/lock-in.sle
Represents persistence caused by switching cost, network effects, path dependence, or institutional inertia. Required: target. Optional: basis value unit time context. Output: lock-in proposition.
knowledge/risk/path-dependent.sle
Represents dependence of current state on the particular historical sequence. Required: target path. Optional: basis time context. Output: path-dependence proposition.
knowledge/system/emerge.sle
Represents a system-level property not attributable to any single component in isolation. Required: target part. Optional: basis scale model context. Output: emergence proposition.
knowledge/system/aggregate.sle
Computes or represents a macro property from member-level values under an aggregation rule. Required: part whole method. Optional: value unit scale context. Output: aggregate proposition.
knowledge/system/feedback-positive.sle
Represents a loop whose effects amplify a deviation or process. Required: source target path. Optional: strength delay scale context. Output: positive-feedback proposition.
knowledge/system/feedback-negative.sle
Represents a loop whose effects counteract a deviation or stabilise a variable. Required: source target path. Optional: strength delay scale context. Output: negative-feedback proposition.
knowledge/system/threshold.sle
Represents a system boundary at which behaviour changes discontinuously or materially. Required: target threshold. Optional: value distance model scale time context. Output: system-threshold proposition.
knowledge/system/phase-change.sle
Represents transition between qualitative regimes after a threshold or parameter change. Required: target from to. Optional: threshold model time context. Output: phase-change event.
knowledge/system/scale.sle
Indexes a proposition to the scale at which it is asserted. Required: proposition scale. Optional: basis context. Output: scale-qualified proposition.
knowledge/system/micro.sle
Marks individual or component-level scope. Required: proposition. Optional: scale context. Output: micro-level proposition.
knowledge/system/meso.sle
Marks organisational, network, or intermediate-level scope. Required: proposition. Optional: scale context. Output: meso-level proposition.
knowledge/system/macro.sle
Marks population, institutional, ecosystem, or system-wide scope. Required: proposition. Optional: scale context. Output: macro-level proposition.
knowledge/system/externality.sle
Represents a benefit or cost imposed on an entity outside the primary transaction or decision. Required: source target value. Optional: unit mode time context. Output: externality proposition.
knowledge/system/commons.sle
Represents a shared resource with access, governance, depletion, and benefit relations. Required: target collective. Optional: authority boundary value unit time context. Output: commons proposition.
knowledge/system/resilience.sle
Represents ability to preserve function or recover after disturbance. Required: target. Optional: basis degree model time context. Output: resilience proposition.
knowledge/system/antifragile.sle
Represents improvement under a defined distribution of stressors rather than mere recovery. Required: target. Optional: basis degree distribution scale context. Output: antifragility proposition.
knowledge/system/fragile.sle
Represents disproportionate harm under variability or stress. Required: target. Optional: basis degree distribution scale context. Output: fragility proposition.
knowledge/system/cascade.sle
Represents ordered propagation of effects through a system. Required: source target path. Optional: effect-order delay scale context. Output: cascade proposition.
knowledge/system/network-effect.sle
Represents value or behaviour that changes with network participation. Required: target member. Optional: basis value scale time context. Output: network-effect proposition.
knowledge/system/second-order.sle
Marks effects produced by direct effects rather than the initiating action alone. Required: source target. Optional: path model time context. Output: second-order proposition.
knowledge/system/third-order.sle
Marks effects produced through at least two intermediate consequence layers. Required: source target. Optional: path model time context. Output: third-order proposition.
knowledge/system/future-generation.sle
Indexes benefits, burdens, rights, or risks to people not yet existing. Required: target holder. Optional: time value uncertainty context. Output: posterity proposition.
knowledge/system/goodhart-pressure.sle
Represents pressure for a measure to become distorted when used as a target. Required: target. Optional: basis degree incentive time context. Output: Goodhart-pressure proposition.
science/research/hypothesis.sle
Declares a scientific hypothesis and the context in which it is tested. Required: proposition context. Optional: source model confidence status description. Output: hypothesis artefact.
science/research/observation.sle
Records an observation with observer, target, time, and source. Required: source-agent target. Optional: evidence-mode source-span time location context. Output: observation artefact.
science/research/measurement.sle
Records a measured value with unit, uncertainty, instrument, and method. Required: target value unit. Optional: uncertainty instrument method dataset time source-span context. Output: measurement artefact.
science/research/experiment.sle
Declares an experiment with hypothesis, protocol, intervention, and environment. Required: target method. Optional: premise dataset code environment start end context. Output: experiment artefact.
science/research/protocol.sle
Declares a versioned procedure and its obligations. Required: name version. Optional: when then unless authority environment source description. Output: protocol artefact.
science/research/dataset.sle
Registers a dataset with identity, version, schema, scope, and provenance. Required: id version. Optional: source content-digest coverage valid-time transaction-time description. Output: dataset artefact.
science/research/analysis.sle
Declares an analysis linking inputs, method, code, environment, and outputs. Required: method dataset. Optional: code model environment value uncertainty time context. Output: analysis artefact.
science/research/model.sle
Registers a scientific, statistical, or simulation model with assumptions and scope. Required: name version. Optional: premise method code dataset profile description. Output: model artefact.
science/research/simulation.sle
Declares a simulation run under a model, parameters, and environment. Required: model environment. Optional: dataset code value uncertainty time context. Output: simulation artefact.
science/research/result.sle
Records an output with method, uncertainty, and source artefacts. Required: value. Optional: unit uncertainty method dataset model code context. Output: result artefact.
science/research/claim.sle
Declares a scientific claim with scope, modality, and support relation. Required: proposition. Optional: source source-span model dataset confidence valid-time context. Output: scientific claim.
science/research/support.sle
Links evidence or results to a claim under a named method. Required: source target. Optional: basis strength confidence context. Output: support relation.
science/research/refute.sle
Links evidence or a counterexample to a challenged claim. Required: source target. Optional: basis witness strength context. Output: refutation relation.
science/research/replicate.sle
Records a replication attempt and its relation to an earlier experiment or result. Required: source target. Optional: method dataset environment result time context. Output: replication relation.
science/research/review.sle
Creates a review artefact targeting a claim, method, dataset, or package. Required: source-agent target. Optional: status severity proposition source-span time context. Output: review artefact.
science/research/challenge.sle
Records a specific objection to a premise, inference, identity, scope, or evidence relation. Required: source-agent target proposition. Optional: basis source-span severity context. Output: challenge artefact.
science/research/retract.sle
Records scientific withdrawal of a claim or artefact with reasons and authority. Required: target authority. Optional: basis time source context. Output: scientific retraction.
knowledge/provenance/derive.sle
States that an artefact or proposition was derived from one or more inputs. Required: source target. Optional: method code model time context. Output: derivation relation.
knowledge/provenance/attribute.sle
Attributes an artefact, action, or claim to an agent. Required: source target. Optional: role time authority context. Output: attribution relation.
knowledge/provenance/generate.sle
States that an activity generated an entity or artefact. Required: source target. Optional: time environment context. Output: generation relation.
knowledge/provenance/use.sle
States that an activity or analysis used an entity or artefact. Required: source target. Optional: time version context. Output: usage relation.
knowledge/provenance/quote.sle
Links a quotation or claim to exact source material. Required: source target source-span. Optional: language context. Output: quotation provenance.
knowledge/provenance/cite.sle
Links a claim or document to a bibliographic or data source. Required: source target. Optional: source-span basis context. Output: citation relation.
knowledge/provenance/version.sle
Links artefact versions and their content digests. Required: source target version. Optional: content-digest time context. Output: version-provenance relation.
knowledge/provenance/span.sle
Links a semantic node to an exact lexical, table, figure, code, or data span. Required: source source-span. Optional: content-digest context. Output: span relation.
execution/workflow/depend.sle
Declares that a build target depends on another node or artefact. Required: source target. Optional: mode version context. Output: build dependency.
execution/workflow/build.sle
Requests or records construction of a target from its dependencies. Required: target. Optional: profile environment budget time context. Output: build event.
execution/workflow/invalidate.sle
Marks cached or derived nodes invalid after a dependency change. Required: target source. Optional: basis time context. Output: invalidation event.
execution/workflow/recompute.sle
Requests or records recomputation under a new epoch. Required: target. Optional: source environment budget time context. Output: recomputation event.
execution/reasoning/derive.sle
Records a derived proposition with premises, rule, profile, and proof. Required: conclusion premise. Optional: rule profile proof context. Output: derivation result.
execution/reasoning/entail.sle
States that a target follows under a named formal semantics and coverage. Required: premise conclusion profile. Optional: proof coverage context. Output: entailment result.
execution/reasoning/contradict.sle
States incompatibility between propositions under a named profile. Required: proposition proposition. Optional: proof context severity. Output: contradiction result.
execution/reasoning/may.sle
States that a proposition holds in at least one admissible interpretation. Required: proposition context. Optional: coverage witness profile. Output: may result.
execution/reasoning/must.sle
States that a proposition holds in all admissible interpretations represented by the analysis. Required: proposition context. Optional: coverage proof profile. Output: must result.
execution/reasoning/cannot.sle
States that a proposition holds in no admissible interpretation represented by the analysis. Required: proposition context. Optional: coverage proof profile. Output: cannot result.
execution/reasoning/unknown.sle
Records inability to decide because of unsupported semantics, coverage, budget, or evidence. Required: target. Optional: missing-reason coverage budget context. Output: unknown result.
execution/reasoning/conflicted.sle
Records simultaneous support for a proposition and its explicit negation. Required: proposition. Optional: source alternative context. Output: conflicted result.
execution/reasoning/prove.sle
Requests or records proof under a named calculus or checker. Required: target profile. Optional: premise proof budget context. Output: proof result.
execution/reasoning/explain.sle
Produces a source- and proof-linked explanation of a result. Required: target. Optional: query return proof source-span language context. Output: explanation result.
execution/reasoning/witness.sle
Records a replayable model, trace, input, or path supporting possibility or violation. Required: target witness. Optional: environment source-span context. Output: witness result.
execution/reasoning/counterexample.sle
Records a replayable case refuting a universal claim or constraint. Required: target witness. Optional: environment source-span context. Output: counterexample result.
execution/query/whether.sle
Asks whether a proposition is true, false, unknown, or conflicted in a context. Required: pattern context. Optional: profile coverage guarantee return budget. Output: truth-status query.
execution/query/select.sle
Returns bindings satisfying a pattern. Required: pattern return. Optional: context profile coverage guarantee order limit budget. Output: selection query.
execution/query/count.sle
Counts matches under explicit classifier and coverage. Required: pattern. Optional: return context profile coverage guarantee limit. Output: count query.
execution/query/compare.sle
Compares results, models, versions, or scenarios along named dimensions. Required: source target basis. Optional: return context profile guarantee. Output: comparison query.
execution/query/why.sle
Requests derivation, provenance, causal path, or assumption explanation. Required: target. Optional: return context profile guarantee limit. Output: explanation query.
execution/query/what-if.sle
Evaluates a proposition under explicit counterfactual changes. Required: target assumption. Optional: alternative context profile guarantee budget. Output: counterfactual query.
execution/runtime/validate.sle
Runs syntax, schema, type, profile, provenance, and policy validation. Required: target. Optional: profile severity context. Output: validation result.
execution/runtime/reject.sle
Records deterministic rejection and its exact cause. Required: target basis. Optional: severity source-span context. Output: rejection result.
execution/runtime/diagnose.sle
Produces a machine-readable diagnostic for a stopped or degraded execution. Required: target status. Optional: basis severity source-span alternative context. Output: runtime diagnostic.
language/english/clause/declarative.sle
Constructs a proposition presented as a declarative clause without itself asserting that proposition. Required: proposition. Optional: source-span context information-structure. Output: clause object.
language/english/clause/interrogative.sle
Represents an interrogative form and its requested variable, polarity, or alternative set. Required: question. Optional: addressee context source-span. Output: question object.
language/english/clause/imperative.sle
Represents an imperative form while keeping command force distinct from operational authority. Required: action addressee. Optional: speaker strength context source-span. Output: directive object.
language/english/voice/active.sle
Records an active-voice surface alignment where relevant to source analysis. Required: proposition. Optional: subject agent source-span. Output: voice annotation.
language/english/voice/passive.sle
Normalises passive alignment while recording whether the agent is expressed, unknown, backgrounded, or strategically omitted. Required: proposition patient. Optional: agent omission-reason source-span. Output: voice-qualified proposition.
language/english/aspect/progressive.sle
Presents an event from within its unfolding interval. Required: event reference-time. Optional: boundedness interruption context. Output: aspect-qualified proposition.
language/english/aspect/perfect.sle
Relates a prior situation to a later reference point without assuming completion or current relevance beyond the selected profile. Required: event reference-time. Optional: result-state context. Output: aspect-qualified proposition.
language/english/aspect/habitual.sle
Represents a recurrent disposition or pattern rather than one actual occurrence. Required: event-pattern holder. Optional: frequency interval exceptions context. Output: habitual proposition.
language/english/tense/past.sle
Places reference time before the evaluation time under an explicit temporal profile. Required: proposition evaluation-time. Optional: remoteness interval context. Output: tense-qualified proposition.
language/english/tense/non-past.sle
Records English non-past morphology while requiring another operator or context to distinguish present, generic, scheduled, or future interpretation. Required: proposition. Optional: evaluation-time interpretation context. Output: tense annotation.
language/english/modality/modal.sle
Disambiguates an English modal auxiliary into epistemic, deontic, dynamic, predictive, or logical mode. Required: proposition mode. Optional: holder authority degree source-span context. Output: modal proposition.
language/english/negation/sentential.sle
Represents sentence negation with explicit structural scope. Required: proposition. Optional: scope focus context source-span. Output: negative proposition.
language/english/negation/not-all.sle
Represents narrow negation of a universal claim rather than universal negation. Required: quantified-proposition. Optional: context source-span. Output: scope-qualified negative proposition.
language/english/reference/article-indefinite.sle
Records an indefinite noun phrase and its introduction of a discourse referent or generic reading. Required: type. Optional: variable quantity genericity source-span context. Output: reference object.
language/english/reference/article-definite.sle
Records a definite description with its identification basis rather than assuming uniqueness globally. Required: description. Optional: referent familiarity uniqueness-scope source-span context. Output: reference object.
language/english/reference/pronoun.sle
Represents a pronoun and candidate antecedents, agreement features, discourse role, and confidence. Required: source-span. Optional: antecedent alternative person number gender perspective context. Output: reference candidate.
language/english/reference/expletive.sle
Marks syntactic subjects such as weather it or existential there that do not denote ordinary semantic agents. Required: form clause. Optional: source-span context. Output: expletive annotation.
language/english/discourse/ellipsis.sle
Records omitted recoverable material and candidate reconstructions. Required: source-span alternative. Optional: antecedent context confidence. Output: ellipsis interpretation.
language/english/discourse/presuppose.sle
Records content conventionally treated as backgrounded or already accommodated without automatically asserting it in every context. Required: proposition trigger. Optional: holder context source-span. Output: presupposition object.
language/english/discourse/implicate.sle
Records a defeasible pragmatic enrichment distinct from literal content. Required: proposition basis. Optional: speaker addressee context confidence cancellable source-span. Output: implicature candidate.
language/english/information/topic.sle
Marks the discourse topic associated with a proposition. Required: target proposition. Optional: contrast context source-span. Output: information-structure annotation.
language/english/information/focus.sle
Marks focused material and the relevant alternative set. Required: target proposition. Optional: alternatives contrast context source-span. Output: focus annotation.
language/english/information/cleft.sle
Represents cleft constructions with explicit focus and presupposed background. Required: focus background. Optional: source-span context. Output: information-structure object.
language/english/coordination/and.sle
Represents coordination while allowing the relation to be specified as addition, sequence, result, contrast, or joint participation. Required: proposition proposition. Optional: relation order context source-span. Output: coordinated proposition.
language/english/coordination/or.sle
Represents inclusive or exclusive alternatives with explicit scope and exhaustivity. Required: proposition proposition. Optional: mode exhaustive context source-span. Output: disjunctive proposition.
language/english/subordination/conditional.sle
Represents condition and consequent with explicit modality, counterfactual status, and temporal relation. Required: condition consequence. Optional: mode world context source-span. Output: conditional proposition.
language/english/subordination/causal.sle
Records that an English because or related clause presents an explanatory or causal relation, without deciding the causal profile automatically. Required: source target. Optional: causal-layer evidence context source-span. Output: causal candidate.
language/english/subordination/concessive.sle
Represents a concession relation between an expectation and a proposition. Required: expectation proposition. Optional: holder degree context source-span. Output: concessive proposition.
language/english/report/direct-speech.sle
Preserves quoted wording, speaker, addressee, time, and source span without asserting quoted content. Required: content speaker. Optional: addressee time source context. Output: quotation object.
language/english/report/indirect-speech.sle
Represents reported content with tense, person, and perspective adjustments kept distinct from acceptance. Required: proposition source-agent. Optional: perspective time source-depth context source-span. Output: report object.
language/english/nominalisation/event.sle
Expands an event nominalisation into a candidate event frame and records omitted roles. Required: nominal source-span. Optional: agent patient theme time cause alternatives context. Output: event candidate.
language/english/compound/noun-noun.sle
Represents the underspecified semantic relation inside an English noun compound and preserves alternatives when consequence changes. Required: modifier head. Optional: relation alternative context source-span. Output: compound interpretation.
language/english/comparison/comparative.sle
Represents comparison along an explicit dimension, standard, and degree. Required: source target basis. Optional: degree unit context source-span. Output: comparison proposition.
language/english/comparison/superlative.sle
Represents a maximum or minimum relative to an explicit comparison set and coverage. Required: source basis comparison-set. Optional: order coverage context source-span. Output: superlative proposition.
language/english/possession/genitive.sle
Treats the English genitive as an underspecified relation requiring ownership, kinship, authorship, part, control, association, or another declared interpretation. Required: possessor possessed. Optional: relation alternatives context source-span. Output: possession candidate.
language/english/phrasal-verb.sle
Resolves a verb-particle construction to a declared semantic operator while preserving literal alternatives where relevant. Required: verb particle source-span. Optional: operator alternative context. Output: lexical-semantic candidate.
language/english/light-verb.sle
Represents constructions such as take a walk or make a decision through an event operator rather than treating the light verb as the principal action. Required: verb nominal. Optional: agent roles context source-span. Output: event candidate.
language/english/control/subject.sle
Records the understood subject of a non-finite complement when controlled by a matrix argument. Required: controller embedded-proposition. Optional: relation context source-span. Output: control relation.
language/english/raising.sle
Records a raising construction in which a surface subject is not assigned its semantic role by the matrix predicate. Required: source embedded-proposition. Optional: evidential-mode context source-span. Output: raising analysis.
language/english/attachment/alternative.sle
Preserves materially distinct prepositional, relative-clause, or modifier attachments as explicit alternatives. Required: source-span alternative. Optional: ranking confidence context. Output: interpretation set.
APPENDIX D
English and Cross-Linguistic Constructions as Executable Operators
This table records how the English baseline and the cross-linguistic survey are translated into executable distinctions. Each row identifies a grammatical or conceptual phenomenon and the SLEnglish operator pattern that can represent it. The mapping is refined through grammar consultation, corpus analysis, speaker or specialist expertise where relevant, translation tests, and reasoning tasks.
Term
Explanation

English baseline mapping. Constructs a proposition presented as a declarative clause without itself asserting that proposition. Required: proposition. Optional: source-span context information-structure. Output: clause object.

English baseline mapping. Represents an interrogative form and its requested variable, polarity, or alternative set. Required: question. Optional: addressee context source-span. Output: question object.

Grammatical evidentiality
Languages with grammatical evidentials require or strongly conventionalise distinctions such as direct observation, report, and inference. Representative scholarship includes Aikhenvald and the WALS evidentiality maps. SLEnglish mapping: knowledge/evidence/observe.sle, knowledge/evidence/report.sle, knowledge/evidence/infer.sle, knowledge/evidence/remember.sle, knowledge/evidence/measure.sle; profiles may require evidence-mode and source-depth.
Mirativity
Mirative systems mark information as unexpected, newly integrated, or contrary to a model. The category is debated and often overlaps evidentiality. SLEnglish mapping: knowledge/epistemic/expect.sle, knowledge/epistemic/counterexpect.sle, knowledge/epistemic/surprise.sle with mirativity, holder, prior model, and degree.
Inclusive versus exclusive first-person plural
Many languages distinguish a “we” that includes the addressee from one that excludes them. English normally leaves this optional. SLEnglish mapping: language/reference/inclusive-we.sle and language/reference/exclusive-we.sle, with explicit speaker, addressee, members, and collective scope.
Dual, trial, paucal, and associative number
Languages may grammatically distinguish exactly two, exactly three, a small plurality, or an individual plus associates. SLEnglish mapping:
language/number/dual.sle, language/number/paucal.sle, and language/number/associative.sle; classifiers and coverage specify what is counted.
language/number/trial.sle,
Numeral classifiers
Classifier languages require counting through a classifying unit that can encode shape, animacy, arrangement, or conventional individuation. SLEnglish mapping: science/quantity/classifier.sle and science/quantity/count.sle with classifier, identity criterion, and coverage.
Alienable and inalienable possession
Possessive systems often distinguish transferable ownership from kin, body-part, constitutive, or otherwise inalienable relations. SLEnglish
mapping: language/possession/inalienable.sle, language/possession/kin.sle,
language/possession/alienable.sle,
language/possession/body-part.sle, language/possession/stewardship.sle.
and
Absolute spatial frames
Languages documented by Levinson and others may habitually use cardinal, uphill/downhill, riverine, or seaward/landward frames rather than body-relative left/right. SLEnglish mapping: language/space/north-of.sle, uphill, upriver, seaward, and related operators with explicit anchor and scale.
Remoteness tense
Some tense systems distinguish recent and remote pasts or near and remote futures. SLEnglish mapping: language/time/recent-past.sle,
language/time/remote-past.sle, language/time/remote-future.sle, with interval definitions supplied by a context profile.
language/time/near-future.sle,
Rich aspect systems
Perfective, imperfective, progressive, habitual, perfect, prospective, iterative, durative, punctual, telic, and atelic distinctions expose event contour rather than only chronological location. SLEnglish mapping: the corresponding language/time/* operators.
Switch-reference
Clause-chaining systems can mark whether adjacent clauses share a
central
participant.
SLEnglish
mapping:
language/discourse/same-reference.sle language/discourse/different-reference.sle with clause nodes, participant anchors, and source spans.
and
Logophoricity
Logophoric systems identify forms interpreted relative to the consciousness or speech perspective of a reported individual. SLEnglish mapping: language/reference/logophor.sle and language/perspective/logophoric.sle with explicit holder and context.
Egophoricity
Some systems mark privileged personal authority or involvement, especially for one’s own states and intentional actions. SLEnglish mapping: language/perspective/egophoric.sle with holder, proposition, evidence mode, and context; it does not automatically imply truth.
Obviation and proximate status
Algonquian and other discourse systems can distinguish a more central third person from less central third persons. SLEnglish
mapping: language/reference/obviative.sle, used to stabilise reference and perspective.
language/reference/proximate.sle
and
Direct–inverse alignment
Direct–inverse systems mark whether an event follows or reverses a
person/animacy language/perspective/pivot.sle plus explicit event roles; experimental operator libraries may add alignment/direct.sle and alignment/inverse.sle if corpus evidence justifies them.
hierarchy.

mapping:
Applicative constructions
Applicatives promote beneficiaries, instruments, locations, or recipients into core event structure. SLEnglish mapping: language/event/applicative.sle or specialised event verbs with explicit promoted role.
Antipassive constructions
Antipassives foreground agent and activity while reducing patient prominence or definiteness. SLEnglish mapping: language/event/antipassive.sle with explicit underlying proposition, agent, optional patient, and aspect.
Causative constructions
Languages grammaticalise adding a causer and causee in varied ways. SLEnglish mapping: language/event/causative.sle and the knowledge/causation/* library; the representation must distinguish causing an event from merely asserting causal relation.
Middle and reflexive voice
Middle and reflexive systems alter participant structure and affectedness. SLEnglish mapping: language/event/middle.sle and language/event/reflexive.sle with explicit co-reference or agent suppression.
Pluractionality
Some languages mark event plurality, distribution, repetition, or participant plurality morphologically. SLEnglish mapping:
language/event/repeat.sle, language/time/iterative.sle, and language/number/distributive.sle.
language/event/distribute.sle,
Serial verb constructions
Serial verbs encode tightly linked actions, paths, aspects, or participant sharing without ordinary coordination. SLEnglish mapping: language/event/serialise.sle with ordered proposition nodes and declared shared roles.
Medial verbs and clause chaining
Clause-chaining languages use medial forms to mark non-final clauses, participant continuity, temporal relations, and discourse dependency. SLEnglish mapping: discourse switch-reference operators plus time and workflow dependencies.
Realis and irrealis
Languages may mark whether an event is presented as actual, intended, hypothetical, feared, possible, or counterfactual. SLEnglish
mapping: knowledge/modality/irrealis.sle, knowledge/modality/counterfactual.sle, and explicit contexts.
knowledge/modality/realis.sle,
Quotative and reportative constructions
Many languages distinguish exact quotation, report, hearsay, and
speaker language/discourse/quote-direct.sle, language/discourse/report-indirect.sle, knowledge/evidence/quote.sle, and knowledge/evidence/report.sle.
perspective.
SLEnglish
mapping:
Honorific and humilific systems
Speech may grammatically encode relative rank, respect, familiarity, or self-lowering. SLEnglish mapping: social/relation/honor.sle, social/relation/humble-self.sle, social/relation/address-deferential.sle, and social/relation/address-familiar.sle.
Taboo and avoidance registers
Some communities maintain special vocabularies or registers for particular kin relations, rituals, or contexts. SLEnglish mapping: social/relation/taboo.sle and social/relation/register.sle with community authority and validity scope.
Noun classes and animacy hierarchies
Languages classify nouns by gender, animacy, shape, social status, or other semantic and grammatical criteria. SLEnglish mapping: typed entity classifications and explicit classifier profiles; no one hierarchy is assumed universal.
Ideophones
Ideophones can depict sensory scenes, movement, texture, intensity, or manner with high expressive precision. SLEnglish should retain the original form as data and may map interpretable dimensions through perception or event operators without claiming exhaustive translation.
Olfactory lexicons
Research on languages such as Jahai and Maniq shows that odours can be named with greater abstraction than English stereotypes suggest. SLEnglish mapping: knowledge/evidence/sense.sle with olfactory mode plus domain smell operators, while culturally specific lexemes remain versioned concepts.
Relational nouns
Some languages lexicalise relations that English expresses as possessed nouns or loose prepositions. SLEnglish mapping: dedicated relation or possession verbs rather than generic ownership.
Body-part incorporation
Incorporation can encode recurrent participant structure inside the verb. SLEnglish mapping: language/event/incorporate.sle plus language/possession/body-part.sle and explicit affected participant.
Distributive versus collective predication
A plural proposition may apply to each member, to the group jointly, or to a subgroup. SLEnglish mapping: language/number/distributive.sle, language/number/collective.sle, social/collective/* constructors, and explicit quantifier scope.
Inclusive imperative and hortative
Languages can distinguish commands including speaker, addressee, or third parties. SLEnglish mapping: participant-set constructors combined with language/speech-act/command.sle or a hortative profile.
Evidential source depth
Natural languages rarely grammaticalise an arbitrary number of testimony hops, but the distinction is crucial for science and online information. SLEnglish invention: source-depth and knowledge/provenance/derive.sle chains attached to evidence verbs.
Process identity
No single grammatical category universally solves the identity of clouds, rivers, flames, organisms, or institutions. SLEnglish conceptual invention: knowledge/identity/process.sle with causal path, boundary grade, scale, interval, and degree.
Collective agency architecture
Natural languages readily personify states, firms, markets, and societies. SLEnglish conceptual invention: collective constructors and social/agency/delegated.sle, distributed, emergent, or centralized to distinguish decision from outcome.
Responsibility vectors
Ordinary grammar compresses causation, knowledge, control, benefit, mandate, and remediation into “responsible.” SLEnglish conceptual invention: social/responsibility/* operators that allocate these dimensions separately.
Reversibility grade
Ordinary verbs rarely mark whether an action can be reversed cheaply, only at high cost, or not at all. SLEnglish conceptual
invention: knowledge/risk/irreversible.sle, and reversibility parameters.
knowledge/risk/reversible.sle,
Causal depth
Everyday causal language mixes trigger, direct mechanism, enabling condition, structural cause, and feedback. SLEnglish mapping: distinct knowledge/causation/* verbs and causal-layer.
Scale address
A proposition may be true at individual, organisational, population, or ecosystem scale and misleading elsewhere. SLEnglish conceptual invention: knowledge/system/scale.sle, micro, meso, and macro.
Boundary grade
Clouds, communities, species, and institutions may have fuzzy, operational, dynamic, or contested boundaries. SLEnglish conceptual invention: knowledge/identity/boundary.sle and the boundary parameter.
Unknown versus unknowable
English can express the distinction lexically but rarely requires it.
SLEnglish knowledge/epistemic/unknowable.sle, coverage, and missing-reason.
knowledge/epistemic/unknown.sle,
Absence versus non-existence
Missing records, zero measured quantity, unavailable evidence, and non-existence are different. SLEnglish mapping: explicit missing-value atoms, science/quantity/zero.sle, knowledge/entity/exist.sle, and coverage-sensitive knowledge/logic/none.sle.
Goodhart pressure
Metrics used as targets can change the system they describe.
SLEnglish knowledge/system/goodhart-pressure.sle linked to incentive, target, and observed distortion.
conceptual
invention:
Posterity reference
Future people cannot participate in current pronoun systems or contracts but may bear effects. SLEnglish conceptual invention: knowledge/system/future-generation.sle with time, uncertainty, benefit, and burden.
Option value and opportunity cost
Ordinary language often hides the value of retaining alternatives and the best forgone path. SLEnglish mapping:
social/decision/option-value.sle social/decision/opportunity-cost.sle.
and
Unknown unknowns
A system may know that its current question set is incomplete without enumerating what is missing. SLEnglish conceptual invention: knowledge/risk/unknown-unknown.sle with an explicit frontier and coverage statement.

APPENDIX F
Evaluation Matrix
Term
Explanation
Outer syntax
Compare canonical SLEnglish with frame-oriented CNL, JSON/AST, and parenthesised forms. Measure parser size, generation validity, correction time, semantic diff quality, and token cost. Failure response: change the surface while retaining the IR.
Open verb libraries
Use held-out domains and typological constructions. Measure coverage, category confusion, schema burden, library fragmentation, and migration cost. Failure response: merge or split verbs, move distinctions to parameters, or narrow standard libraries.
Parameter registry
Compile corpora of n-ary scientific and institutional claims. Measure awkward encodings, extension frequency, role agreement, and interoperability. Failure response: add namespaced roles, promote proven roles, or abandon premature universal roles.
Visible SSA
Compare explicit and compiler-generated IDs. Measure human load, provenance quality, cache precision, diff stability, and correction locality. Failure response: hide IDs in normal views while retaining canonical SSA internally.
Folder namespaces
Test refactoring, federation, and multi-team packages. Measure rename errors, discoverability, merge conflicts, and stable identity. Failure response: make folders editorial only and use manifest or content-addressed naming.
LLM compiler
Evaluate held-out prose, concepts, negation, scope, identity, evidence, and alternatives. Measure semantic fidelity, invented assumptions, source alignment, abstention, and correction time. Failure response: hybrid parser/encoder, narrower domains, or direct authoring.
RDF-stack comparison
Build equivalent end-to-end research packages. Measure artefact count, authoring and review time, interoperability, validation, query correctness, update impact, and false assurance. Failure response: use SLEnglish only as source syntax or abandon runtime primacy.
Missing-grammar profiles
Compare ordinary English, annotated English, SLEnglish, and graphical views on source, identity, agency, causality, and systems tasks. Measure target error reduction and cognitive load. Failure response: make markers optional, domain-specific, or remove them.
VSA/HDC
Hold corpus and exact kernel constant while varying retrieval. Measure recall, latency, memory, noise robustness, calibration, and provenance loss. Failure response: remove VSA/HDC from the core and use dense, lexical, or graph retrieval.
Multi-formalism lowering
Compare native and lowered execution across Datalog, RDF/OWL, ASP, SMT, temporal, and proof profiles. Measure semantic preservation, certificate quality, adapter complexity, and cross-engine inconsistency. Failure response: narrow the portfolio.
Incremental circuits
Compare incremental recomputation with full replay under edits, retractions, contexts, and recursion. Measure trace equivalence, invalidation precision, latency, and stale-cache errors. Failure response: restrict incremental execution to proven operator classes.
Executable science
Ask an independent team to reproduce and modify a pilot package. Measure answerable queries, rebuild correctness, proof utility, expert judgement, cost, and false assurance. Failure response: narrow the goal to semantic audit or discontinue the broader claim.
Controlled realisation
Compare templates, constrained LLM rendering, direct generation, and post-hoc checking. Measure modality preservation, omission, unsupported additions, readability, and source traceability. Failure response: use deterministic views for high-assurance output.
Governance
Test competing libraries, cultural provenance, jurisdiction, deprecation, malicious packages, and authorisation. Measure user comprehension, conflict visibility, security outcomes, and authority laundering. Failure response: prevent production deployment until governance is adequate.
Folder-path refactoring test
Move operator files without semantic change and verify that manifest aliases, content identities, migrations, and historical packages remain valid.
Backend neutrality test
Execute equivalent SLEnglish fragments through at least two compatible backend families and compare results, receipts, loss reports, and correction effort.
English construction coverage
Measure translation fidelity for tense, aspect, modality, negation, reference, nominalisation, coordination, clause linkage, and information structure.
Typological operator review Require linguistic specialist review for selected evidential, clusivity, reference, possession, space, and discourse operators and document where analytical mappings fail.
APPENDIX G
Critical Reading Map
The reading map relates the programme to linguistic relativity, typology, formal semantics, conceptual engineering, process ontology, collective agency, controlled languages, knowledge representation, formal execution, and reproducible research. It distinguishes what each line of work establishes from the constructive claims proposed here.
Work or research line
Contribution, limitation, and use in this programme
Scope of the reading map
The literature behind this book does not form one unified field. It spans linguistic anthropology, typology, psycholinguistics, neuroscience, philosophy of language, metaphysics, social ontology, translation studies, conceptual engineering, and the history of artificial languages. The following map is therefore organised by intellectual function rather than disciplinary ownership. It identifies what each work makes possible, what it does not establish, and how the present book departs from it.
John A. Lucy: turning Whorf into a research programme
Lucy’s Language Diversity and Thought (1992) remains one of the best places to begin because it reconstructs linguistic relativity as a methodological problem rather than a slogan. The book explains why earlier discussion repeatedly oscillated between grand claims and weak evidence, then proposes a comparative strategy: establish a precise grammatical contrast, derive a cognitive contrast from it, and test speakers on tasks that do not merely repeat the wording of their language. His companion study, Grammatical Categories and Cognition, compares English and Yucatec Maya and shows how grammatical treatment of number and nominal reference may organise classification. Lucy contributes the discipline this project needs. A proposed cognitive marker is not justified because it sounds philosophically illuminating. One must specify the contrast, the task, the mechanism, and the alternative explanation. His limitation is historical rather than conceptual: the programme predates much later work in neuroscience, computational modelling, and large-scale typological databases. This book adopts his experimental caution while extending the target from inherited language differences to deliberately designed distinctions.
Stephen C. Levinson: the strongest model of a domain-specific case Space in Language and Cognition (2003) is a model of how cross-linguistic work can connect grammar, field observation, gesture, memory, and non-verbal reasoning. Levinson distinguishes relative, intrinsic, and absolute frames of reference and documents communities in which speakers routinely use geocentric systems rather than speaker-centred left and right. The importance of the work lies not in the picturesque fact that some people maintain cardinal orientation. It lies in the causal architecture: conversational success repeatedly rewards a spatial coding strategy, which then becomes available in memory, gesture, and navigation. For the present book, spatial framing provides a template for the idea of cognitive interfaces.
It shows how a linguistic requirement can train a reusable attentional habit without constituting an impermeable prison. The limitation is equally instructive. Space is an unusually embodied, measurable domain with clear behavioural tasks. Identity, collective agency, and responsibility are socially contested and may not yield effects of comparable size or simplicity. Alexandra Aikhenvald and the typology of evidence Aikhenvald’s Evidentiality (2004) is the indispensable survey of grammatical systems that mark information source. It shows that languages may require distinctions such as visual, non-visual sensory, inferred, assumed, or reported evidence, and that these systems vary in both structure and discourse use. The work also separates evidentiality from simple certainty: indirect evidence need not be weak evidence, and direct perception need not be reliable. This distinction is foundational for the proposed epistemic checksum. English commonly compresses source, confidence, trust, and truth into vague expressions such as apparently or I think. Aikhenvald shows that source mode is a legitimate grammatical dimension in its own right.
Yet typological existence is not evidence of cognitive superiority. A community with obligatory evidentials is not automatically more sceptical, scientific, or truthful. The design opportunity is narrower: source marking may reduce particular provenance failures in settings where claims travel through long human–AI chains.
Caleb Everett: a broad empirical synthesis, with uneven terrain
Everett’s Linguistic Relativity: Evidence Across Languages and Cognitive Domains (2013) surveys research on space, time, quantity, colour, objects and substances, gender, and other domains. Its strength is cumulative: linguistic relativity is not one all-or-nothing hypothesis but a family of local claims supported to different degrees. The book also makes field linguistics central. Without grammars of typologically distant languages, cognitive science repeatedly mistakes properties of familiar European languages for properties of the mind. The breadth is also the danger. Colour boundaries, exact number, spatial frames, grammatical gender, and temporal metaphors involve different mechanisms and evidential standards. They should not be stacked as if each provided an equal vote for a single doctrine. The present book follows Everett in treating linguistic diversity as cognitive evidence, but insists on an epistemic label for every example: attested grammatical contrast, demonstrated behavioural effect, philosophical extrapolation, or new design hypothesis.
Anna Wierzbicka: escaping English as an invisible metalanguage
Imprisoned in English (2013) argues that English has become not only a global medium but an unexamined conceptual standard in the humanities and social sciences. Terms such as mind, emotion, experience, evidence, self, and privacy carry histories that are not neutral windows onto universal reality. Wierzbicka’s Natural Semantic Metalanguage programme attempts to analyse meanings through a small set of cross-linguistically grounded semantic primes. The book’s most valuable contribution is diagnostic: a scholar can criticise linguistic imperialism while continuing to formulate every comparison in English categories. The proposed escape through universal semantic primes is more controversial. Semantic decomposition may itself import theoretical commitments, and universality is difficult to establish across underdescribed languages. This book accepts the diagnosis without depending on the full NSM programme. Its method is plural: retain several partial metalanguages, expose translation loss, and design terms that declare rather than conceal their assumptions.
Barbara Cassin and the philosophy of untranslatability
The English edition of Dictionary of Untranslatables: A Philosophical Lexicon (Cassin et al. 2014) follows nearly four hundred philosophical, literary, and political terms across languages and intellectual traditions. Its achievement is to show that translation is not the replacement of labels over identical concepts. Words such as logos, Dasein, pravda, stato, and saudade carry networks of contrasts, etymologies, controversies, and institutional uses. An “untranslatable” is not necessarily impossible to explain; it is a site where translation must remain active. This book differs from a dictionary of untranslatables in two ways. First, it is interested less in lexical jewels than in distinctions that grammar makes hard to omit. Second, it moves from historical diagnosis to conceptual design. Still, Cassin’s work offers an ethical warning: a new term should not pretend to settle a debate merely by naming it. Good concepts preserve the tensions they clarify.
N. J. Enfield: language as coordination rather than transparent In Language vs. Reality (2022), Enfield presents language as an Blasi and colleagues: English bias as a scientific problem Blasi, Henrich, Adamou, Kemmerer, and Majid (2022) argue that cognitive science’s reliance on English and a narrow range of languages hinders the discovery of both variation and universals.
description
extraordinary tool for social coordination whose efficiency depends on selective compression. Words do not reproduce the world at full resolution. They supply public handles that allow people to align attention, negotiate commitment, tell stories, persuade, and act together. Misfit between word and world is therefore not a defect accidentally added to an otherwise perfect representational system; it is partly the price of usability. This argument prevents the present project from becoming naïvely perfectionist. A language that encoded every relevant dimension in every sentence would be unusable. Better cognitive interfaces must choose where friction is worth imposing. The design goal is not maximum explicitness but strategically priced precision: make a distinction cheap when omission has predictable costs, and leave it optional elsewhere.
Guy Deutscher and John McWhorter: the useful popular opposition
Deutscher’s Through the Language Glass (2010) offers an accessible defence of moderate linguistic influence through colour, spatial orientation, and grammatical gender. McWhorter’s The Language Hoax (2014) attacks romantic claims that grammar embodies a people’s worldview or makes some cultures more perceptive, rational, or profound. Read together, the books define the rhetorical danger zone. Deutscher shows why the topic is too interesting to dismiss; McWhorter shows why attractive anecdotes can outrun evidence. Neither should serve as the primary scientific authority for a research programme. Their value is argumentative. Any public-facing book on language and thought must be able to survive McWhorter’s objection: an evidential marker does not make its speakers better epistemologists. It must also preserve Deutscher’s insight: habitual requirements can matter even when they do not alter the ultimate limits of thought. The present thesis is deliberately calibrated to occupy that middle ground.
English is typologically unusual in several respects, yet it frequently supplies experimental materials, theoretical categories, and intuitions that are treated as transparent. The problem is not simply missing cultural diversity in samples. It is a feedback loop in which English determines the questions, tasks, and answer formats. This work supports one of the book’s strongest claims: linguistic comparison is not decorative anthropology added after a theory has been built. It is a method for debugging theory. The constructive response is not to reverse the hierarchy and romanticise non-European languages. It is to make cross-linguistic robustness a requirement for claims about human cognition and to include speakers and scholars of the languages as intellectual partners rather than sources of data. Fedorenko, Piantadosi, and Gibson: the communication-first correction Fedorenko, Piantadosi, and Gibson (2024) synthesise evidence that language is primarily a system for communication and cultural transmission rather than the substrate of all complex thought.
Neuropsychological dissociations, brain imaging, and the limited overlap between the language network and domain-general reasoning systems challenge the idea that thought is simply inner language.
Their account is a necessary counterweight to any expansive Whorfianism. The present thesis survives this correction because it does not identify language with cognition. Interfaces shape use without constituting the machine. A notation can transform science even though scientists also think visually, spatially, emotionally, and mathematically. The communication-first view may even strengthen the social version of the argument: because language coordinates many minds, small representational defaults can influence institutions and cumulative knowledge even when individual thought remains multimodal.
Gennari and the turn toward mechanism and task
Gennari’s 2025 review argues that language effects in higher-order cognition must be situated in task goals, context, and available mechanisms. Linguistic categories may be recruited when a task demands fine discrimination, memory, verbal rehearsal, or social interpretation and may disappear under other conditions. This approach replaces the search for a global verdict with an analysis of when language becomes functionally relevant. That is the proper model for testing SLEnglish. Source tags should not be expected to improve every form of reasoning. They may help in delayed summarisation, contested claims, and multi-hop provenance while adding useless friction to direct perception. Identity operators may help in software lineage and institutional succession but not in ordinary object recognition. A design succeeds locally or not at all.
John, Macedo-Rouet, and Gros: current counterevidence matters
A preregistered 2026 study of 1,200 participants across English, French, German, and Mandarin tested whether language-specific temporal metaphors altered strategies for solving arithmetic word problems. The results favoured semantic content over linguistic metaphor: ordinal versus cardinal interpretations were driven by the problem structure rather than by the preferred spatialisation of time in the participants’ languages. This finding is valuable precisely because it is negative for a broad relativity claim. It demonstrates why the field must prefer preregistration, multi-language sampling, and task analysis to memorable one-off effects. The present project treats such failures as design information. A linguistic marker will not change reasoning merely because a philosopher can describe a conceptual difference. The marker must be recruited by the task, learned sufficiently, and connected to a useful operation.
WALS and Grambank: a design library with epistemic limits
The World Atlas of Language Structures and Grambank transform typological knowledge into searchable comparative infrastructure. WALS offers curated chapters on evidentiality, clusivity, number, classifiers, tense, possession, and many other dimensions. Grambank aggregates more than four hundred thousand data points across more than 2,400 language varieties and 195 grammatical features. Together they make visible both the breadth of human design and the severe unevenness of documentation. Databases tempt users to treat a feature as a binary property detached from discourse, history, and analysis. “Has evidentiality” can conceal radically different systems; “lacks a feature” may mean that the grammar is incomplete or that analysts disagree. For conceptual invention, databases are hypothesis generators, not substitute grammars. Every promising comparison should return to specialist descriptions and, whenever possible, speakers.
Nicholson and Dupré: biology after substance ontology
Everything Flows: Towards a Processual Philosophy of Biology (Nicholson and Dupré 2018) argues that organisms and biological systems are better understood as stabilised processes than as substances that merely undergo change. Chapters by DiFrisco and Pradeu address criteria of identity, persistence, and genidentity in biological processes. The volume gives philosophical depth to the intuition behind the cloud, flame, and organism examples: continuity may consist in organised activity, causal dependence, and regulated replacement rather than sameness of matter. The relevance extends beyond biology. Software services, institutions, markets, and AI systems can also persist through controlled transformation. But process language should not become another universal solvent. Some legal and moral practices intentionally track bodies, persons, signatures, ownership, or lineage. The proposal is not to replace object ontology with process ontology; it is to mark which mode of identity a claim invokes.
The Problem of the Many: why a cloud is philosophically serious
Weatherson’s contemporary presentation of the Problem of the Many begins with a cloud whose boundary admits many nearly equivalent collections of droplets. If each candidate is equally eligible, ordinary singular reference appears to select one object without a fact that clearly privileges it. The puzzle connects vagueness, composition, identity, and counting. For this book, the cloud is not a decorative metaphor. It exposes a general error: grammar offers crisp singular nouns where the world offers graded boundaries and scale-dependent partitions. A boundary-grade marker would not solve the metaphysics, but it could prevent downstream systems from silently converting operational boundaries into natural ones. This matters in ecology, medical diagnosis, social groups, datasets, and algorithmic categories.
List, Pettit, and Tuomela: groups as agents under explicit criteria
List and Pettit’s Group Agency (2011) develops conditions under which organised groups can satisfy standards of agency through decision procedures that generate coherent beliefs and preferences. Tuomela’s Social Ontology (2013) distinguishes acting in the I-mode from acting in the we-mode and examines collective commitment and group reasons. These works provide a vocabulary richer than either individualism or loose anthropomorphism. The central design lesson is architectural. A company does not become an agent merely because a sentence places it before a verb. Its agency depends on mechanisms for representation, decision, correction, memory, and authorised action. Conversely, reducing every organisational outcome to isolated individuals can miss structural commitments and emergent constraints. Collective markers should therefore encode the path from members to act, not merely label the size of the subject.
Infinity across mathematics, physics, and metaphysics
The 2025 revision of the Stanford Encyclopedia entry on infinity, by Easwaran, Hájek, Mancosu, and Oppy, treats infinity across mathematics, cosmology, physics, and theology. This breadth is essential because the word infinite conflates several questions: completed mathematical totalities, indefinitely extendable procedures, unbounded magnitudes, singularities in theories, spatial extent, divisibility, and divine attributes. The present book adds outfinit as a deliberately engineered concept: a process is outfinit when no final operational boundary is specified, although every realised stage remains finite and resource-bounded. The proposal does not compete with mathematical infinity. It isolates a practical form of open-endedness relevant to computation, scientific exploration, institutional evolution, and cultural production. Its value must be judged by whether it prevents invalid movement between “not yet bounded,” “too large to exhaust,” and “actually infinite.”
Cappelen, Chalmers, and the conceptual-engineering turn
Cappelen’s Fixing Language (2018), the volume Conceptual Engineering and Conceptual Ethics edited by Burgess, Cappelen, and Plunkett (2020), and Chalmers’s account of conceptual engineering shift philosophical attention from analysing inherited concepts to assessing, designing, and implementing better ones. They distinguish diagnosis from construction: showing that a concept is vague, harmful, or theoretically defective does not yet produce a replacement people can use. This literature gives the book its constructive method. A new distinction requires a target function, semantic specification, implementation path, adoption strategy, and evaluation. It also raises an ethical question often missing from engineered languages: who gains authority when a category becomes mandatory? A provenance system can protect inquiry or become surveillance. A responsibility vector can reveal institutional causation or provide bureaucratic theatre. Conceptual engineering must include governance and the right to contest the ontology.
Maat and the history of philosophical languages
Maat’s Philosophical Languages in the Seventeenth Century (2004) reconstructs the projects of Dalgarno, Wilkins, and Leibniz in their intellectual contexts. These schemes sought a systematic language whose symbols reflected an ordered catalogue of reality. Their ingenuity is inseparable from their fragility: once the taxonomy is disputed, the vocabulary becomes a monument to the designer’s assumptions. SLEnglish therefore rejects the dream of a complete language of things. It proposes small, revisable operators around recurrent reasoning failures. Its units need not encode the essence of a whale, state, or emotion. They can encode relations such as source, lineage, scale, and reversibility while leaving the underlying ontology open. The system should have versions, deprecations, competing profiles, and explicit unresolved categories.
Large language models as a new experimental site
Mahowald and colleagues (2024) distinguish formal linguistic competence from functional competence in large language models. A system can produce well-formed language while lacking reliable world models, grounded goals, causal understanding, or calibrated use of evidence. This distinction mirrors the central argument of the book: richer expression is not automatically better thought. At the same time, language models make conceptual engineering unusually testable. Researchers can insert operators into prompts, intermediate representations, or training data and measure whether models retain provenance, distinguish forks from copies, or assign responsibility more accurately. Models can also act as adaptive interfaces that request a marker only when ambiguity threatens a decision. The danger is institutional scale: an unexamined schema embedded in widely used AI can harden one contested ontology into infrastructure.
What this map leaves open
No existing work combines all four movements attempted here: global typological comparison, cautious cognitive interpretation, metaphysics of identity and collectivity, and experimentally governed conceptual design. That combination is the opportunity, but also the main source of risk. Each field has standards that the others can easily violate. Linguistic categories can be oversimplified, experiments can be overgeneralised, metaphysical distinctions can be presented as facts of nature, and designed concepts can become moralised without evidence. The proper form of synthesis is therefore not a grand unification. It is an interface among disciplines with explicit type checking. Every passage should permit the reader to ask: Is this a fact about a language, a replicated behavioural effect, a philosophical model, or an invention proposed for trial? The book’s originality depends less on having unprecedented intuitions than on keeping those modes distinct while making them cooperate.
Controlled Natural Languages and executable semantics
Tobias Kuhn’s survey provides the most useful map of the CNL design space and its trade-offs among precision, expressiveness, naturalness, and simplicity (2014). Attempto Controlled English demonstrates deterministic interpretation, discourse representation, querying, and theorem-proving integration. Common Logic Controlled English shows how English-like forms can express first-order semantics and role-based event structures. PENG demonstrates compilation into Answer Set Programming. SLEnglish should be evaluated as a deliberately less natural but more circuit-oriented member of this lineage, not as if the problem had no history.
Semantic Web standards as baselines and interoperability targets
RDF, OWL, SHACL, SPARQL, and PROV-O collectively cover graph representation, ontology semantics, validation, querying, and provenance. Their maturity makes them the strongest engineering baseline. The SLEnglish hypothesis concerns source uniformity and execution locality, not representational impossibility. A serious implementation must export and import useful subsets, document semantic loss, and avoid proprietary reinvention of standard concepts.
Datalog, ASP, constraints, and temporal reasoning
Datalog provides a clean basis for recursive rule evaluation; Answer Set Programming supports non-monotonic rules, defaults, and optimisation; SMT and CSP systems support arithmetic and finite constraints; event calculi and temporal logics support changing worlds. SLEnglish should not conceal these differences behind one vague “reasoning engine.” Its profiles are contracts to established semantic families.
Vector Symbolic Architectures and Hyperdimensional Computing
Plate’s Holographic Reduced Representations and Kanerva’s account of hyperdimensional computing established a family of distributed compositional representations. Later surveys by Kleyko and colleagues systematise architectures, operations, hardware, and applications. Their role here is deliberately bounded: they may provide approximate memory and retrieval over exact semantic graphs. Whether that hybrid yields a net advantage is an experimental question.
Reproducible workflows and research objects FAIR workflow research, computational notebooks, provenance standards, and RO-Crate show how data and computations can become portable research objects. They also reveal the remaining separation between executable computation and prose-level claims.
Executable science should be understood as an extension of this ecosystem, not a replacement for it.
